divested of their knighthood, but they included the Dukes of Ormonde, Monmouth and Buck- ingham. The Order of the Garter has played a very important part in history, and in the annals of the human race. It has often been called the Order of St. George, and was evi- dently founded on the famous, but perhaps legendary, Knighthood of "The Round Table."

The Response of Birmingham. Birmingham has responded nobly to the call for soldiers and sailors, and now that the turn of the women has come, they have proved as eager to help, and to be as self-sacrificing and patriotic, as the men. Women of all classes turned out to hear the Hon. Mrs. Haversfield explain her Volunteer Reserve Force, and no fewer than seven hundred women and girls en- rolled themselves within a few hours. This was in November of last year. The first women's battalion is now one thousand strong, and there are five hundred members in the second one now forming. Each volunteer undergoes a medical examination before joining the force. The daughters of the wealthy classes are greatly in- terested in the movement, and are enthusiastic- ally practising soldiers and fire drill, attending ambulance classes, learning camp cooking, and stick fencing, semaphore and Morse signalling, and preparing to act in any emergency that may arise. Branches, have been formed in all the neighbouring towns, and there can be no doubt that to some extent the standard of women's health will be improved—a matter of national importance—if nothing more be done.

The FavouritaSolours. , The blouse suit is shown in its mot charming guise, and is to be had in light fine serge, as well as in every description of cotton material. In zephyr, these ready-to-wear frocks are par- ticularly attractive and useful, as are also those in gingham, pique and cotton foulard. White dresses are to have a great vogue, and the white muslin and lawn blouse gait is a marvel of beauty and cheapness. It will prove a blessing to the busy girl who has but little time to devote to shopping and to the making of dresses. All ready-to-wear dresses, whether expensive or the most moderate in price, are now .Teell cut, well made, and finished. In these respects they improve every season. The most fashionable

colours for these frocks are navy blue, nigger brown, black and white, and various shades of grey, sand and beige. Grey will be very fashion- able in millinery, as well as in dresses, and in both it is most successfully contrasted with a lovely shade of rose pink. At the present moment beige may be said to eclipse all neutral shades in popularity. Washable Buttons.

A great many blouses, and the greater number of cotton dresses, are trimmed with white wash- ing buttons, made by hand, of crochet. The idea is quite a recent one, but it caught on so rapidly that the demand quickly exceeded the supply, consequently the manufacturer stepped in with a white washable guimp button, that closely resembles • the hand-made article in ap- pearance. It is rain and damp proof. A tiny, bell-shaped button in this make is used most lavishly on dresses and blouses of all kinds. Sometimes it is placed in vertical rows, at others in circles and' clumps. So popular have these washable buttons become that they are nowide- manded in colours, and will doubtless soon be shown in the shops. Buttons are a curious decoration for stockings, but American manu- facturers are showing hose that have simulated button holes, with buttons, to give the effect of high boots, fastening at the sides to a pair of shoes.

An Australian Lady's Feat. It is quite a relief 'to turn to exploits uncon- nected with war performed by women in these terrible times. The climbing feats of a young Australian lady, Miss Freda du Faur, take us back to the days that now seem so )ong ago, when the world was at peace. Miss du Faur enjoys the distinction of being the first lady to climb Mount Cook, the highest peak in New Zealand, which is considered by members of various Alpine Clubs more difficult thEtn any ridge in Switzerland. The Alpine Clubs of New Zealand and Canada are doing no work this year, for the greater number of members are in Europe or in Egypt, fighting the battles of the Empire. Miss du Faur has been prevented by the war from visiting Switzerland, but she hopes to do so later Off, and then to extend her travels to Asia, in order to reach the summits of some of the Himalaya ranges.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

Hianours for Women.

The King and Queen continue to devote their energy and time to good works of all kinds. The King has lately decorated many officers and men of the two Services, who have distinguished themselves on land or sea. Sister Mary B. Bennet, a member of Queen Alexandra's Royal Naval Nursing Service, has been decorated with the R.C.C. medal. The work done by women during the war has been of such exceptional value that the question of similar recognition to that awarded to men is in contemplation. The honour conferred on Lady Dorothy Fielding by the King of the Belgians, is regarded as a pre- cedent for similar honours by our own King.

The Belgian Royal Family. The Queen of the Belgians has been suffering from an affection of the throat, contracted whilst nursing the sick and wounded, but the heroic patient is now convalescent. Their Majesties quite recently had the pleasure of a visit from their two younger children, and de- spite the serious risks attending the journey, they were taken to Belgium, and have since re- turned to Lord Curzon's country seat. Their elder brother, the Duke of Brabant, who is only fifteen, has a commission i- 4;he Belgian Army, The Prince shares the der ';;Ts, and the fatigue of the work in the trenches, and desires that no concessions shall be made in his favour, on account of his rank.

HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes and Useful Suggestions.

Some Dishes Without Meat.

The number of people who have become non- meat eaters or who eat far less meat than formerly is large, and the number of the latter has been added to since in many homes the necessity for cutting down household expenses had to be faced.

The warning that flesh meat is to become still more scarce and consequently dearer be- cause of the demands of the armies in the field will add to the desire of housewives to know how they can 'manage with a minimum of meat. Below we give a few valuable recipes:

Poached Eggs and Macaroni (hot). Break some macaroni up into inch pieces, rinse it well; put 2 oz. of butter into a fire- proof dish, put the macaroni in, and pour enough milk over it to thoroughly cover it, stirring it now and again. When three-quar- ters cooked lift it out, and put it into another dish, seasoning it well with pepper and salt, and pour over it half a pint of good brown gravy; then put it back in the oven again and leave it till cooked. Wnen ready lay poached eggs on it, sprinkle liberally with Parmesan cheese, coralline pepper, and serve. Vegetable Pie.

Butter a pie-dish and put into it a layer of sliced potatoes, a layer of sliced tomato and of part-cooked Spanish onion. Season with salt and pepper, and moisten with vegetable stock, cream, or milk. Fill with more vege- table. Cover with crust, and bake in a well- heated oven for one hour. If it is inconvenient to make pastry, make a pie as follows: Grease the dish, and put in a layer of part-cooked onion, tomato, and mashed potato well flav- oured with salt and pepper, and moistened with stock and milk. Add hard-boiled egg and dicq of cooked carrot and turnip, and some cooked Brussels sprouts; finish with tomato, onion, and a crust of mashed potato. Score with a fork, and bake until the potato browns. If you have mushrooms add them to the pie.

How To Fry. It only means a very small outlay at the beginning; you probably will have a stewpan you can use for the fat, and the frying basket costs 104 d. Get 2 lb. of clarified dripping from the butcher, and this will last you for mouths, for the heating prevents it from becoming rancid, and you can add to it any odd dripping you may have left, over from time to time. Put it in a stewpan, and keep that for frying only; let the fat solidify in it when cool, and then it is always ready for. use. Strain it occasionally. Never fry anything in it until a faint blue smoke rises from the fat. If you follow that rule your fried fish will be crisp and free from grease, and you can then use the same fat for frying sweet fritters or any- thing you wish, for -no flavour can get into it when the fat is used at that heat. You will find this such a boon; well worth the small outlay, and always ready to your hand when required.—" Everywoman's Weekly."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
Tomato and Spanish Onion Au Gratin.

Slice about eight well-wiped tomatoes with a very sharp knife, and part boil four Spanish onions. Then slice the onion. Arrange on a greased dish alternately, moistening with some stock, milk, or white sauce, and season with salt and pepper. Strew thickly with bread- crumbs, add some pieces of butter, and bake until brown. When eggs are cheap prepare this dish in round white fireproof pans, and serve a poached egg on each.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

" Tfta Mentaliy Defective."

The item under this head in the report of the last meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians naturally turned one's thoughts once more to the urgent and difficult question of the control of the feeble-minded generally. It has been under consideration a long while, now separately and independently, and now in conjunction with the larger problem of which it forms an important part; and not only must the earliest favourable opportunity be taken to deal systematically and on a national basis with the mentally defective, but the future treatment of the criminally insane will also have to be decided ere very long. -.As to the feeble-minded, a restriction of the liberty of those who are a menace to

the common interests of the .public seems im- perative and inevitable. I have always taken the optimistic view that the supposed in- crease in lunacy recorded in the official re- turns was apparent rather than real, being, in fact, chiefly attributable to a more effective registration of the feeble-minded; still, it re- mains true that, while it is not long since almost every village Lad t least one idiot at large, and these unmistakable cases are mostly now accounted for and under control, too many of the unfit continue at liberty, a danger to themselves and a source of weakness to the

community. ' I know the principal objections to further legislative enactment along the lines now indicated, but I am strongly of opinion that the all-round benefits would far outweigh the undoubted risk. Absolutely un- restrained, the mentally deficient constitute a constant and serious peril to the common weal; and sure I am that if the precautions so long in contemplation had been adopted years ago, grave mischief would have been prevented in the districts in Lancashire and Yorkshire in which I have spent half a cen- tury or so. Adulteration of Fcodstuffs. .

Dr. Joseph, the deputy-mayor, recently c0n- ...-ratulated Warrington on the fact that this practice, concerning which I have repeatedly written in this column, has practically dis-

appeared so far as this borough is in ques- tion. The general body of tradesmen have all along been above suspicion, any calculated, fraudulent faking of foodstuffs for extra profit that has occurred in the past, apart from an occasional and unimportant technical offence into which a perfectly honest shopkeeper may have been unconsciously betrayed, being con- fined to an odd greedy and unscrupulous one here and there, whose mistaken methods led to their permanent undoing.

Coffee and Ch:cory. Dr. Joseph specifically deprecated the mix- ing of coffee with chicory in .undue propor- tion. In one town in which I lived some time was a very strict Quaker who had acquired a wide reputation and wealth by retailing tea and coffee; and I was much exercised in mind on finding—and I confirmed it by a succession of experiments—that whenever I went to that shop and asked for " best coffee, the pro- prietor or his assistant invariably inquired if I wanted " pure " coffee! To be sure, as has been observed elsewhere, the mixture of chicory with coffee is legal so long as it is declared, but the temptation to add chicory costing 4d. a pound and sell as coffee at 2s. is considerable. I recollect reading in some magazine article on the subject that a very useful preliminary test for ascertaining the presence of chicory in coffee, which may be easily applied in the house. is to strew some of the sample on the surface of cold water, contained in a glass jug. Owing to the pres- ence of oil in the coffee it will float on the sur- face, but the chicory, from which oil ig—absent, will rapidly sink to the bottom of the jug.

Cheap and Nasty. I have in remembrance some amazing in- stances of adulteration of cocoa, tea, milk, and even margarine, but leave these for a word or two regarding mysterious mixtures which one has known to be retailed as jam. I recall the case of the young man who described himself to the magistrate as a raspberry-seed manu- facturer; his work being to make out of wood the little seeds used in bogus raspberry jam. As was observed when that had been before the Court, the manufacturer of the fearful and wonderful concoction so labelled, who in- vests heavily in turnip and vegetable marrow for the purpose of his trade, must naturally make up the deficiency in seeds, and appar- ently this is how it is done! As I have seen

by keeping an eye on small firms in obscure parts of towns in which I have lived, in addi- tion to common root vegetables, the unscrupu- lous manufacturer finds uses for rotten fruit of all kinds, and in addition fruits are substi- tuted one for another to. a large extent as the crops are varyingly successful. It has been significantly remarked that no British food- faker finds it necessary to take much trouble over his frauds. The practice of this sort of dealer is to serve a purchaser with honest goods until confidence has been established and the purchaser has become a customer; and then systematically to cheat that customer. You will always get good butter at a fraudu- lent -shop where you are not known. You might be acting for an inspector. Finally, for the present, it is good to have authorita- tive assurance that so far as Warrington is affected there is nowadays very little if any adulteration of food, and that taking the coun- try as a whole the malpractices here indi- cated are steadily decreasing. A Fearless Critic.

I have been reading the Rev. A. M. Mitchell's characteristic criticisms. in the Bur- tonwood Parish Magazine, and I must say I admire his outspokpnness. I am somehow reminded of an incident in the life of John Smith, the Yorkshire revivalist. At the close of an exciting service in those old days, the chapel-keeper reported to the preacher: "There is a man in the gallery who is in great distress because of his sins." Mr. Smith went up and said in his blunt way, "Now, then, what's the matter? Why don't you be ?" "I can't believe," was the reply. "But you must believe. Look here," said the evangelist, "I'll give You. ten minutes, and if you don't believe in ten minutes I'll kick you down the stairs." Strange as ft may appear to the modern reader, the threat suc- ceeded. I cannot enlarge here (that is why I have given this lfttle illustration), but always think of the Vicar of Burtonwood as a man of that stamp. Nrhaps I may venture to return to the point one of these days in the near future.

HERE AND THERE.

Girls never could throw straight, so if a girl throws herself at a fellow and misses him she generally hits another.

One Good Result. Mrs. Johnston: "Do you think that our sending Edith to the cookery classes has done any good?' Mr. Johnston: "Certainly it has!" Mrs. Johnston: "But then the things she cooks are uneatable and we have to give them away to tramps at the door." Mr. Johnston: "Well, haven't you noticed that we have almost entirely got rid of the tramps?"

Clergyman's Startling Address. .21, clergyman once startled the young ladies of his flock with the following advice : • "The buxom, bright-eyed, rosy-cheeked bouncing lass who can darn a stocking, make her own frocks, command a regiment of pots and kettles, feed the pigs, chop wood, milk cows, wrestle with the boys, and be a lady withal in company, is just the sort of girl for me, and for any worthy man, to marry. "But you, ye pining, moping, lolling, screwed-up, wasp-waisted, mortgaged, music- murdering, novel-reading daughters of fashion, you are no more fit for matrimony than a pullet is to look after a family of fourteen chickens. The truth is, my dear girls, you want more liberty and less fashionable re- straint, more kitchen and less parlour, more exercise and less sofa, more pudding and less piano, more frankness and less mock modesty."

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Useful Information in Popular

Language.

Bad Teeth Dull The Mind

About half of the American school children have seriously defective teeth; and children with defective teeth take half a year longer to complete the eight grades of the elementary course thaii the children with good teeth. This is gleaned after experimenting with 40,000 school children in New York city during the last two years.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
Help The Eyes.

An oculist once gave this advice to a lady who consulted him about her eyes :—" Use much cold water in washing the eyes. One's sight begins to fail as the eyeball begins to flatten, 60 when you bathe the eyes gently squeeze them from side to side—the forefinger at the side of the nose, the thumb at the opposite side of the eye—and thus the con- vexity will be preserved. When the eyes are weary, give them rest." Don't read in bad lights or near a flaming fire, or much in trains.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
Drink Lemonade and Walk.

If you want to live long follow Edward Gold- smith's example. He lives in Lynn, Mass., and is past 98 years. He ascribes his longevity and activity to walking every day five miles before breakfast, drinking only lemonade and not using tobacco in any form. He feels young and strong and expects to live a dozen years yet. He drinks several glasses of lemon- ade daily. Rain or snow is no deterrent to his early morning walk. He goes over the ap- pointed distance, no matter what the elements are doing. Our Lungs As Garbage Furnaces.

Oxygen has a remarkable combining power. and will form compounds with almost every known element. It is the active agent in breathing, and bright red arterial blood, rich in ogygen, may readily be distinguished from the bluish venous blood laden with its burden of carbonaceous waste. The lungs are really garbage furnaces, and the carbon that is oxydised there and elsewhere in the body fur- nishes as true an example of burning, with its attendant production of heat energy, as does a locomotive fire-box glowing with its red- hot coals.

The only difference is one of quantity and of concentration. Oxygen is for ever burning up the waste matter, and in this manner every breath is a contribution to the fire, which, though flameless, is none the less a fire.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====

Make delicious and nutritious puddings with apples, figs, dates, golden syrup, jam, &c., and Shredded ATORA Beef Suet. Always ready for use and saves all the trouble of chopping. Ask your grocer for it; refuse substitutes.

A COMPETITION IN WHICH YOU PLEASE

YOURSELF.

MY DEAR CHILDREN,— You will, of course, be wondering who has carried off the prize for the essay on "My Favourite Book." Well, it is

GILBERT COOKSON, 29, Folly-lane,

I am very glad to see Gilbert again taking part in our competitions, and hope that his lead will be followed at once by many other old com- petitors. Just fancy; there are nearly a hun- dred of my nephews and nieces who have not entered for one of our competitions during the last twelve months We must change this with- out delay! • The other entries for the "favourite book" competition were very nice, but I was surprised to notice that the good old stories such as "Robinson Crusoe," "Swiss Family Robinson," "Arabian Nights," "Fifth Form .at St. Dominic's," "Little Meg's Children," "No Place Like Home," "Teddy's Button "—and so on and so on—were never even mentioned. All your favourites were quite recent school tales and adventure stories. Oi course, these are all capital, but we must got on any account forget the older and better books—which are, I think, much more interesting, as well as valuable. It was for this reason that I sent our last winner a very nice copy of "A Peep Behind the Scenes." The competition which closes on Monday is sure to be ,o prime favourite. As I said last week, it is one in which I am going to let you please yourself to a large extent. The prize will be given for the %best lines upon "Kitchener" or " Jellicoe " or "French." Only write about one, and send your letter—which should be quite short—along to Your loving

UNCLE NED.

THOUGHT FOR THE WEEK.

A good name is rather to be chosen than great riches.—PROVERBS XXII. 1.

"WASHED ASHORE."

"29, Folly-lane, Warrington, May 17, 1915

"DEAR UNCLE NED,—You ask me which is My Favourite Book.' Well, it is a book which I won in one of your competitions. It is entitled 'Washed Ashore.' I like the book very much because It is full of adventures both on land and on sea. On land it deals with smugglers and underground passlges, which are very inter- esting and very exciting to read. Then again on sea it is also interesting and exciting. When I read this book I kept thinking what was com- ing next, and how it was going to end. I hope to win more interesting books in your nice Qom- petitions.—l remain, your loving nephew, " GILBERT COOKSON:' A very good effort indeed!

THE STREAM AND THE OCEAN. (B Y JESSIE Srmm.]

Dancing glancing—o'er the pebbles, smoothly worn though great, though small— Tiny torrent, crafty cascade, weirdly-winding waterfall, Hesitating, scintillating in the sparkling summer sun, By my cot upon the hillside flows the brooklet on and on To the ocean lazy, hazy. Thus its bright career is done, And its day is gone. . . Lost in vaster, deeper Life anon. So I see the silent ocean

Which my eyes have ever seen Lying lulled in mystic motion, With a secret none may glean. So the daylight dimly closes O'er the waters dark and vast; And my brook secure reposes In their depths at last.

Sliding, gliding down the swiftly-sweeping stream of earthly bliss, Chaffing, laughing at the currents, free from care 'mid Nature's kiss, Staying rarely, singing fairly 'neath Life's golden-glittering sun, By my cot upon the hillside pass the people on and on To a dreamland dim and distant. Thus their bright career is (lone, And their day is gone. . . . Lost in vaster, deeper Life anon. So I see the arch of Heaven Which my eyes have ever seen As a symbol and a haven, • • With a secret none may glean. So at length the shadows gather And the world is lost to sight ' As they meet the Great All-Father In that land of light.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
ARTIER OF PRINCIPLE.

The problem as to whether or not it is advisable that Boards of Guardians should send girls under their care into domestic service on licensed premises, came up again for discussion at last week's meeting of the Warrington Guardians, and, on the initiative of Mr. T. Dale, supported by the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, the Board decided, by seven votes to six, not to approve the minute of the Cottage Homes Committee recommending that a certain girl be sent into the service of a person named. It was urged that the question was one of principle and not of whether the individual would-be employer was an estimable person or otherwise. No one suggested there was any doubt on that point. Mr. Dale gave notice of his intention to move a definitely general resolution on the subject. The minute referred to read as follows: "The Clerk informed the committee that .he had searched the 'committee reports covering a number of years, but was unable to find a resolution prohibiting the sending of girls from the Cottage Homes into service with persons re§ident in houses licensed for the sale of in- toxicating liquors. It was Proposed- by Mr. McKinley, and seconded by Mr. Brooks, that in the ease under consideration the girl be sent into the service of the lady, named, as pre- viously recommended. The resolution /was carried by five votes to two."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
A DANGEROUS PRECEDENT.

Mr. bale, in moving that the minute be not approved, said he was sorry the question had to be discussed further. At the first meeting at which .the Board discussed the matter, the Clerk stated that he was under the impression that there was a minute to the effect that no girls should be tient from the Guardians' homes to licensed premises, or that if there was no minute there was at least an implicit under- standing amongst the members to that effect. In -consequence of such an understanding, the Board had adhered to the embodied principle. Now there came another application, and the committee saw fit to deal with it in the way they had. Of course, arguments had been used as to the person concerned being a very respect- able individual. That was not qtwstioned, but it was felt that the breaking of the principle was a dangerous precedent to set. Other people might make similar applications and think they had every reason to be received favourably, and the committee might not feel disposed to grant their application. He thdught that a general principle laid down in that respect was the best thing, and by adhering to it they did not offend anyone outside. The Guardians sought to clothe the children in their care, and to look after their" physical con- ditions and social surroundings, and he thought it was, at least, as important that they should consider their moral well-being even after they had left their immediate control. The Guard- ians took upon themselves the responsibility of parental control when they admitted chil- dren intd their keeping, and they sought their highest good. It had been mentioned that the girl in-que•stion was not very sharp. That, he contended, constituted a reason for special pre- cautions being taken in her case. It was said that she might be sent back to the Guardians in a month's time, as she was not likely to nrove sham enough for a domestic servant. He did not think that it was at all desirable for a girl to he sent out like that when a prdwect of her being sent back as unfit within a month was entertained. NO FADDISM.

The Rev t AI Mitchell seconded. lie was, he said, heartily in agreement with all that Mr. Dale had said. Of course, there was a lot to he said on the other side of the question. As far as lie, personally, was concerned, it was not a matter of mere opposition, nor was it a

matter of what one might call faddism. AS illustration of the fact that he did not ali°k: his own particular convictions to lead hisla.ll:3/ faddism he might say that although he strong 'a disagreed with smoking, yet when he PA cigarettes offered to him he accepted them gave them to " Tommies." Ile did not gether like being opposed, in that matter a tn; sending of girls to licensed premises, to snli3je of the best members of the coramittee, but. ii felt it was unwise to go against any tradltiots which was calculated to safeguard the interlie and welfare of the young people in 'iv Guardians' charge. He hoped those agreed with the minute would not press tt'e matter. As guardians of the welfare of tmt children under their control, they must uila place them in such positions that they be subject to the influences to which they kt`•:r l Guardians) would not think of subjecting theo own children. Personalli, he would not dreg!, of allowing a child of his to go into service ° licensed premises. No matter what precautil might be taken in the matter of licens premises, there was always a certain atra°s(l phere—it was inevitable—which was calculatere to create in the minds of the girls who 'OA placed there a certain attitude towards r g. outlook upon life that, to hi ff way of thinkinrn was most undesirable. Those who v.-ere to favour of the minute argued that the pen"Uloi whom it was proposed to send the girl w*.s absolutely suitable person. They all " Amen!" to that. But it wasn't the point.v they granted one application they would h` other requests of a similar character very so„-a and those concerned might reasonably contelf, that unfair treatment had beenemeted nut their annlications were unfavourably receicee' It would %e opening the door—creating a i cedent. If they agreed with the miaute the girl was sent, the matter would not st°ll there; it might mean something very seirl°v in the time to come. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 292 1915.

LIYMM

Scholars' Holiday.—The Lymm and district school children have been holidaying this week, and have found much pleasure in the fairs which have visited the township. " Terrier's " Grumble.—"lt's all right"— declares Private Joseph, Clare—" the people of England saying: 'Why don't they advance here, and advance there?' It's just those that ought to come and 'have a packet at it.' We do a bit of grumbling sometimes, but it is no use. For it has got to be done."

Bowdon Officer K I led.—Second-Lieut Richard Killick, who has been killed in the Dardan- elles operations, was a son of Mr. T. W. Killick, of Bowdon. For some years he lived in Madras, and was an officer in the Madras Volunteer Guards. At the beginning 'of the war he was transferred to the 6th Manchester Regiment. For many years the second- lieutenant's father acted as chairman of the Altrincham District Liberal Association, and is well-known in Lyrnm. Baptist Young Worshippers.—The Rev. E. E. Lovell, pastor of the lligher-lane Baptist Church, instituted among the scholars of his Sunday School some little time ago a Young Worshippers' League. This League has been formed with the object of encouraging the scholars to attend divine worship, and at the close of Sunday evening's service badges were presented and "pinned "—by Miss Daintree— to twenty of the League's first members who have completed the necessary six weeks' un- broken attendance. In suitable terms Mr. Lovell explained the object of the League, and expressed his hope that parents would encour- age their children to attend the church ser- vices and thus augment its numbers.

Sons and Nephews in Army.—Mrs. Hacklen, of Pepper-street, has now three sons and two nephews in His Majesty's Forces. Her son Ernest, of Lymm, has for many years belonged to the Territorials, and now serves at the Frost with the Lymm lads. Another of her sons— Arthur, who lives at Workington—joined the 3rd Border Regiment a few weeks ago. ' Previ- ously he had served eight years in the Army, with experience in India, and also had been in the reserve for five years. Still another og her sons—Walter, of Statham—belongs to the Ter- ritorials, with whom he is now training in the South, after five years' membership. Mrs. Had- den's nephew, Pte. Arthur Hadden, Lymm, is now with a section of the Cheshire Territorials a short distance away, whilst ner nephew, Ser- geant Herbert Dendy—of Manchester, who served through the Boer War—is now in Egypt with another Cheshire Territorial Regiment.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
MINISTER DEPARTS.

Valuable. Presentations And Expres-

sions Of Regret.

The Rev. J. A. Penaliggon has now bidden farewell to the Lymm and Bucklow Hill Con- gregational Churches.

At the latter church on Sunday evening he preached his farewell sermon—which pro- foundly touched the large number of Bucklow Hill and Lyram worshippers who gathered there.

After the service a handsome presentation was made. This took the form of a silver howl and set of books—which had been subscribed for by the congregations of both his churches. Mr. Penaliggon was also handed a list of the con- tributors.

Mrs. 011erenshaw made the presentation, and voiced the general and sincere regret at Mr. Penaliggon's departure. With deep feeling the reverend gentleman returned thanks, and said that whilst in the district he had, of course, had his good times and his bad times; hut they had been mostly good ones, and he would always cherish the recollection of the days he spent at Lymni and Bucklow -Hill. Penaliggon commences his ministry' at Littlemoor, Glossop, on the second Sunday in June.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
GR.ITEFUL REFUGEE

Pays Fine Tribute To kindness And

Comfort At "Beeckwood."

Mr. Alfred Watkin, chairman of the com- mittee or " Beechwood "—the Lymm home of the Belgian refugees—ha,s received the follow- ing pleasing and gratifying letter : " London, the 13th May, 19M. Holloway. "I can not leave written expressing to you my deep gratitude for all the kindness you have shown us.

"We shall- never forget the devotion, the care we have been handled, to forget and to appease our pitiful fate. We shall conserve for over a memory of the gratitude for your goodness, generosity you and the population shown us

"I will finished my letter with deep ,respect, thanks and greetings of the "FAMILY PONJAERTS (ITAGERS)." M. Ponjaerts and family—as announced in our columns at the time—left Lymm a week or two ago to take up his new position at Holloway. By occupation he is a pilot.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
INEBRIATED TRIPPERS.

Glorious But Imaginary Careers In Army and Navy.

On Whit Monday night there were among the trippers to Lymm Thomas Bostock, labourer, and John Brookes, spade-maker, both of Stock- ton Heath. About ten o'clock they became—it is alleged—drunk and disorderly in the village, and were arrested.

The following morning they were brought before Vice-Chancellor Dudley Stewart-Smith and Mr. S. Davies.

Serit. Davies stated that their behaviour had been very bad along the road from the Post Office to the Council's Offices, and when stopped one of them said he had been fighting in the trenches, whilsf the other declared he had been serving in the Dardanelles. They "didn't see why they should be interfered with by the police !" It appeared that the two had been boasting about their patriotic services up and down the village. Their story was quite untrue —although one of them did serve a few weeks in the Army, before being ,discharged. Each of the men was fined five shillings.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
"A DANGEROUS GAME."

Traversing Muddy And Cut-Up Roads

Under Shell-Fire.

Private Ernest Hadden, of Pepper-street— who, it will be remembered, accompanied along the line his sick major—now writes: "I am back among the Lymin boys now, and was quite expecting to be in the trenches by now; but a deal of sickness in the battalion has held us back. ,

"But we have had plenty of fatigue work this week. It is a dangerous game at times— working mostly behind the firing trench; and the nights have been very wet, miserable and cold. We have got to walk several miles be- fore we get to the place, and not on roads like they are at home. Sometimes they are nearly knee-deep in mud, and are cut up with shell- holes. One has got to be careful not to fall in.

"Last night we were working only a few yards from the first British lines, and I took a walk up. It is the first time I have ever been in the firing-line, and I was surprised to find how cosy they looked; and the men in them seemed quite happy. "We are mostly under shell and rifle fire while going and coming from these jobs. We had one lad shot through the shoulder last night while returning—but no one that you know. The Lymm lads are quite safe so fair.. • • :1

===== 0002601_19150529_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
STAIIIAM FESTIVAL.

Large Crowds Enjoy Favoured A\ hit- Monday.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
UNPRECEDENTED SUCCESS:

Statham was en fete on Monday. The occa- sion was its fourth annual festival; which— despite the war—proved a greater success than ever, being favoured with generous support and glorious weather. The festivities commenced with a procession, beaded by the Lymm Subscription Band. Leav- ing Cross Brow early in the afternoon, it took the direction of Statham-lane to Lymm Cross, via Booth's Hill—returning by the station to the large field by Statham. Crossing. The band was followed by Councillor F. Lockyer—prime supporter of the festival—in an open carriage. He was uhable to walk, owing to his recent illness and operation. Miss Mary Fowler fol- lowed in a gay, fancy costume. Then came the Maypole dancers, garland dancers, other children, and the infants brought up the rear in gaily-decorated lurries and floats, kindly lent by Messrs. Norcott, Lloyd and Longshaw (all of Statham). All the children who took part belonged to Statham, although a few of them attend the Lyinm Schools. The route was thronged with spectators, especially in Lymm—where crowds of visitors congregated.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
DAINTY DANCERS.

Upon arrival at the field the Maypole and garland dances, and double-plaiting of the .Maypole, were gone through in first-rate style by the daintily-dressed little damsels from the school. They had been splendidly trained by Miss Appleton, one of the.teachers; and to her' great -credit is due. The dancers were:— Maypole Dancers.—Edith Wibberley, Nellie R,usliforth, Edith Sharples, Annie Struthers, Doris Clarke, May Norcott, Cissie Riley, Mabel Bell, Maggie Bell, Annie Bate, Eva Struthers, Florrie Hill, Winnie Hill. Nancy Ditchfield, Muriel Ward, Linda Hill, Doris Hewitt, and Olive Taylor (18). Garland Dancers.—Emma Rushforth, Eva Clarke, Elsie Wibberley, Connie Walton, Edith Antrobus, Mary Whitlow, Winnie Hill, Francis Ardern, Doris Clarke, Annie Joynson (10). The same girls took part in the double May- pole-plaiting. So the afternoon was pleasurably beguiled, and presently the children adjourned in pro- cession to the schoolroom—where a capital tea was partaken. The catering was by Mrs. Appleton, who gave her services in the prepar- ation of the tea. Afterwards photographs were taken, and each child was presented with an orange before going back to the field.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIZES AND SPEECHES.

There, upon return, the happy youngsters received from the hands of Mr. Lockyer and Mrs. W. /'lynch handsome prizes. Each lady present over sixty years, and every widow, re- ceived a pound of tea; whilst every equally old man was given a quarter of a pound of tobacco. Mrs. "lynch took a prominent part in the festival—for which she worked very hard. Miss Appleton was presented by Mr. Lockyer with a set of carvers in case, in recce- nition of her services as dancing mistress; whilst to Miss Fowler Mrs. Hynch handed a pair of photo frame's. The children then again went through their dances.

In a brief speech Mr. Lockyer expressed his pleasure at the support which the festival had received, and his gratification at the large number of visitors.. The working-class, he added, had this year broken all records in their support—subscriptions from them rang- ing from threepence to five shillings. He heartily thanked the working men and all others concerned. (Applause.) Three cheers were heartily given for Mr. Lockyer, who briefly acknowledged the com- pliment to his organisation.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
BAND AND DANCING.

The Lymm Subscription Band then played in capital style for public dancing, which con- tinued for two hours and was joined in by large numbers.

Liuring the day over six hundred people visited the field. The festival dosed with the National Anthem.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
MAY QUEENS AID RED CROSS.

Children Enjoy Gay Whitsuntide.

Although in the Statham Festival has centred the, chief festal interest this Whitsuntide, Lymm has not forgotten altogether its usual celebrations, for two small. "May Queen" tivals were held on Tuesday and Saturday re- spectively. The localities responsible were Rush Green and Newfield, and each festival had in view a contribution to the funds of the local Red Cross Hosiptal.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
RUSH GREEN.

The Rush Green May Queen Festival, held on Tuesday afternoon, was organised by Mesdames Loundes, Armstrong and Ogden. In very fine weather the procession paced through the vil- lage and district, collecting from the many spec- tators along the route. The sum raised was .g 1 Bs., and it was handed to the well-pleased Matron of the hospital by Lena Bay (the Queen), who has three brothers fighting in the trenches. She is the daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Fred Bay, of Rush Green, and was crowned by Frank Booth.

Other characters were :—Maids ot Honour: Sally Ogden and Ina. Loundes. Rose Queen: Margaret Forest. Maids of Honour: Nora Loundes and Roland Booth. Flower Girls: Ena Armstrong and Lizzie Dilton. Fairy Queen: Fanny Kay. Fairies: Elsie Booth and Agnes Wilson. Gipsy Queen: Edith Leather. Char- acters: Annie Jones, Lettie and Girlie Ogden, Maggie Armstrong and Bessie Lord (Red Cross nurses). Niggers: John Kellsall and Fred Lord. Dancers: Winnie Ogden, May Seymour, Alice Wilson and Gladys Dodson. Scouts: John Gleave and Williarm Armstrong.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
NEWFIELD.

The Newfield May Queen procession took place on Saturday afternoon, and was arranged by Mrs. and Miss Alice Jennings. The latter played the melodeon. The procession marched from Newfield to the Cross—where the "queen" (Louie Jennings) was crowned. Then followed a May- pole dance, and the garland-girls also enter- tained.

From the collections .21 was handed to the Red Cross, and the remainder divided amongst the children—of whom there were about forty. The following were other characters:— Crown and Sceptre Bearers: Eddie Edwards and David Partridge. Train-bearers: Edith Brook.; and Etty Atherton. Garland dancers: Polly Taylor, May Leigh, Hilda' Brookes and Gladys Leigh. Flower girls: Sarah Millington, Louisa Daniel and Bertha Leigh. Fairies: Elsie Leigh (queen), Lily Leigh, Doris Booth and Annie Davies. Niggers: Thomas Brookes, Peter Davies, Harry Davies, Sydney Reed, Willie Hampson, Harry Davies and George Leigh. Characters: Stan- ley Daniels (Robin Hood), Jack Smith (milk- maid), Arthur Taylor (cow-boy), Lilian Ward (Red Cross nurse), and Fred Sutton (stool- carrier). LYMM TERRITORIALS.

ENJOY A WELL-EARNED REST.

Last week we published particulars of how the Lymm Territorials had been serving in the firing-line for periods of twelve days and so on, and how in consequence they were feeling "a bit worn out."

This week the new has reached the township —per Company-Sergeant-Major H. Clare, who was in hospital at the time of writing—that they have retired for a rest from the firing- line. "So you see they are out of danger for a while," he adds; but does not say how far they have retired, or for how long. There is the good news that .no more casual- ties have taken place amongst_ the Lymm lads.

Private Ernest Redden declares: "We are all in the very best of health and spirits here. . . . They are at present all sat round me, and it is surprising to see how happy they look—all laughing and joking, all danger for- gotfkn for a time. But I know we shall all be thankful for the time when we shall re- turn. I see you have several of our wounded at home. . . . I ,can imagine their joy at being in Lymm once again; I know how I should feel. . . ."

A Lymm "Terrier" writes: "I don't think it can possibly last much longer, and I still think I shall be with you in August."

Mystery of the Water.

Writing, on the 20th Private Percy Clare said: "We are not in the trenches yet, but are expecting to go in to-night. So by the time you get this I think we shall be in again.

-On the next day his brother Joseph *rote: "We have been out of the trenches twelve days now; so it has been a change from the trenches—although we have had to go out dig- ging at night. "I notice that someone has given a hint of where we are—when he mentioned Hill 60. But we have never been in any of the battles there, and none we want—for it's nothing but xnur- der. It has quietened down a bit this last week, but they will be having another ' do ' at each other before long. "The Germans have [censored] that runs through our line, so we have got to be very careful of the water we drink. What next they will do I don't know, for they have tried about everything now !" The good weather has had a break in favour of nights "very wet, miserable and cold." In a letter received this week, Private P. Clare says: "We have had( it very wet this week up to this morning [the 20th], and it is now grand again." Yesterday morning a letter—the latest news received—came from Private P. Clare: "We are not in the trenches yet. We are a long way off, • down• the line—for a rest. We left the place where we had been in the trenches last night {the 24th].

===== 0002601_19150529_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
The Sick and Wounded.

Private Joseph Clare writes: " am pleased to say ,I am all right—just getting over being inoculated."

Company-Sergeant-Major Clare, recovered from his neuralgia, left the base hospital to rejoin the sth Cheshires on Wednesday. He is now with them again. Private Stanley Gibbs, wounded and in hos- pital. is stated to be rather worse this week. Private Richard Shaw has this week arrived home on furlough. The condition of his wounded foot leaves little to be desired.

Private Shaw arrived at noon on Wednesday from a convalescent home at Bradford, where he had been a fortnight. His leave will extend for eight days; so he will leave on Thursday. At the Bradford Hospital he was with Rifleman Tom Sudell, of Orford-lane, Warrington, who was wounded in a bayonet charge at St.-Eloi. Private James Taylor is to undergo an operation. He has not yet recovered the use of his wounded left arm, and remains at the Grantham Red Cross Hospital. A " Terrier " writes home: "I believe they have got it in Lymm that H. Brookes is wounded. Well, there is no truth in it. I think it a .mistake because there ie J),nother Brookes in the battalion. But it *does really upset one when h.e....hears from his parents asking him if it's true he is 'wounded I"

===== 0002601_19150529_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
ALIVE AND WELL.

It would appear that neither Private Henry Brookes nor Private Henry Cadman has just been wounded at the Front—as was last week reported by the War Office. Both write under recent dates to say they are quite well; and the wife of the former—in reply to inquiries— has received from Captain T. C. Hunt, Divi- sion Record Office, Shrewsbury, the following: "I . . am pleased to say that, as far as I am aware, your husband is alive and well and serving with his unit overseas. . ."

PRIVATE STANLEY GIBBS.

No Further Advance To Recovery.

Mrs. Gibbs, of ilenry-street, has this week again paid a visit to her wounded husband at Tosteth Park Military Hospital, Liverpool. Unfortunately he has not been so well for the last day or two, and has developed a very high temperature.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
"INTO THE TOWN AND 111)1 ND ABOUE"

But Shells Did Not Reach Lymm Men

—ln Fields.

To his sister, Mrs. S. Owen, of Brook-field Cottages, Private Henry Cadman writes: "1 now have got over the accident which I met with a week or t\vo back, and with the merry lads from Lymm am having a rest out of the trenches.

".Things over here are about the same. Shells keep on coming intp the town and round about; in fact, they are dropping within a stone's. throw of where we are now. We are living in the fields; it is just like being in camp again. "I lost all my letters and parcels while I was in hospital, but they might come back to Eng- land again, as they could not find me. . . ."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
Wet to the Skin.

To Mrs. G. Rixon, of Pepper-street—another sister—Private Cadman says: "We have been having some very bad weather, and it gives us no heart to do anything. We have to go out every night digging trenches, and we have been wet to the skin two nights running. And then we go "home" I The "home" is a cart sheet in a field, but still it is very welcome when we get back from our work.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
"Do With a Rest."

" We are still where the fighting is coming off—just on the right of it; I expect you have seen it in the papers. A good many have gone down the line for a rest; the doctor said that the battalion could do with a resit. Well, I should think so; for w 6 have done plenty of work since we came out."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
"TURN ROUND CORNERS."

Remarkable Way Bullets Appear

To Go.

Mr. David Daniels, Booth's Hill, Lymm, has received from Private Percy Clare word which runs: "While we have been out of the trenches we have had to go working just behind them at night, and we feel more done up with that work—because it is such a long way to the trenches, and we have to carry a pick or a spade; do three hours' work; and then walk back.

"But it is all for a good cause, so we mist not grumble when we are coming back from the trenches. . .

Bullet Right Through Body.

"Early this morning one of our lads got shot right through the hack, and it came out of the chest. He is not a -Lymm lad. We think he will get over it all right. "It is very funny how those bullets come. They seem to turn round corners—as this one missed about half-a-dozen of us and caught the poor chap in the back. . . ."

WOUNDED IN DARDANELLES.

Lance-Corporal W. R. Hillier's Experiences.

Mr. W. E. Hillier, outfitter, of the Village, Lymm, received on Tuesday the news that his son—Lance-Corporal W. Reginald Hillier, of the ,6th Manchester Territorials—had been wounded. The Lance-Corporal was serving in the Dar- danelles with, the East Lancashire Division of the Mediterranean Expeditionary Force. The news of his wounding came in a letter which he sent to his father.

This letter stated that he was on board the hospital-ship, returning to Alexandria— slightly wounded in the left thigh. He was going along very well, and was in excellent health; he would write partioulars as soon as possible, and in the meantime they at home were not to worry.

By the same post arrived a field card, dated May 7th, which stated that he was then quite well. The exact date when he was wounded is not yet known.

Attended People's College.

Born in Lymm twenty-three years ago, the Lance-Corporal is the eldest of .Mr. and Mrs. W. E.lier's four sons. He was educated at the People's College, Warrington, and later at the Lymm Grammar School, under Mr. Cecil Willson's headmastership. Leaving there at the age of fifteen, he entered the service of Illtessrs. H. Williamson, Ltd., wholesale silversmiths, at their Manchester branch. After two or three years he was trans- ferred to their head office at London; and just before the outbreak of hostilities had been ap-

===== 0002601_19150529_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
LANCE-CORPORAL HILLIER.

Wounded.

pointed the firm's representative in another part of the British Isles.

Four years previously, however—whilst in business at Manchester—he had joined the Ter- ritorial movement, and was called up at the

London mobilisation in August last.

Since that

time he had been in training, and journeyed out to that very " hot " region, the Dardanelles, some time ago. Sportsman and Singer.

The Lance-Corporal has always devoted much time to sport, and is a footballer, cricketer, swimmer and runner. His first distinction was a medal in People's College swimming competi- tions. Also he gained several prizes for run- ning at Frodsham, Oughtrington and other places in the district. He had belonged to the Lymm amateur football and cricket clubs, the Sydenham cricket club, the Astral football club, and other sports institutions. In religious convidtion a Churchman, he be- longed as a boy to the Lymm Parish Church choir, and also to the choir of St. Bartholo- mew's, Sydenham. Ofter he has sung at local parties and concerts.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
WITH TIIE

Experiences And Revelations From

The Front.

Mr. Reginald Barker, of Oak Lea, Statham— secretary of the Lymm Liberal Association— has received a highly-interesting letter from a friend, Private F. R. S. Harrisson, on active service with the R.A.M.C.

"Thank you ever so much," Private Harris- son writes, "for your three nice letters—also for the cake, which I distributed around as far as possible, and which everybody particularly enjoyed, as it is 'once in a blue moon that any ginger cakes come this way. . . . I wonder it stood the distance so well. I am enclosing a P.P.C. that may interest you. . (The card shows the 'ruines' of the beautiful cathedral of Saint Martin—which suffered at the hands of the Huns in the ' cam- pagne de 1914. Name of place censored.] I stayed at (censored); luckily we left the next morning. . . . Your letters bring very pleas- ant memories back—it seems all a lovely dream, never to happen again. I did enjoy myself at aear old Lymm. Long and Arduous Days.

"Our hours now are: Rise at 6 a.m. ; break- fast, 7; 8.15 parade, and work till 12.45 p.m.; then dinner till 2; work till 4.45; then tea, and work again from 6 to 8; and lights out at-8.30. "Our work is of a very varied nature. In the first place there are a number of fellows who have been discharged from hospital, but who are unfit for the trenches—so we have them.

They number up to a hundred, and are detailed off on various sanitary jobs each day, with either one or two of us in charge. Of course, we have to be able to show them what to do, and also we help them if we care to. I always do, as I find I can get more out of them without losing my position; I have always found that so.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
"Our Chief Work."

"Much to the surprise of some people, our chid work is digging earth latrines, and pits, rubbish pits, incinerators for the troops of our division; also disinfecting their billets, and just lately—since this battle has been in progress— we have had to dig graves and bury some poor fellows. We have also had to bury cattle and horses that have been killed; clear away rub- bish heaps behind the back doors of cottages in villages, and empty ditches. The French troops have a great delight, when leaving a village or a house, also to leave raw meat, and cheese, &c., lying about in the rooms, which, after a few days, 'welcome' you as you inspect that same house. _ _

"We also provide baths and a change of cloth- ing for troops, which, of course, have to be arranged for before hand by their officers. That is how I managed a month or so ago to get my first hot bath since I left England—now just on three months ago. This is one out of three I have actually -had since I left—three, that's all! It's horrible, for we suffer just like the other fellows do. Awful Revelations.

"I 'went stretcher-bearing the other night. It was terrible. Some of the sights were awful; I had no idea how mutilated our poor fellows get. We worked from 9 p.m. to 8.30 a.m., with one and a half hours' interval for, sleep, but the groans and cries kept you awake. We also gave a general hand' where needed at the dressing station. There were three deaths while we were there. . . . Cheer up The war can- not go on for ever. . . ."

Because life hands you a lemon, don't try to corner the Vinegar Trust.

When extremes meet they don't always recognise each other,.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE COURTS.

WANTED A WATCH!

A-011111j NVane-Earner's Lapse From

The Straight Path.

A well-dressed boy of fourteen, accompanied into Court by his mother, a neat little woman in black, was charged before the Warrington Bench on Tuesday morning with stealing a watch. _ _

From the statement of the Chief Constable, it appeared that' the boy, Nvho left school at the age of thirteen in order to help his mother to make ends meet, took the watch from the pocket of a workman's waistcoat he found hang- ing up in the place where he was employed. The watch was missed by the owner, and in- quiries were set on foot, with the result that the theft was traced to the lad, who made no denial of the charge. The Chief Constable went on to say that be didn't think the boy took the watch with felonious intent. It was not as if he had tried to sell it or had denied the theft when taxed with it. He was the eldest of a rather large family, the father of whom enlisted shortly after the outbreak of war. The money he had been earning had been a considerable help in keeping the house going. He had been wanting his mother to buy him a watch, and she was intending to do so.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
Why He Took It.

Asked by the Chairman (Mr. J. Rymer Young) why he took the watch, the boy replied, after some hesitation, that he wanted one. In reply to the Chairman, th 6 lad's mother said he had always been a good boy before, and she had never had any trouble with him. The Chairman, having conferred at Some length with his colleagues, addressed the youth- ful prisoner sternly. The Bench felt that he was old enough to understand that he had done a very serious thing. To take a watch just because he wanted one was' as bad- as taking a shilling that belonged to somebody else. They had seriously considered the advisability of ordering the birch, but had decided to take a very merciful course and let him off. In doing so they felt that they were being too kind. He must understand that if ever he came before them again on a charge of that kind he would be very severely dealt with. It was largely due to the Chief Constable that the Bench, had adopted the course they had. • Before the boy, who was in tears, left the Court, the Chairman extracted a promise from him that he would never do anything of the kind again.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
MIGHT HAVE CAUSED A RIOT.

Six Boys' Stupid Attack On A

Naturalised Subject's Shop.

At the Borough Felice Court on Saturday, six youths were each fined ss. and ordered to pay 4s. damages for being concerned together in an attack on the shop kept by Mr. Christo- pher Hornung, a naturalised British subject, at 2, Gerald-street, on May 18th. The defendants were: Harry Melbourne (17), 86, Ellesmere- street; John Wright (18), 9, Arpley-walk ; Harry Tork (17), 46, Tilley-street; Stanley Harris (18), 116, Algernon-street; William Egerton (17), and Christopher Fitzgerald (18), of 37, Victoria- street. They were charged with wilfully break- ing a plate-glass window and a dish at the shop, thereby doing damage to the amount of £l. They all pleaded guilty.—Defendants were fur- ther charged with committing a breach of the peace by being disarderly. "Matte It Up to Throw at the Door."

The Chief Constable told the Court that Detective-Sergeant Summers ascertained that defendants—who worked in the "wet shop" at the works of Messrs. R -lands Bros.—made it up to go to the shop a o some damage. At ten minutes past six in t e evening they went to the shop and kicked at the door, and Melbourne threw a large stone, which broke the plate- glass window and a dish inside. When inter- viewed, Melbourne said, "We made it up at the works to go there and throw at the door. I threw a stone and it went through the win- dow accidentally." Wright said, "We all made it up"; Pork replied, "I was there when it was done"; Harris, "I was there; r have nothing to say"; Egerton, "I thought we would make a .start first as last"; Fitzgerald, "We all made it up to go and bang the door." Mr. Wads- wbrth, the works manager for Messrs. Rylands Bros., gav'e considerable assistance to the police in the course of their inquiries, and the defend- ants were present at the court to answer to the charges through the courtesy of the firm. He wished to emphasise that the defendants' be- haviour might have led to a serious riot. The shop was occupied not by an alien subject, but by a man who had been naturalised 30 years. Defendants, made a start on this shop, and also planned among themselves, and with others, to go to a shop in the centre of the town, and do the same sort of thing. It was a serious matter for the police and for the Corporation, who would have had to pay for any damage in- curred.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
Lived in Fear and Trembling.

Mr. Hornung told the Bench that be had lived in the shop ten years, had bee ia in this country 53 years, and had been naturalised 30 years, and added that he had lived in fear and trembling the past ten day 4 Alderman Tinnion, one of the magistrates, told the boys that they had behaved stupidly.

OVER _A HUNDRED CONVICTIONS. Man Who kicked Detective And Bit Schoolmaster.

A man with 105 previous convictions against him stood in the dock at the Borough Police Court on Monday morning to answer charges of having been drunk and disorderly in Cheapside on Saturday afternoon, and of assaulting Detec- tive-Sergeant Summers and Mr. Wm. Mason, the headmaster of the Beamont Council School. He admitted the drunkenness and assaulting the constable, but denied that he assaulted Mr. Mason.

Defied Any Warringtonian To Lock Him Up.

The evidence was to the effect that the police officer had occasion to advise the man, who was in an obviously drunken condition, to go home. The man started to do so, but after going about six yards down Corporation-street he turned round and commenced laughing and jeering. A bystander said that he ought to be locked up, and the prisoner turned to the officer and observed "You lock me up! Why, there is not a fnan in Warrington who could do that." The officer, thereupon got hold of the prisoner and started to take him to the Police Station.

COST OF THE BRIDGE.

Sir,—ln answer to "A Ratepayer," who wants to know the total cost of Warrington Bridge: The amount was correctly stated in "The Ex- aminer" as .219,9e2 19s. 10d., figures got out by the Engineers, Messrs. Webster and Co. I cannot give information on the other points raised because I am not the chairman of any of the other committees who have carried out improvements on and in the neighbourhOod of Warrington Bridge; but I repeat the total cost of the erection of the Bridge itself was .219,962 19s. 10d. JAMES SMETHURST, Chairman of the Bridges Committee.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
WAR'S AFTERMATH.

Salvation Army and Employment for

Ex-Soldiers.

From various sources public attention is being directed to the necessity of ensuring our soldiers and sailors against unemployment on their return from the wars, and it is further urged instead of relying on the time-honoured policy of muddling through when the hour arrives, that preparations for tackling a prob- lem of such obvious magnitude should be entered upon now. An important Committee, which includes Earl Grey and Sir H. Rider Haggard has been formed for the purpose of finding work at home and in the dominions for service men on the disbanding of the forces, and pressure is being brought to bear on the British Government to act in conjunction with the various dominions interested towards having the matter organised on an imperial footing. The Salvation Army, with its highly efficient emigration and colonisation department is naturally interested in the overseas aspect of this movement, and Commissioner D. C. Lamb, who is a member of the committee already referred to, has issued a circular in which, while dealing fully with the after-war situa- tion, he brings forward some practical sugges- tions for the absorbing of the vast body of men who will find themselves displaced in the in- dustrial dislocation which it is assumed will form a chief feature of the war's aftermath.

. A call has come to everyone to give his best, if need be, his all, for his country's safety. The call has been loyally responded to; taking the country as a whole the answer has been magnificent.

There is, however, one form of service the importance of which has scarcely been real- ised. Attention-has been called to it by the chancellor of the Exchequer. We refer to the necessity of saving the national wealth. This is desirable at all timeS, but at this, hour it is an imperative necessity. As Mr. Lloyd George pointed out, we are the richest coUntry in the world, and if we could realise our wealth, our present expenditure, enormous as it ,is,' would not exhaust our resources for many years. But we have only to consider in what these riches consist to see how exhaus- tion could come about while the aggregate amount of individual possessions might still be great. Our riches consist in goods, land, plant, buildings, &c., all over the world. We own the larger part of the public undertakings in our Colonies; the railroads in South America are largely the property of Britishers, and so all over the world. But riches in these forms cannot be used in making shells,or in feeding troops; they carinot readily be changed into serviceable wealth. In ordinary. conditions, when the world has surplus wealth to invest we can readily realise our posses- sions, because they are saleable as shares, and when there are 'buyers our scattered wealth .can be changed in form and brought home. There is no doubt that a considerable amount of realisation is going on, as the increase in our imports indicates, for it must be borne in mind that any wealth realised abroad can only come home in the form of goods. But in tithes like the present, where not alone' at home, but all the world over, there is a large and an increasing drain upon wealth in liquid form for war purposes, .it is difficult, almost impossible, to realise. No doubt there is still a market for these shares, but we could only get about half to. two-thirds of their real value. Thus our wealth if realised is at once reduced by"a third, And the more shares are forced on a market that cannot absorb them the more they depreciate in saleable value, although what they represent in plant or tock may not be affected one whit:

The Government have considered seriously the possibility of the exhaustion of available fluid capital, and to conserve this have sternly restricted all unnecessary expenditure of capital, including a great deal which in nor- mal times would be considered necessary and productive outlay. Our local school exten- sion now held up is a case in point.

, •• From what has been said the necessity of saving should be apparent. Our spending should be on necesary things and our surplus banked. There are still some who entertain a confused idea about the effect of spending, and think it is a good thing to spend because it makes work. It ought to be unnecessary to point out tha it is not work we Want to make but welfare, an that work is only a means not an end. Making work for an individual. or class may for the moment bring temporary wellbeing to certain 'units, but if it is not work of a productive character it is a loss to the community. But even•the desirability of providing benefit for individuals at the general expense by making work has now largely passed away; hence those who 'approved and excused their unnecessary spending cannot now take refuge in this argument; there is now more productive work to be done dian there are workers to do it. Having effected saving with a view of serving the country, it is not necessary to invest directly in war loans. If the money is put in any bank it. will gravitate to the right quarter, because banks are large subscribers to the war loans. How does this thing work? A. puts his spare money in the Warrington Savings Bank, this bank invests the accumulated savings in some larger financial concern, which in its turn invests the money in Government stock,

;4

issued to provide for the successful prosecu- tion of the war.

In .this work of saving everyone can help, and no one .need think. that his hide saving is of no importance. The surplus from high Wages, as well as large Profits, which many are now receiving, should be saved and banked. There is nothing heroic, nothing that strikes the imagination in this, but it is a form of national service, the necessity of which, if the war be prolonged, will grow greater. It should be remembered that the present indugrial activity is artificial and wasteful. Europe is wasting in value 'of labour products day and night £lO,OOO a minute. A gigantic shell .is fired, costing anything from £2O to £2OO, and with what result? A deafening report, a puff of smoke, a big hole in the ground a .few miles away, a dozen men killed or crippled. All dead loss, no replacement of the capital or labour pro- duct destroyed. This has been going on now for ten months, night, and, day, and with such a chain: on the world's wealth how is it possible for us to count the state of well- being at the end of this war? He would be a bold man who would confidently prophesy what the world's economic state after this war will be. The conditions are so entirely new that past experiences afford little data upon which to base, prediction. We inow, however, that all past wars have been suc- ceeded by periods of stagnant trade, bringing in their train want and hard times for all classes. • The reason for this is that spare capital has been consumed .by War and there is a shortage in one of the main factors of lindustrial development. If this be the cause of depression then the enormous consumption of wealth in this gigantic war may, and prob- ably will, result in a 'prolonged period of bad trade. But, as we say, the present con- ditions are so unique that past experiences afford us little guide. We cannot positively say what conditions of industrial life will stand revealed when the smoke of battle rises and disappears, and the beneficent nursing Angel of Peace begins her mission of healing and restoration—but whatever the future holds for us it is well to prepare, and every family should lay by, for the proverbial rainy day, especially as by so doing such action will help the Country in its hour of sore need.

WARRINGTON MOTOR AMBULANCE.

May oress's Appeal.

The . Mayoress of Warrington (Mrs. Peter Peacock) in an appeal for funds with Which to purchase a niotor ambulance which shall bear the name of the town and be used for the con- veyance of .oounded at the front, says:—

"I have been communicated with by the British Red Cross Society, with regard to their urgent need of motor ambulances for the front. It is suggested that each town might present a motor ambulance, which would bear its name, and, I feel sure that there are many who would welcome the opportunity of contributing towards the donation of a motor -ambulance bearing the name of the -town from which so many have gone forth to fight for their coun- try. Sir Frederick Troves has, detcribed the fleet of motor ambulances as 'the most valuable service ever rendered to theAr`my Medical Depart- ment in the form of voluntary aid. In the sav- ing of life, in the lessening of suffering and in the securing of prompt surgical treatment for the wounded, these ambulances have done a work the value of which can hardly be ex- aggerated.'

£6OO Will Meet All Costs.

"I am sure you will appreciate the weight

of these words, coming as they do fkom such an "authority. The need 'for the service in- creases every day, and it is only by the volun- tary self sacrifice of Ike charitably disposed that the work can be carried through to ,the end. I am issuing a public appeal for a War- rington ambulance ; may I have the pleasure of including your name in my first list of sub- scribers? The amount required for a fully. equipped four-Stretcher motor ambulance £4OO, and the upkeep and running expenses will add an additional £2OO, or ..tOOO in all."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
A GOOD FIRST LIST, PLEASE:

Owing probably to this being a holiday week, the Mayoress has not received the sup- port which the nature of the appeal warranted. It is to be hoped that Warrington will not be behind other towns in this work of mercy, and that the 3layoress will have a good list of donations to announce in her first list, which will be published in the local papers next Satur- day.

Oh happy, joyous Springtime, when Nature's at her best; • And earth and all creation emerge from Winter's test, In early morn awaking thy wondrous sights I see, A voice within e speaking of a grander Deityl -

I sit on the hill-crest musing, in sunny-golden rays. And. look eim earth's fresh mantle—while birds . pipe out their lays— Down rocks the brook is rippling in fairylike , cascades, To freshen flowers and seedlings, and the grass's tender blades.

The bells in the quaint church-tower • their peaceful message send, And my 'full heart sings with the Springtime, as I hasten to attend. . . . But al! what a cloud is hovering; there is anger in the sky, For the bugle-call is sounding, and the enemy is nigh. .. .

Fierce and wild on sea and land the deadly cannon roar; Confusion reigns on the battle-field—La seething mass of gore: Husbands, sons and comrades—heroes proven in the test— . Freely giving life for country, sink to nameless rest. •

Hot tears dim my eyes. A black-edged missive came to-day, And my swimming bead could not take hi what the fatal words might Fay; Bat I braced myself for the Ordeal—ancl the letter said he'd gone, For thb children's sake and his wife's sake, for his country and his home. .

Transformed is the crystal fountain to a weltering stream of blood; The bells with their once-soothing cadence now wail in ominous wood, And the mystical musical waters flow on with a gurgling sigh. Sweet fields, flower-bedecked, have been altered to a death-couch where heroes may lie.

B. .1. B.
