THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 29, 1915.

DRUIDS A.M.C.

Roll Of Honour Of Fighting Members

To Be Prepared.

IMPORTANT CENTRALISATION

PROPOSALS.

Continued from Pages 6, 7 and 8.

The final session of the _lanual Movable Delegation of the Order of Druids Friendly Society was held yesterday at the Patten Hall, Winmarleigh-street, when Grand Master Edwin Allen presided, his advent on the platform being the signal for loud applause, the members recalling the cause, of his absence the previous day. Before qpening the business the Grand Master thanked the delegates for their resolution of sympathy with him in his dark hours of uncer- tainty concerning the safety of his son, who [as recorded elsewhere] was, serving aboard H.M.S. Triumph when she was sunk. The G.M. "hoped that his absence had not had all adverse effect upon the business arrangements. Up to that morning he had received no definite news as to the safety or otherwise of his son,, though he hoped that when the news did come it would be of a cheering nature, and would say that- he had been saved. (Hear, hear.) .

A ROLL OF HONOUR.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
Permanent Record of Cru:r;-Soidiers to be

Made.

Bro. G.. Blackburn (Leeds) proposed that the Board of Management should be asked to in- struct the Grand Secretary to prepare a record of the names of every single member- of the society who had volunteered his services for the Army or Navy. The least they could do would be to preserve in the head offices. at Manchester the names of every member who had come for- ward in the defence of his country; aye, of civilisation. (Hear, hear.) . Bro. Thomas March (Sheffield) seconded; re- marking that the least they could do for the gallant members was to keep their names before them and pass them on to posterity. (Hear, hear.) The proposition was carried.

ARE THERE TOO MANY DELEGATES? Majority of Those Present Think Not.

The Delegation then turned its attention once more to revising the constitutional rules. The Darlington and Hanley Equalised Dis- tricts sought to persuade the Delegation to make ah alteration of rule, whereby at future A.M.D.'s it should only be possible to send one delegate for every thousand members, in- stead of one for every 500, as at present. The proposal to reduce the Delegation was defeated by 80 votes to 67.,

TELLERS' ALLOWANCES INCREASED.

An amendment of rule, whereby the tellers will receive 10s. per day, instead of the 7s: 6d. hitherto paid, was unanimously adopted.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CENTRALISATION PROPOSALS

Referred Back For Reconsideration.

A lengthy debate arose out of the report of the Board of Management, which proposed centralisation in groups in the Order for the purposes of administering the National Insur- ance Act. The Scheme, which was explained to the delegates by the Grand Secretary, had for its objects the banding together of certain districts for mutual assistance, or for "spread- jag the risks," in dealing with the , delicate claims of National Insurance, but it also in- volved, as a matter of course, an alteration in the proportion of funds ' allocated to general funds, and retained by the branches. The scheme, said. Bro. Shaw, was submitted by the Board in order that it might be in a position to administer the Act in respect of those branches which were themselves getting tired of administering it, or who were not in a posi- tion to administer it efficiently. In the case of such branches the Board thought that the best plan was that their engagements should be transferred to the Order, who would be in a position. to administer the benefits for them. and the resolution which was to be submitted would apply to such branches as had already passed a resolution favouring the transference of their engagement under the National In- surance Act to the Order.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
A DELEGATE'S FEARS.

Bro. Blackburn (Leeds) feared that the pass- ing of the scheme "would swallow the Order as a Friendly Society and turn it into an indus- trial insurance company." The proposal made would rob the society of its democratic char- acter; the centralisation proposals would, in effect, constitute a Board of Directors. Bro. Caunt (Sheffield) said that the lodges would become little more than collecting agen- cies if the Board's proposals were adopted. _ _ _ _ _ .

'The Grand Master -denied , that there was warrant for the feeling that if, the centralisa- tien proposals were given effect to branch life and activity would be crushed, for as a matter of fact the branches would have the same oppor- tunity of taking the same interest in the man- agement of their • business as they had A the present time. After considerable discussion,' Byo. Copping (Darlington) proposed as, an 'amendment that the matter be referred back to the Board for further consideration, and eventually his amendment was carried, 46,215 votes out of a total of 73,918 being cast in favour of it."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
THANKS-IN VERBAL AND TANGIBLE

FORM.

The remaining proceedings were mostly com- plimentary in 'character, the new Grand Officers being formally installed and the retiring Grand Master thanked for his , services during a strenuous year of office. .

The MM.- of £2O was- voted to the retiring Grand Master, and the sum of 10 guineas was set aside to be handed to the Mayor for dis- tribution among local charities. A purse, con- taining 8 guineas, subscribed by the delegates, was also handed .over to Bro. Arthur Shaw, the secretary of the local committee, in acknow- ledgment of services rendered in making the arrangements for the delegation._ _

The- local Press were -accorded a vote of thanks, after 'which the proceedings were brought to a close with the singing of "And Lang Syne " and the National Anthem.

REV. W. BRACECAMP.

Denounces Ahti-German- Riots

Appeals For Recruits.

Major the Rev. W. Bracecamp, otaplain of the South Lancashire Brigade, Made a-stirring appeal at a recruiting Meeting held on Tun- bridge Wells Common on Sundays when over 7,000 people. were present. In the course of his remarks he deprecated very strongly the anti-German riots which had taken place• in the town the previous night. - In his ''opening remarks the reverend gentle- man thanked the people of Tunbridge Wells for the way they had. looked after his "boys." He could assure them that, the troops appre- ciated their kindness. lie had a letter from one that morning, in which the ,writer asked why did so many able-bodied Men still remain at home When their country needed them, and appealed to them most urgently in a caue which ww. just and honourable. No man should s, he , would join the Army when there was not work: to be done, or when the loafers .from the street corners Went. He thought this was an awful tiling for a Britisher to say, to depend upon loafers of the towns to safeguard the Women and children and homes of their Empire. Lord ' Kitchener believed, that one volunteer, was far better than two pressed men,_ and he hoped the country would never have need for conscription:, but that they would prove themselves Britishf in the long run. .The Belgians and French had proved their worth, and now it was for the British to/ do. the same, and, with the help of the remain- ing able-bodied men, they would do so, and show the German_ church-destroyers and Red Cross nurse killers what it whs to fight for honour and to avenge an insult. That was a letter written. •by one, of the " boYs," who some of them entertained -,when in Tunbridge Wells, and that was the message to them that afternoon. would he very sorry for the men who refused to go, when peace was de- clared: lie was sure their conscience would .not be very easyi, and their friends would look down upon them as cowards. Ile believed the reason men held back was because they did not understand what -patriotism meant. Some men seemed to think that to be a patriot all one had to do was to shout and join in a riot, as some of, them did. the •previous night. That was not patriotism. , SUGGESTED LOVERS' I'ALK.,

He then appealed to the women to help. He wanted all the lovers in that large meeting that afternoon to have a serious talk as they walked home, and. to say to their boys some- thing of this kind, "I love you from the bot- tom of my heart; I long to get married later on. (Laughter.) I want you to make me a home, but before you do it I want you to do your little bit for King and country. I want you to go out there and dd your part, and then come back and I shall love you all the more." (Applause.) He said the girls in Tun- bridge Wells had got on splendidly with the soldiers, and it was: because they realised that " Tommy " was a decent chap, because he was making some sacrifice. He also wanted employers to ask themselves whether they were really sacrificing enough in order to let some of their employes go, and they wanted the ladies to help 'them. He was in a shop in Tunbridge Wells when a lady drove up in a motor car and made a small purchase and was very much annoyed, when those be- hind the counter said they could not send her parcel home. He was delighted they could not send it home. No lady to-day should ex- pect things to go on in this terrible war as they did in peace, and and poor— should make sacrifices.

Major Bracecamp concluded with a stronc, appeal to the men to come forward and enlist. We thought because the war was across the water we were perfectly safe, but we were not, and they knew from the Bryce Commission what would happen if the enemy landed.

GREAT DAY IN LOCAL CATHOLIC HISTORY.

Cardinal To Visit New St. Benedict's.

The new St. Benedict's Church, Orford-lane— in course of erection—noir rapidly nears com- pletion, and has progressed favourably in spite of many vicissitudes and delays occasioned by the war.

On Sunday, July 11th—as briefly announced in last 'week's "Examiner"—will take place the solemn opening—at which His Eminence Car- dinal Bourne has promised to attend, together with His Grace the Archbishop of Liverpool and the Bishops of Shrewsbury -and M-enevia. The Rev. Father Herbert Vaughan will preach at the opening Pontifical High Mass—to be sung by- the Right Rev. the Lord Abbot of Ampleforth on the day of the ceremony. It may be noted that Cardinal Bourne will be • the first Cardinal to officiate at any reli- gions service. in Warrington since the Refor- mation, as well as the only Cardinal to visit the town since Cardinal Manning delivered his lecture here on Temperance many years ago. PATRIOTIC ARCHITECT.

The architect for the new edifice is Mr. Matthew Hontin, ,of Liverpool, who , has just left for the front as a captain .with the 10th South Lancashire Regiment. His last work before joining the Army was the series of additions to St. Nicholas' Pro• Cathedral, Copperas-hill. , At St. Benedict's on Sunday evening the Rector (Father Swarbreck) made a touching reference, before Benediction, to Mr. Honan's • departure, and Asked the . congregation's .prayers for him who had done so much for the new church and. the schools of the Parish. C.Y.M.S. MASS MEETING.

. . At a recently-held representative meeting of members of the Warrington and District Cath- olic Young Men' S' Society, it was unanimously decided to hold an annual mass meeting of losAl branches. WHIT-MONDAY TRAGEDY.

Sankey Youth Drowned Whilst

Bathing In. The Mersey.

Coroner Brighouse conducted. an inquiry at the Sloop Inn, Sankey Bridges, yesterday, into the -circumstances surrounding the death of a youth named Arthur Ward, aged eighteen years, of 28, Huntley-street, Sankey, who was drowned in the lersey at Penketh on Whit Monday afternoon. Evidence of identification was given by the deceased's grandmother, Mrs. Mary Hoxley, with , whom the young fellow had been living. Witness stated that her grandson left home, at three o'clock on Monday afternoon, saying that he was going for a wak. Said He Could Swim.

, John Demaine, a joiner, of 18, Huntley-street, Sankey, said that at a quarter to four on the afternoon in question he was walking .along the bank of the river, near Fiddler's Ferry Station, when he saw deceased bathing. He was then about ten yards out. Witness suspected he could not swim and advised him to keep nearer. the edge. Deceased replied 'Wm all right. I can swim." A few minutes afterwards witness noticed that deceased appeared to be in difficul- ties. lie immediately undressed; but on get; ting t 8 the edge of the water saw deceased' dis- appear. He witched for him to come up again, but watched in vain. He then informed the .police. ' The Finding of the Body.

John Sinth, an ironworker, of 38, Plumpton- btreet; Warrington, told how he found the body. He was walking along the bank of the river on Wednesday afternoon, when he saw the body lying on the edge of a sandbank, about a hundred and twenty yards acress the river and a mile from the Ferry, Station Witness went across, dragged the body further on to the sandbank, and gave information to the police. P.C. Henderson gave evidence as to dragging the river in cOmpany with P.S. JOwitt, without meeting with any success. Two days later he was informed that the ;body had been found, and on going to the spot indicated saw the body lying face downwards in the mud. He had it removed and made an examination, finding no marks of violence or any suspicion of foul play. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidentally drowned whilst. katking.".

The exhibition is being held at the Junior Conservative Hall, Market-Place, and opens at 2 p.m., the price for admissiotl being 6d.; after 6 p.m., 3d. Shorthand . Writers.

At a well-attdnded meeting of the Warring- ton Shorthand Writers' Association a paper' from the N.S.A. Lecture Exchange, entitled. "Helps and Hindrances in Acquiring • ,ced," was read. to the members. ' A blackboard demonstration on the choice of outlines and phrases was given in conjunction.' with this in- teresting lecture. The reading matter fox' the speed practice was taken from the writings of Carlyle, Macaulay, Mazzini, and Marie Corelli. Four special tests of difficult matter were given from Carlyle's "Heroes and Hero- Worship," followed by a short discussion on the outlines 'suggested by the demonstrator of the evening.

• More Arrivals at Winwieli AVar Hospital. A further batch of about 160 wounded sol- diers arrived at the Lord Derby War Hospital at Winwick on Monday evening, their arrival being witnessed by the Mayor. There are now upwards of 500 patients in the hospital. Dr. Nash, the Medical Officer of Health for Wim- bledon, has been appointed by the War Office to take control of the hospital. Last Saturday afternoon teams representing the staff of the hospital and a number of convalescent patients who came over with the Australian contin- gent met in a friendly game of cricket, the soldiers winning.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
But Don't Ask. For War Bonus.

Private Ernest Smith, 4th 'South Lancashires, of 21, Norris-street, writing to his brother, John, who is in the Ammunition Column, Bir- mingham, says "I suppose you were begiiming to-think- that I had got lost, but you must excuse me, be- cause I have been in the trenches 13 days, and 'You know very well we ban-not write when in there, because it takes us all our time to keep these poisonous murderers away. When we are off sentry we have to fill sand-bags, and so build up the parapets. We came out of the trenches last night, and I can assure you that I was very glad indeed to get a rest. As you know, we have to go through it. The reason we had such a long spell was because the Germans have been trying to break through at YpreS, and our relieving party was called there, 60 we had to work overtime; ,but we did not get any war bonus. lam surprised at them arguing about war bonuses, and us going through the mill over here. What if we came on strike? Do you think we should get it? , "I have been receiving champion ingredients (cake, &c.) from several of my friends, and also from home. So you see I am having a rare old time. What ho! Cake ! ! Think of it, and in the Army! I believe our Fred (Lance-Corporal P. Smith) is again fit and is again in the firing-line. I am very glad all the Orfoi.d St. John's members are doing their duty, and 'ago that they are all in good health,. &c."

He concludes and wishes to be remembered to all, his workmates (at Richmond's), and also to all his friends, and only hopes that every fit young man of the old town will realise his duty and enlist, as the placard says: "Boys, Come Over Here; You're Wanted !" Yes, and it is. true!

MESSRS. J. &W. DUTTON,

LTD.

‘Veek's Attractions. •

A startling change of fashion is seen this season, and Messrs. J. and W. Dutton, Ltd., of Sankey-street, are to be congratulated .upon the excellent display of the authentic new styles, which we have been privileged to see this week. No such fashion show could be complete without the new models of Royal Worcester Kid- fitting Corsets, and Messrs. Dutton are show- ing all the latest shapes, including those indi- cated in "Fashion's Debut," for wearing with the superb model gowns by the leading French and London houses, now on view in the show- rooms. Every shape is certified correct, and the new models give exceptional value. There are some very fine shapes at 4s. 11d., ss. ltd., and upwards, and an extensive choice of style for all figures.

To-morrow's Band Concert.

The Band of the 2/4th Battalion (P.W.V.) South Lancashire Regiment will perform an attractive programme of music in Bank Park to-morrow afternoon.

Church Walking Day Decision. At a meeting of the Church clergy and Sun- day school superintendents on Wednesday, it was decided not to hold a united procession of Sunday schools on Sunday July 4th, in lieu of Walking Day, but to leave each parish to make its own arrangements in the matter.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
Health Statistics.

Of the nineteen deaths recorded in Warring- ton last week five were from heart disease, four from pneumonia, and two each from enteritis and bronchitis. Twenty-five new scarlet fever cases were admitted to hospital, malcing the total 91. The • births numbered 371,-20 males and 17 females.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
Druids' Back Kitchener.

-At the Delegation banquet .of the Druids Friendly Society, held this week in Warrington, and presided over by Mr. A. H. Crosfield, 180 delegates representing over 100,000 Members of the society being present, a. unanimous resolu- tion was passed. expressing absolute and whole- hearted confidence in Lord Kitchener, enthusi- astic admiration for the magnificent services he has rendered to the nation and the Empire, and. deepest gratitude for his splendid and un- paralleled achievements.

Churchmen and Drink.

At a meeting of the Rural Deanery of ;Win- wick at the Church House, Warrington, last week, the Rev. Ilanon Willis, rural dean, pre- siding, " the Rev. Canon Howson, Rector of Woolton, read a paper on "The duty of Eng- lishmen with regard to the use of intoxicants during and after the war." He intended, he said, to deal with the matter in a spirit of "sweet reasonableness," and from the point of an Englishman's duty in time of war. There was no doubt that the output of m.unitions of war had been hampered by the drinking habits of a certain portion of the workers. The evil had been exaggerated, but it existed. It was nqt often that men had. such a chance, of showing the stuff they were made of by re- stricting themselves for the sake of the com- ,-ou good: If some people denied themselves a good example would be set, and not a few who, during the war, had been total abstainers would remain go when it came to an end.—A prolonged discussion ensued, those taking part including the Revs. A. M. Mitchell, E. E. Chamberlain, R.-, H. Watts; A. White, S. G. Stanton, H. C. Moor, and Mr. J. H. Smethurst. The following resolution was adopted: "That, in the Opinion of this meeting,- every Church- man shoUld be an abstainer during the war, and that this resolution be circulated by the clergy, throughout the Deanery."

The Late Licensee of the Barley Mow.

• The funeral of Mr. James Gleave, who was for nearly forty years licensee of the Barley Mow Hotel in the Market-place, took place on Monday afternoon, the interment taking place in Warrington Cemetery. A service was first conducted by the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton at Holy Trinity Church. The principal mourners were: Mrs. Gleave (widow) and family, Messrs. Har- old Sikock (son-in-law), Arthur Keenan and G. F. Freeman (nephews), and E. Byerle3-. The Warrington and District _Branch of the Licensed Victuall.-Ts' Association was repre- sented by Alderman John Evans (president), Messrs. J. S. Earle, A. E. Denham and T. Partridge. Messrs. Earle, Denham and Part- ridge also represented " Rock " Lodge, No. 1,289, of the Order of Freemasons (Birkenhead). The mourners also included Mr. James Hepherd, and the following representatives of local lodges of the Order: Bros. Edward Rose, T. C. Wads- worth, H. Maddock. Williams, ID F. Green- ough," Allen and W. Cherry. Amongst the inscriptions on the floral tributes were "His wife and children," "Edith and Harold," -" Marie and Egbert," Mr. and Mrs. Alfred J. Silcock, " Annie " and "Marjorie and Burty " (grandchilllren). The funeral arrangements were carried out by Mr. E. Connor.

The Licensed Victuallers' Association, at their last Meeting, passed a resolution elpress- ing condolence with Mrs. Gleave and her family, and the sense of loss felt by the Associa- tion. Reference Was also made in the resolution to the keen appreciation ot the members of the splendid service the deceased gentleman had given the Association, and the Trade in general, duriAg the- thirty-eight years of his association with them.

PEN KETH.

Death of Mrs. Hetherngton.—The passing away of Alr,. Maria Hetherington, 6, Warring- ton-road, on Tuesday evening, takes one more of the old and respected residents from amongst us. Born in Manchester 72 years ago, she spent all her early life in that city and had many • interesting stories of those times and the changes of later days. For some twenty years she has been a resident of Penketh, and on Tnesday evening,, about six o'clock, whilst serving in her • shop, she was seized with a stroke, from which she did not recover, passing away at a quarter-past eleven the same evening.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
STOCKTON HEATH.

Banc of Hope Annual Trip.—The annual trip in connection with St. Thomas'. Band. of Hope took place on Thursday, when over 200 xnembers and friends went by tram and 'train to Overton Hills. The party was under the care of the Vicar (Rev. J. Collins) and Mr. J. Hallows (the superintendent) and a willing band of workers, all of whom did their utmost for.the comfort of the party.. The usual sports were dispensed with this year owing to the war. Dinner and tea were provided at Mersey Vier. After tea, medals and prizes were distributed, for compe- titions and for work done during the season, to Misses M. Clegg, M. Smith, F. White, M. bole, M. Hallows, L. Beswick, P. Dean, N. Burden; F. Wibberley, 11. Hooper and E. White, and George Williams, Alfred Smith, .George Sayers and Reggie Haslam. The prizes were kindly provided by • Mrs. • Milling, •Mrs. C. Hallows, Mrs. NeWall, Mr. Stannard and Mr. Whiteman. The Searetary (Mr. Stannard) said the Band of Hope had had a very SuccessfUl season. The attendance • had been good throughout. • The number on the- books was 225. He regretted the absence of his colleague, Mr. Hough, Votes of thanks were passed to , the Vicar, to those who had given the prizes, and to all who had assisted in .any way, Mr. C. Hallows proposing and Mr; Newall• seconding. The party arrived back in the village about 8 p.m., all well pleased with the day's outing. .

MR. W. FURNESS, RECRUITING OFFICER. Mr. William Furness has been appointed a recruiting officer for the Western Command, a most unusual appointment for a civiliari. The position is an honorary one. Mr. Furness proposes to concentrate upon the works and Shops where available men of military age are employed.

PUBLIC ANIUSEMENIS.

ROLLICKING. COMEDY AT THE

ROYAL COURT.

Humour of a refreshingly original kind fornis the chief feature of the very entertaining musical-comedy-cum-revue running at the Itoyal Court Theatre this week. "Sheba" iS the title of the piece, and, as the name would suggest, the scenes are laid in Egypt, and 013- portunity is taken to introduce a weird EgyP- tian ctance, "The Spirit of the Tomb." Mdlle. Katarina Renoff executes the various evolu- tions with Zest, and gives other examples of her powers as a premiere danseuse. Mr. Dan Hardie is the life of the piece as Aberka, au adventurer, and is nobly backed up by Messrs. Ci•Onfield and Railton (Sammy Sherlock and Peter Watson, police officials). Mr. George Morgan (as the Hon. Sir Charles Crawford) and. Mr. Kenneth Gwynne as Dick Brandon) also give good support. • Miss Isa Gibson makes a charming Susie Warren, and her duets with Mr. Gwynne are very effective and greatly appreciated. The pretty drtsses, the varietY of scenery and the music, combined with the general swing and life in the whole produc- tion, all contribute to the pteasure of the audience. •

TERRITORIAL ROLL OF HONOUR.

A HUNDRED AND THIRTY-SIX CASUALTIES TO DATE.

The local Territorials, judging from the letters that have come to hand and are repro- duced in to-day's "Examiner," have had a long spell in the trenches, and communication in those circumstances is difficult. However, news of several further' casualties has come to hand this week. They are :—Killed, "2; Wounded, 4; as follows :

WOUNDED. Corporal R. F. Anderton. Private Leonard Woodward Private Joseph Stokes. Private William Bowness.

KILLED.

Private Frank Stokes.

Private J. W. DagnaN

KILLED.

PRIVATE JOHN HAYES.

'Tore Particulars Of Cheer) "Terrier's"

Death.

As announced in '" The Examiner " last week Private John Hayes—whose, home is at 11, Regent-street—has been killed while on duty bY a. 2 German grenade at the Front. ; This week further particulars have come to hand; the first letter being received on Tuesday 13Y his wife—from Platoon-Sergeant J. Betts, Who wrote':—

Will you please allow me •to tender to you ' the deepest sympathy of the N.C.O.'s and men Of No. 8 Platoon, B Company, in the hair of , Your greatest trial? Your husband was killed trni Sunday last, May 16th, at 2.30 p.m., by a rifle grenade, one of the most terrifying weapons that we have to face in this ungodly war. It may be some consolation for you to know that he died facing the enemy, being on duty at the time. He was struck in the throat, death being instantaneous. I cannot put on paper how we miss him. His ever-ready jokes and cheerinPss were in- fectious, and took the worst side of war out :64 ef his comrades' minds. I am sorry to say that this week I have lost two cf my best ,4 Men, one of whom was your husband. How We shall miss him I cannot explain; words fail me. It may be some consolation for you to know that he was laid to rest by the side of some More of Warrington's heroes who have fallen in this great fight for England's honour. l's4eY God bless and help you and your Children.

Kill And his Wifi

" One crowded hour of glorious life Is worth an age without a name."

PRIVATE JOIN IIAYES,

An. Art Stddent.

and Family,

Lance-Corporal Percy Ford, of 3, Regent- street, sends word that he has visited the grave—which is in a little churchyard, about a mile behind the firing-line. a. • Other neighbours' Soldiers tell of the sad casualty.

The Mayor tto whom Private •Hayes was related by marriage) has also sent a much- appreciated letter of condolence to the widow.

Born in .Warrington 28 years ago, Private Hayes .v,--as the .son .of the late Mr. William Hayes—who was in his day a well-known foot- ball player , for this town. *Having attended St. -Ann's School till ten years old, the private .gained a 'free diploma to the 'School of Art, .which he attended for the ensuing six months. He then became apprenticed as a painter to Mr. -Frank R.athbone, afterwards being em- ployed by Messrs. Garnett and other firms in Warrington and Altrincham. '` Just before re- enlisting he had obtained a Permanent post with Mr. William Winstanley. , Ten years ago the private joined the Volun- teer movement, leaving after four years. He had altogether had ten years' experience of civilian army work. In October, however, .he decided to rejoin for the duration of the war. • His marriage took place also ten years ago, and he now leaves a widow and four young children. In religion Private Hayes belonged to the Church of England. Several cousins of the dead private are servinc, with • the. Forces—including one wounded in Egypt.

PRIVATE J. W. DAGNALL.

Trenches.

• Private John William Mignon, of '62, School- titi,-;"11. /member of B Company of the War- -6'°ll Territorials—was killed at the front 911 the 20th. The news first reached Warrington 1(14 a Pathetic letter, dated May 21st, to the , ead Private's wife. It was written, by his Plrother-in-law, Private* Joseph Birchall—of School-brow—and ran:— It is with a broken heart that I have-to write this to inform you of the death of your husband, Private John Dagnall. He got hit in the head last night (May 20th), and all that could be done for him was done, but despite the best of attention he did not live above five minutes. It happened while I and a lot more Were carrying barbed wire to the trenches, and he was next to me. You must not let it break your heart . . . but thank God that he did not suffer. Believe me it is a great te3t to have to write this sorrowful news to you, my sister, and my heart opens out for you . but you must bear it with good heart, like a lot more women have had 1° do, and trust in Him above. 0111 Platoon-Sergeant J. Betts, Mrs. Dag. 21,11 has since' received the following, some- " Quit comforting letter::—

It is with extreme regret that I write to in- You of your husband's sad death. He "as fatally shot last night at about 9.30. He one of a party carrying wire entangle- ents to the trenches when he was hit in the "ead by a bullet, death being instantaneous. it is very hard indeed for you to lose such h good husband, which I am sure he must h..ave been. Morale and an honest, upright aring speak for themselves. He was a type Of 'llan that England cannot afford to lose.

His read cheery spirit never left him. Always was Y and willing to lend a pal a liand, he the °rle of the finest men I have ever had nay, honour to command. under your irreparable loss. May God rest his soul and give him everlasting peace, for he died bravely.

The letter adds that Private W. Rowlands (an old 'friend of Private Dagnall) sends his deepest sympathy.

Story Of His Life.

The son of Mr. Thomas ' Dagnall—who now lives at Collin-street, . and is employed at Bewsey Forge—Private John W. Dagnall was born in this town •twenty-six years ago, and received his education at the National School.

Leaving at the age of thirteen, he went to work at MesSrs. Craiks! Ropery, Hopwood- street, where he remained for three years. He then joined Messrs. Rylands in the netting de- partment. At the time of his enlistment he was employed in the Warehouse. Married four and a half years ago, he leaves, to mourn him a widow and one child. He was a bright, cheery young man and enjoyed the regard of his workmates at home and of his comrades-in- arms. He worshipped at the 'Parish Church.

Private Dagnalfiook a keen interest in foot- ball, and at one time played for Rylands's Re- creation F.C. It was on the outbreak of war ilia Private Dagnall answered the call of his country, and entered upon the customary period of training up and down the country. Along' with the other Warrington "Terriers" he proceeded to the front, and in a last letter to his wife, re- ceived on Sunday, he stated that the battalion went into the trenches on May Ist, and did not come out until the 17th, thoroughly tired. He wrote in affectionate terms or his wife and child, and hoped that: with God's help, 'he might be able to return to them.

Major F. C. Cary, commanding A Company, wrote that the men had both been wounded, but by the same post there arrived .a field- card from Private J. Stokes, stating that he himself was wounded and that Frank was dead.

PRIVATE FRANK STOKES.

Brother Wounded Al The Same Time.

News was received on Wednesday of last week that Private Frank Stokes, of the War- rington Territorials, and whose home was at 9, Edward-street, Warrington, had been killed, and that his brother, Private Joseph Stokes, of 12, Buckley-street, had been wounded, both by a German rifle-grenade flung between them as they sat talking. Joseph is now lying in St. Ansehn's Rospital, Wale; Kent.

The following is an extratt from a letter re- ceived from Corporal Robert F. •Anderton, 4th South Lancashire Regiment, May 26th :

"On Wednesday last (Xla-y 19th) about five p.m., whilst in the trenches, a shell burst al- most over my head, a piece of the shrapnel hitting me in the middle of my back between the shoulders. How; on earth I managed to escape by only being hit in one place I don't knbw, as pieces of the shell hit the earth all around me! It was one of those miracles one cannot explain. At the time the shell burst I was bent down warning some of my boys for

guard, and it was a good. job I was in this position,' otherwise I am afraid Is should not be now alive to tell the tale. lam at the base hospital." Corporal Anderton, who, at the outbreak of the war, was engaged in the 'Goods Offices at Bank ,Quay, is a well-known and highly-re- spected Member of . the Warrington Musical Society, and for about six Years has taken part in their operatic productions. He is• a mem- ber of the Conservative Club and his abilities as a billiard player and, bowler enable him to assist materially, in upholding the reputation of th 4 'club teams in those games. He is an old -National--Sahool boy, Mr.' Dewhiurst having been responsible for his education.

THE EXAVIINER, SATURDAYS MAY 29, 1915.

Eoth His Feet Blown Off.

The following was the letter sent by Major Cary to Mrs. Frank Stokes : I am deeply grieved to inform you that your husband, Pte. F. Stokes, was severely wounded this evening. I greatly fear his case is hopeless, as both his feet have been blown off. On behalf of the officers and men of the company may I tender sincere sym- pathy?

===== 0002601_19150529_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
Last Thoughts For His Wife And Children.

Private B. Lynskey, of Refuge-square, War- rington, wrote to Mrs. Stokes, stating that he was with her husband when he died. He dressed his wounds, but he died an hour after he was hit. "God bless my wife and children," were his last words.

Brother Wounded In The Left Arm. Under date May 16th. Major Cary wrote to Mrs. Joseph Stokes as follows :

I regret to inform you that your husband, Private J. Stokes, was severely wounded in left arm this evening by a rifle-grenade. I am glad to say however, that his life is not in danger. Brother Beccribes BroCaer's Death.

Writing from Boulogne Hospital, Private Stokes describes 'how he and his brother were hit.

I dare say by now you will all have heard the sad news," he wrote, "Sad it is for me, but I am sure you will all try and face it, as -I am trying; but it is hard! I have got a

nasty wound in my left arm, but I can thank God 'it is no Norse, and the experi- ence I had I shall lever forget. Frank and I were talking to one another when the Germans pitched a grenade right amongst five of us. One was (Wed instantaneously and our Frank died soon afterwards. I got my arm hurt, an- other chap part of his big toe blown off, and the fifth a slight graze above the eye. I can assure you that Frank suffered no pain."

Dead Man Four Years A Terrier.

Private Frank Stokes. who celebrated his" twenty-first birthday in the trenches, had served for four years in the Territorials, and when the war broke out he had just completed his period of service. A short tune ago he along with a number of comrades had a narrow escape, for a wooden hut in which they. were staying Was .shelled. A fragment of shell broke his watch to pieces, and .tore his waist- coat and. jersey into fragments. He was educated at the Heathside School, and afterwards went to work for the Whitecrosa Company, Ltd., but for the last two years he had been emplOyed by • Messrs. N. 'Greening and Company, Ltd. His workmates received the news of his death With much regret, for he was very popular among them. He leaves a widow and two children.

Wounded Man Rejoined When The Call Came

Private Joseph Stokes served for several years in the machine gun section of the old Volunteers. He rejoined the civilian army as a ,Territorial when the call came at the out- break of war. He was employed in the netting- room at Messrs. Rylands Bros. Ltd. Like his brother he was educated at Heathside School. The brothers Stokes were sons of the late Mi. Thomas Stokes, who worked: for the Pear- son and Knowles Coal and Iron Co. at Bewsey Forge.

Private T. Stokes, another brothei, is sniying with the King's Liverpool Regiment in India'.

rifle-grenade, which exploded and injured all three of them, Private Woodward being hit on the hand.

His Hurt Nct Serious.

"It is not serious," added the writer, ‘!so do not worry, for I shall soon' be all right. I am enclosing two small pieces of the grenade. There are not many people living here, because of the shelling."

PRIX ATE 11.11.111 BOVA NESS.

One Bullet l‘ounds Both His Legs.

Private William Bowness, who resides with his, wife and three children at 47, Tanner's- lane, and joined the Territorials last August on the outbreak of the war, is lying at Cam- bridge in the First Eastern Generar Hospital, wounded in both legs. A bullet hit his right knee, passed through histleg, and lodged in his left instep. The bullet has been removed. Private 13owness is employed in the patent house at Whitecross. He has served that firm since he was 13 years of age, and is highly respected on all hands—both at work and in the field.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
\ON HONOUR'S SCROLL.

LIEUTENANT ALTHOHI) IIIDGINAY.

Dies From Wounds At Cairo.

FOUGHT IN DARDANELLES.

On Thursday morning the news reached Mr. and Mrs. T. J. Ridgway, of Oughtrington, Cheshire, .that their younger son—Lieutenant Althorp Ridgway, of the Australian contingent, which he only joined since the outbreak of war—had died at Cairo from wounds received in land fighting in the Dardanelles. The information was conveyed •in word des- patched from London by, the High Commis- sioner of Australia—who, as we stated last week, sent the first news that the Lieutenant had been wounded.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
High Colonial Position.

• 1 Lieutenant Ridgway was brother of Captain T. Ridgway, of. the Cheshires, and in peace times held the highly important position of Assistant 'White House Engineer to the Com- monwealth of Australia. . •

Shot In The Head Whilst Pointing Out

===== 0002601_19150529_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
At Tie Front Since December.

Twenty-three years of age, Private Kirkham, prior to going into the Army, made his home with his widowed mother, • Mrs. Mary Jane Kirkham, at 13, Liverpool-road, War. rington. His father. Thomas Kirkham, die( when he was quite baby. Ile receive( his education at thl Heathside School, am upon leaving, some years ago, entered th, employ of Messrs. Jos. Crosfield and Sons, with whom he re- mained up .to the tim, of answering the Ca' for men for the Fron He, was drafted to It scene of action in the second week in .Dec- ember and spent his Christmas in the PRIVATE KIRKHAM. trenches. Killed.

" Ever Willing and Cheerful."

Private Kirkham was killed in action on May 11th. Writing four days later, Captain R. N. 0. Myles, of his regiment, said : "I deeply regret to have to inform you of the death of Private D. Kirkham We are ill anxious to share our sorrow with you. He was a real good chap—ever willing and cheerful and always to the front in the matter of doing any Work that was required. I hope you will look with favour upon the action. I took in opening the parcel you sent Mm and distributing the contents among his comrades. I trust that you will bear up under this sad blow and again assure you of the deepest sympathy of all his coinrades."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
" One You Could Always Rely Upon."

Another letter 'was 'written by Sergeant W. McLellan, of "C" Company, of the 2nd South Lancashires=the company to which the deceased belonged. This letter ran :- -"' "You will, no doubt, have heard from the War Office by now. I had a sincere regard for your boy, as everybody else had, too! He was a very quiet, unassuming young fellow, and was one whom you could always rely upon to do his duty. We can ill afford to lose such good chaps and it must be a great blow to any mother to lose so good a son as I am sure Davy was, for I feel certain that ,he was one of the best. . . . Your boy was buried in the corner of a field near a wood and he has a nice little wooden cross over his grave."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
A Brother in the Navy.

The deceased soldier's eldest brother, John, is in the Navy and has attained to warrant rank and is serving on one`of the finest battle- ships in commission, viz., H.M.S. Chatham. Another of the deceased's brothers, Thomas, fought through the South 'African War. Private Kirkham was the youngest of five brothers and he had four sisters. At present his grief-stricken mother is residing with one of her daughters at 67, Scott-street. The late Private Kirkham was, until a com- paratively short time' before enlisting, a mem- ber of a Bible Class in connection with St. Peter's Church.

LOCAL BOXER 11-01'NDED

Had "A Game" ‘Aith The Germans And "Good News" From Home.

Rifleman Joseph McDonough, the well-known local boxer, son of Mrs. McDonough, 53, Oliver- street, and who lives with his sister, Mrs. Thompson, 2, Hale-street, has been wounded. Rifleman McDonough .has two brothers at the Front And two brothers-in-law •in training. He attended St. Benedict's School as a boy, and at the outbreak of the war was employed at Walker's Brewery.

Rifleman McDonoUgh, writing to his mailer and sister from hospital, says his wound is not much, and the doctor says he will be all right

ON THE TRIUMPH.

Alidshipotaii Who Had Sailed All Over The World.

DRUIDS' GRAND MASTER'S SON..

When H.M.S. Triumph was sunk it had on board Midshipman Reginald Allen, son of Mr. Edwin Allen, the Grand Master of the Order of Druids, who, this week, held their annual dele- gation in the town, over which he presided, and of Mrs. Allen, of the Limes, Grappenhall. On Thursday morning Mr. Allen. upon hear-

ing of the Triuinph's fate, immediately com- municated with the Admiralty, and an hour or two later received the following reply : List of survivors Triumph not yet received. BRILLIANT NAVAL CAREER.

Midshipman Reginald Allen was born in War- rington just twenty years ago, and received his first education at the Boteler Grammar School— where he remained until attaining the age of fourteen.

Ile then went 'aboard H.M.S.' Cadet Training Conway for two years, commencing what proved to be 'a brilliant and extensive naval career. In addition to many .certificates for nautical profi- ciency, he gained also the Langton Prize for seamanship. Eventually he was one of four boys—out of 220 Conway scholars—to be nomin- ated as a Royal Naval Reservist for midship- man rank.

Entering now as an apprentice the services of the Elleman Steamship Company, his first ship was the City of Lincoln, and on this he served for twelve months. At the end of that period he was transferred to the Walton Hall, and still later to the City of Norwich, on which he ter- minated his apprenticeship.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
WAR-TIME WHITSUNTIDE.

No Holiday For Many Of The Workers

As befitting times in which the nation is en- gaged in a life and death struggle, the ,Whit- siintide 4 holiday was Observed in a subdued manner.; ~ indeed to many of the workers— these more particularly engaged 'in the manu- facture of necessities for the successful carrying on of the war---it was no holiday at all, but, rememberig the great issue at stake and, maybe, thifiking of the infitely greater sacri- fices , which ar being made "Somewhere in France," there was not a• one who grumbled. This year greater •emphasis in. Warrington, as elsewhere, was laid upon the religious rather than the secular , side of Whitsuntide, and there were large congregations in Most of the local churches ' and chapels. Several of the 'churches in the town were decked with choice early-summer bleoms and in all of them the sermons contained' special reference to the special solemnity of this year's Pentecostql season, the like of which England haS never before lived through: Such as made holiday on, Monday did so -quietly. , "There was none,of the extra special attractions'" usually associated with Whitsun- tide, and in their absence holiday-makers found health and pleasure in the surrounding countryside, the fact that there were none of the, customary excursions preventing those who are not included among the long pursed from venturing far afield. In the evening the usual places of amusement did good business. but, as one manager told our representative, "nothing out of the way for a Monday evening."

SERGE.I NT E. KELLY. Killed.

conveyed in .a letter written by Major Ernest English, who is in command of "B " Com- pany. . .

"Conscientious and a Real Good Fellbw."

The letter, which . was received on Tuesday, was as follows : ,

• "I regret extremely to have to inform you that your husband, Sergeant E. Kelly, of my company, was shot in the head on the morn- ing of the 18th inst., and was badly wounded, and am very sorry to add that, yesterday, *e heard that he had died of his wounds. Please accept deepest sympathy in your and your children's loss. He was doing his duty at the time he was wounded, 'and was working in front at some wire we were putting up. I shall feel his loss extremely, as he was a very good sergeant—conscientious and a real good fellow, from what I knew of him! lam sure he was a good husband and father. This is a terrible war, and none of us know when our turn is coming, and very many of us—l among others—have wives and children at home, who are going through the same anxious time. Again accept all my sympathy and condolence."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
Much Liked By All.

The following letter has also been received from Corporal Hudson "I. presume that by this time you will have received the, news of your husband's death. I can assure you that we are all very sorry to lose such a good and capable N.C.0., who was very much liked by all—officers, N.C.O.'s and men, who all send their deepest sympathy to you. I can, also, assure you that he always did his duty with a good heart. In fact, he was in the act of pointing out to my platoon officer some barbed-wire entanglements that he and myself had put out the night before, and only his head was e:.,:posed to the enemy's trenches, when a bullet from the left front struck him in the head. He was immediately attended to and made as comfortable as pos- sible under the circumstances. He was wounded at 1.50 on the 17th inst. and died on the following day. If there is anything you would like to know I should be only too pleased to oblige ,you. lam sending a few of his things from :headquarters—his watch, chain and discharge sheet—as soon as I come out of the trenches."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
Fond of Football And Bowling.

Sergeant Edward Kelly; who was a reservist, formerly resided with his wife and two chil- dren at 102, Hume-street. He was employed as a shunter at the Whitecross Company's Aworks. He was educated at Arpley-street School. He was very fond of football and bowling and .had played the former game for the Whitecross team. He wept to the Front in August.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
"MUST HAVE SOMEONE PRAYING FOR ME."

Ilin% Tv% ice-Wounded Lance-Corporal Explains Ills Escapes From Death.

Writing on May 6th to his wife, who lives at 91,, Pierpoint-street. Bewsey, Warrington, Lance-Corporal W. Clarke, of the 4th King's Liverpool Regiment, says.: We have , had some. terrible times to pass through during the last two weeks, as our brigade has been doing, some hard fighting. I must have had someone praying for „me, as I have had some narrow escapes

===== 0002601_19150529_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
Led His Platoon into Action.

"1 led my platoon into action, as our sergeant got hit as we started, and I tell you it Was just like being in hell. I had men of my section on. my right and left getting killed and wounded, but still I kept on, and bow I missed getting hit .God -only knows. . . . I was just leaving our captain. after receiving orders, when a Jack Johnson' burst right over us in the trench, covered us with dust, and gave me a slight wound in my •eye. Sergt. Robinson, who was with 'me, was killed. How I missed being hit I don't know, but I am still trusting in God. I have to see a lot.tOf 'fighting yet, and a chap does not know the minute he is going to get hit . . I have not had my 'boots off for three weeks, sO that you can guess what I am like." ,

dead in a field which I crossed. I got your letter, but I could not read it for a few days, for we were fighting all. the time. .

If the Young Men in England Only Knew -

"I am writing these few lines under heavy shell fire. If the young men in England only knew what we are going through, ad the -hard- ships we have to endure, they would ,all enlist, and come to help us. We are gaining ground all the way and giving the Germans all they want. I went out last night and buried four men, and we got shelled. AS soon as I got back to my trench a shell burst and wounded two men by my side. I also got wounded in the head, but not. seriously. I could do with a good feed, st) please send me a small parcel and a bit of 'bacon.' We lost two killed and 'fifty wounded yesterday, so you see a chap does not know when it is his turn." -

Lance-Corporal Clarke served for two years in the Boer War.

PRIVATE DAVID KIRKHAM.

f` lie Was A Real Good Chap," Writes ills Captain.

Another of Warrington's brave sons who has given his life for his country is Private David Kirkham, of the 2nd South Lancashires, who on the Ist of September last offered his, ser- vices to King and country and was accepted, find, in a few days. was undergoing that mili- tary training which was to fit him to be a good soldier he actually proved to be, for his officers spoke very highly of him.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
Fighting for a Fortnight.

In a later letter Lance-Corporal Clarke says: "I can't tell you all the terrible things I have gone through these last two weeks. I have been fighting all the time, and the sights I have seen and the terrible hardships I have gone through, ! I have been hit twice—once in my head land also on my hip, but the. hurts are not severe. It is not warfare but murder I passed along some trenches where the dead ,were five feet high, and there were hundreds of

in a week' S time and fit to go back—" so you have no need to worry, as I am in the pink." "I got hit in the ear and neck with a piece of shrapnel," he. continues. . " • • I .don't think it will last long, as we have been giving it to them pretty-hot this last week Or so. I was wounded at Ypres. We did make them run. What did not hop it we took prisoners. We did have a game with them. Two lads and I had two tied to a tree. They kept saying 'Good English won't shoot.' The sisters and nurses in the hospital are nice and gentle, and will do anything you ask. "At the time I got your letter, mother, and Dick's and J. Creaghan's, I was in the trenches, and I was more pleased than if someone had given me a pound note, because ,I was worrying and thinking how things were at home, and when I opened the letters and found things were all right at home I was happy 83 a king.'s-

FROM EAST TO WEST.

-By now he had journeyed all the world over. His first trip was to South Africa and Australia —and on that he was absent for twelve mcinths. Thence.-he voyaged to New York; and later to India. After a return home he went Out to China and Japan—calling at a large number of ports on the way: On the Continent he had visited places around the Adriatic...whilst engaged on merchant ves- sels.

It is interesting to note that twelve months ago he was on H.M.S. Weymouth—a the North of Scotland—during the manceuvres, which were witnessed by Mr. Winston Churchill.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
OUTBREAK OF WAR.

When the war broke out, Midshipman Allen was. on a return voyage from India, and upon his arrival home obtained special permission from the Admiralty to sit for the second mate's Board of Trade Certificate. This he obtained.

Then called up by ,the Admiralty, he was ap- pointed to midshipman's rating, and on Febrizt- ary 19th took part in the first bombardment of the Dardanelles.

In all the action's- which have since taken place he played a part.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
RIFLEMAN WILLIAM SYERS.

Dne Of Four Soldier-Brothers Killed.

News was received on Friday of last week that Rifleman William Syers, of B Company, 2nd Battalion Rifle Brigade, lind been killed in action. The news, which took the form of a Postcard, written by Sergt. Holloway, of the Rifle Brigade, was conveyed to a friend in Cart- wright-street. It stated that the Rifle Brigade had been engaged in a charge on the enemy's position, and it was when returning with his Battalion that Rifleman Syers received a fatal wound.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
H ibern ians Express Their Sympathy

The following message of sympathy was re- ceived from Mr. T. Griffin, hon. secretary of the local branch of the Ancient Order of Hiber- nians, of which the late Private Was a mem- ber: "I am requested by the members of our division to forward you a letter of condolence and heartfelt sympathy in the irreparable loss which you have sustained throngh the death of your beloved son."

Went to the Front in January.

Rifleman Syers was educated at Sacred Heart School, and after leaving there, at the age of fourteen, went to work at .Messrs. Reynolds's, tannery in Mersey-street, 'where he remained until offering himself for service in September. After undergoing the necessary training at Win- chester, and other places, he was drafted to the Front in January. He formerly attended the Sacred Heart Church. Three of the deceased soldier's brothers are serving with the colours

ANOTHER PEARSON & KNOWLES'

LABOURER KILLED.

Shot While Driving Away Germans.

Rifleman Michael Glynn, son of Mr. and Mrs. Glynn, 14, Philip-street, whose fathet is serving in the National Reserve, has been killed. Rifleman Glynn was a labourer. at the works of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, and enlisted in the Rifle Brigade at the outbreak of the war. He attended St. Alban's School and Church. He was 20 years of age. • A friend writing to the gallant Rifleman's mother, says that early on Friday, May 14, the Germans opened a terrible shell fire. "Jack Johnsons" fell in the trenches and all around for four hours, and then the Germans made an attack.

"What were left of us," he continues, "gave them some lead, and they went back quicker than they Came. Mick and I were firing as quickly as we could load, and seeing them run- ning we couldn't _help but hit them. Very few got back, but one or two must have been lying in the trenches, for as soon as the enemy re- gained the trenches there was firing through the loopholes, and your dear son was hit and died instantly. He was laid to rest at night Everyone liked him, and I especially miss him very much. •

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 29, 1915.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
Opt R OF DRUI DELEGATION IN WARRINGTON.

service of their country by paying their contri- butions whilst they were away. (Applause.)

For Members Who Have Gone to the Front. The Friendly Societies had a big future before

them when their brethren came -back from the war, but in the meantime those who were left behind were the right people to see that those who had gone should not suffer in the matter of their membership, and he was confident that they would not shirk any extra levies in order to look after the fighting members. (Applause.) He was particularly proud that they had hon- oured Warrington by selecting it for their con- ference. It was such an unusual thing; indeed, it was a matter of forty years since they had the last conference of that kind in the town. Along with some of his brother councillors, he tried a few years ago to get the Ancient Order of Foresters to hold their 'High Court in the town, but the majority of the delegates fixed on Manchester, "apparently regarding Warrington as an unimportant town. The whole of the Friendly Society members Of Warrington felt proud that one among them should have been selected as Grand Master of the Druids. They looked upon Brother Allen as one of the stal- warts in the Frienaly Society world. It had been his (the speaker's) privilege to meet Bro. Allen on the Friendly Society's Council, and he knew the good work he had done—as also had other Druids—work which was a credit to any society. (Hear, hear.) A High Honour.

would like to congratulate the delegates upon the high honour which had been conferred upoßro. R. Verney Clayton, their Grand Treas urer. Speaking in the Friendly Society .sense, he had received, by his appointment as Presi- dent of the National Conference of Friendly Societies, the highest honour in the land. (Ap- plause.) By that appointment they had a friend at court who might, when the Friendly Socie- __

ties came to meet- the National Insurance Com- missioners, be able to get lots of points turned in their favour—(laughter and applause)—and, in any event, they ought to be proud to think that their society had been honoured by having the chairmanship of so important a body as the National Conference conferred upon one of their members. He (the speaker) looked upon the voluntary side of thrift work as something like the voluntary system in the Army, as against conscription. Befoie the National Insurance Act came into operation they all felt that they were on the side of voluntarism in this matter, and they pleaded from different platforms that it was the duty of every man to look after him- self in the matter of providing for his future, and they were not surprised—although they did not like it—when the Government, realising that that provision ought to be made, stepped in and said that they would have conscription in the matter of insuring against ill-health, &c., and said that every man over sixteen must join. (Hear, hear.)

their social differences, and they were all working together as Englishmen who loved their town and their country and were determined to do all in their power to see the struggle through. (Hear, hear.) But "when the hurly-burly's done; when the battle's lost and won," and when once more England's flag flew at the top, supreme over all other flags,

they would return to their duties in other direc- tion's, and proceed anew with the task of im- proving/Warrington. He hoped that in the not very distant future the Druids might come again and that then, after more peaceful and happier times, they might see the great schemes of the Council completed, and find the great industries of the town flourighing even more 'magnificently than they Ji l flourished in the past. (Hear, hear.) Aide: inan Bennett concluded by offering to show any of the delegates interested over the Warrington Academy, an offer which was re- ceived with loud applause.

ALDERMAN TINNION'S FELICITATIONS. Warrington a Town Worth Visiting.

Alderman Tinnion next addressed a few words of greeting to the delegates, and said that as a Friendly Society worker—as one who had taken some interest in the work in which the dele- gates were engaged—he was delighted to know that Warrington was selected as the meeting place of the delegation that year, whilst he was even more particularly delighted that his good friend, Grand Master Allen, had been raised to the highest dignity and honour in the Order that could be conferred upon him. (Applause.) With all his heart he welcomed them to the town of his adoption, and a town of which he was very proud. Alderman Bennett could sing the praises of Warrington better than any man he (the speaker) knew, but Alderman Bennett could not sing them more sincerely than he. Warrington was a fine town; a splendid town— it was a town worth visiting. No town in Eng- land had benefited more than had Warrington from the employment and cultivation of the great and glorious principles which they, as Friendly Society men, represented. (Applause.) He hoped that the result of their deliberations would be progress upward and onward. (Re- newed applause.)

and the workmen engaged on it were so im- pressed with the importance of seeing Alder- man .Bennett that they were delayed in their work. That was why the bridge took so long to build. (Renewed laughter.) Warrington was famous for other things besides those that had been mentioned. It was famous for its gas—particularly in the Council Chamber. (Laughter.) Then there was the Fire Bri- gade! Councillor Gough, who was on the platform that day, was so conscientious in the discharge of the duties devolving on the Chairman of the Fire Brigade that he had given orders to the Captain that no fires were to be put out until )le got there. 'Laughter.) He (Bro. Bradshaw) hoped that the Druids' visit would be, educationally and socially, a very successful one, and that the arrangements made would be to• everyone's satisfaction. Warrington was doubly honoured in that Grand Master Allen was a Warrington man, and the Conference was being held in the town. (Applause.) The chief object in the, life of Bro. Allen seemed to be to do something for his

fellow-members: They had in their- Grand Secretary one of the best secretaries a Friendly Society ever had: It was to be hoped that that seties of gatherings would result in a rousing, of the slackers in the Friendly Society move- ment to a realisation of how very greatly they could help by taking a more live interest in the affairs •of their society. (Applause.)

burst. Almost unrealised by the. nation, it has been overhanging us for many years. Its first and fullest fury is spent, and we are justly proud that the nation, and with it the nation's ttatde (upon which, as a Friendly Society, much of our prosperity depends), has met and sur- vived the shock in a manner that enables us to look forward to the end with confidence and de- termination. Almost 7,000 of our members have joined either the Army or Navy. It is highly probably that before victory and peace is reached, many of these will have laid down their lives in what we consider to be a just and righteous cause. To the relatives of those who have fallen we extend our sincere sympathy. Many of our districts have decided, voluntarily, to be levied in order to pay the contributions of their members who have enlisted. On the other hand, I understand a few districts have decided to forego the contributions, yet keeping the members entitled to receive full 'benefits. This is a very dangerous step, for it cannot be denied that their liabilities are likely to be heavy, and without contributions their funds will soon become exhausted; therefore, instead of their action being one of real\ sympathy and assistance, it may ultimately be one of great hardship to the member or his dependants.

tatively stated that branches with less than 100. members are actuarially unsound, and we have not got to stretch our imagination very far for us to see that it is a very small ditch between that number and the membership of many of our branches. It cannot be denied that in our branches we have members who are following

hazardous 'occupations, some undoubtedly have entered upon them since their advent amongst -its, and this militates against the branches hav- ing a low sickness experience and a consequent solvent and happy position; but surely we do not desire to penalise those branches who are doing their best for the benefit and amelioration of their fellow working man. Branch Life and Activity Need not be Destroyed.

"That we have so very readily adopfed and continue the principle of equalisation in our voluntary organisation with such great advan- tage to the members leads me to the conclusion that you will adopt the scheme of centralisation which is now laid before you, and which has been submitted to the Commissioners under the National Insurance Act. 'lt is possible that the proposed future system of administration may have some influence over your decision, but cen- tralisation for valuation purposes does not meaft that branch life and activity need, or will, be destroyed. • \ Too Much Work for Spare Time Secretaries. 'The clerical work carried out by a large num- ber of our branches is performed by spare time - officials, and as the time at their disposal is so uncertain it is daily becoming more evident that they do not care to assume the responsibility of 't discharging the important duties #nposed upon. secretaries. The Order must, I think, be pre- pared in any event to see, in the very near JV future, a considerable transference of the State accountancy from the branches to the central office. It must not be overlooked that the mem- bers only -contribute four-ninths of the benefit, and those contributing the balance have a right , to expect that there shall be no lax administra- tion. I need scarcely remind you that this meet- ing will be the only opportunity afforded you ill which to consider the scheme prior to the valua- tion, and it behoves you to consider the subject in all its bearings before giving your decision this week. What the Valuation Revealed. "Tile quinquennial valuation report by Messrs. Watson and Son of the branches of the Order for the period ending 31st December, 1911, has now been received. It cannot be claimed that, on the whole, it is satisfactorY• About 30 per cent. of the branches certainly come within the standard of solvency as laid down in constitutional rule 34, and the others are more or less well outside or below the stall" dard. Many and varied are the causes set forth in the report which have brought about this unsatisfactory position. Little or no importance - appears to be attached by some branches to filo rate of interest that ought to be obtained upon the investment of funds. A great many of the branches are content to allow all moneys to re- main either in the POst Office or local savings banks at 2,1 per cent., whereas sound invest- mentp could be made yielding not less than 31 and '4 per cent., and if this was adopted a very marked improvement in their financial posi- tion would ensue.

Reforms Recommended by the Valuers. "It is cause for congratulation that most of the deficiency branches have• adopted, or are considering the adoption, of reforms recom- mended by the valuers, which will have the \- effect of bringing about the improvement which every member of the Order so much desires. Application,' to Dissolve Refused. "During the past twelve months applications have been made by several neutral lodges for permission to dissolve; in almost every instance the Board of Management have refused this permission. The branches were adopted by the Order and registered for the purpose of makin%'. certain payments at a time of sickness and death of members, and not with the ol)ject of acCV mulating wealth and then equally dividing it at any period amongst the existing members, irrespective of length of membership or contri- butions. Legal proceedings have been taken against two branches who have acted contrary ' to our permission, and in one case the Court - made an order for the trustees to refund the money and to pay all costs; the other case is still pending, but an order has been made by the Court that the money is to remain intact until after the trial. The Board of Management realise the importance of unity, and with the object of further cementing the bond of member- ship and protecting the interests of the Order, . they have drawn up amendments and additions to the secession rules, which are now submitted for your approval. The Grand Master's Thanks. "My labours during the past year have been . considerably lightened by the hearty co-opera- tion of our Permanent Secretary, Bro. J. W. Shaw, and his staff, the other Grand Officers, and members of the Board of Management. MY flanks are due to them for their unfailing courtesy and assistance in carrying out the veil important work connected with the Order. And finally, brethren, let me say how much I apPre- ciate the great kindness extended to me by even' . district which I have had occasion to visit." - THE OPTIMISTIC. G.M. Bro. T. W. Grundy (of Rotherham) Proposed that the best thanks of the meeting be given: • the Grand Master for his admirable addreGs. It back never been excelled and rarely equalled in the history of the Warrington District. The Vice-Grand Master seconded. Bro. A.
