EFFECT OF STATE INSURANCE.

Business And Social Sides Of An Interesting Delegation.

FULL REPORT BY OUR OWN REPORTERS.

After a lapse of thirty-eight years, the 'Annual Movable Delegation of the Order of Druids has again been held in Warrington. The visiting delegates arrived in the town on Monday and took up their headquarters at the Lion Hotel, where the local members received and welcomed their comrades very warmly.

Decrease in Membership During Past Two Years.

BUT VOLUNTARY SOCIETIES NOT DEAD YET.

be honoured by a visit from the Druids he set himself to see that their annual conference in the town should be made a success in so far as he could do anything to ensure its success, and all present would agree that the function which he had invited them to that evening constituted a very happy inauguration. (Applause.) The proposition having been carried with loud applause,

===== 0002601_19150529_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
A FEW FIGURES.

The total number of delegates present was a hundred and seventy-two, and they represented over seventy-seven thousand members. In 1877, when the Delegation was last held at Warrington, the delegates numbered sixty-nine and the total membership represented was little more than a third of the present figure, being 28,868. At the end of last year the voluntary membership was 62,630, nearly five thousand lower than the figure for the previous year. The State membership totals 88,424, practically two thousand more than in the year 1913. The grand total of members (excluding the Juvenile Section) with which the society started the present year was 108,142.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SOCIAL SIDE.

The meetings have been held at the Patten Hall, but the week's proceedings, which com- menced on Monday evening and terminated yesterday, were by no means w.holly of a busi- ness nature. Warrington's official welcome was cordially given on Monday evening, when the Mayor and Mayoress held a reception in the visitors' honour. On Tuesday a drive and a visit to Alderman Bolton's delightful orchid gardens formed part of the programme, and, with mid-summery weather prevailing, the outing was immensely enjoyed. On Wednes- day, the delegates were informed by the Grand Master, just before the luncheon interval, that the Corporation had made arrangements for the fountain in Bank Quay Park to play for their especial edification. He added, amid un- accountable laughter, something about "those delegates who are interested in water." On Wednesday evening a banquet was held at the Lion Hotel, and Thursday afternoon was pleasantly and profitably occupied with a visit to the soap works of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYORAL RECEPTION

Delegates and Their Ladies Spend Pleasant Evening at the Town Hall. In the evening the delegates, with their ladies, were invited to a reception given at the Town Hall by the Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Peter Peacock), About 170 delegates, including those from the Warrington lodges, accepted the invitation, many of them being accompanied by their wives, and the guests were received in the Council Chamber by the Mayor and Mayoress, after which they passed on to the lawn at the rear of the Hall. The evening was delightfully fine, and the gardens were looking at their best, and the event proved an unqualified success. Crosfield's fine band enter- tained the company with a number of musical selections, ranging from grand opera, via Gil- bert and Sullivan, down to a popular pot-pourri of topical war ditties,,each number being excel- lently played and winning loud applause from the assembled guests. A number of the gentle- men amused themselves with games of bowls on the splendid lawn, which had been imir)od out for the purpose.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
FLORAL GIFT FOR THE MAYORESS.

Early on in the evening Mrs. Allen, the wife of the Grand Master of the Order, presented the Mayoress with a lovely bouquet of flowers. At dusk the company left the gardens and assembled in the Council Chamber and other parts of the Town Hall and partook of light re- freshments, after which his Worship spoke a few words of welcome to his guests.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
MAYOR'S GREETINGS AND GOOD WISHES

In the name of Warrington, he said, he had great pleasure in welcoming the delegates to Warrington, and he sincerely hoped that their deliberations would help forward the Friendly Society movement. As a Friendly Society man himself, though belonging to another society, he knew how difficult was the task they had set themselves. Anything they could do in War- rington to make the delegates feel comfortable or to encourage, them in their great work, they might depend would be done. Personally, he was very glad that the conference had come to Warrington during his Mayoralty, and he ex- tended to them the heartiest Possible welcome that the town could possibly give.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
A VALID EXCUSE, HAD THE MAYOR WANTED ONE.

The Grand Master proposed a vote of thanks to his Worship and the Mayorespi for their hospi- tality that evening, and for the warm welcome the Mayor had extended to the delegates and their wives and,friends. The country was pass- ing through a period of great anxiety as a re- sult of the war, and that fact would have con- stituted a very valid excuse for any Mayor ha 4 he failed to recognise a gathering of delegates such as that. But they had in Alderman Peacock a mayor whose ,sympathies had always been with Friendly Societies. In that connection, he might mention that he had witnessed many local pro- cessions, but he had never seen one which stood out more prominently in his memory than the one which accompanied Alderman Peacock when he paid his first visit as Mayor to the Parish Church. The Rector, on that occasion, antici- pated a very unusual attendance, and he took as his text the words, "Like the vision plain upon the tablets, so that they who run may read." They as Friendly Society members had had that text very plainly and distinctly written upon their minds. It gave him very sincere pleasure to propose that vote of thanks.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
A FRIENDLY SOCIETY MAN OF A VERY HIGH ORDiR.

Bro. IL Ver.ney Clayton, in seconding, men- tioned that it had been his privilege and pleas- ure to meet the Mayor when making arrange- ments for the conference, and he could con- scientiously say that it had been a pleasure from start to finish. Alderman Peacock, they

had learned, was a Friendly Society man ok a very high order; a man who had worked hard in

connection with Friendly Society matters, as he had in civic life and in many other direc- tions. But he should like to say that however hard the Alderman had worked for the Corpora- tion, for the town, and in connection with other useful and beneficent works, he had never done a better work than that which he had under- taken and accomplished for the Friendly Society movement. (Applause.) It was not always that they had a Mayor who took the view with regard to Friendly Society work that Alderman Peacock did, and it was not always that they got such a _ _ _ _

civic welcome as they had had that night. (Hear, • hear.) They truly owed him a deep debt of gratitude, for, throughout. he had played a prominent part in making filo arrangements, as one of the 'Committee, workTng with a whole- heartedness that had won his (the speaker's) admiration.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
A VERY HAPPY INAUGURAL GATHERING

Councillor Chrimes supported, remarking that the Friendly Societies sat Warrington were thankful to the Mayor for the manner in which he had often entertained them. From the moment his Worship knew that the town was to

===== 0002601_19150529_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYOR AND TOWN HALL ACCOMMODATION.

Hi $ Worship, in acknowledgment, referred to the fact that the accommodation for a large gathering at the Town Hall was rather limited, and added that the rooms they were using that night were, however, the only ones in which a Mayor of Warrington could officially entertain visitors. He had done his best to try to show that the Town Hall was an unsuitable place for gatherings like that, and was not worthy of the position of the town, hut he supposed one could not expect everything. lhe company then dispersed.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
BUSINESS BEGUN.

Interesting Civic Welcome And Greetings From Kindred Societies.

GRAND MASTER'S ADDRESS.

The delegates got tO work on Tuesday morning, the Grand Master uttering a few appropriate words on the war and other sub- jects uppermost in the minds of all in these stirring and troublous times. The Patten Hall presented an animated and businesslike appear- ance, the delegates sitting at four long baize- covered tables, which ran the whole length of the building, whilst the principal officers of the Order formed a small group on the platform, in front of which the reporters were accommo- dated, their table being flanked on either side by a profusion of beautiful foliage plants. Grand Master Allen, Warrington, presided, and was supported on the platform by Vice Grand Master John Lavin, Tunstall, vice- chairman,; Past Grand Master Albert Wilde, Swinton, Rotherham, scrutineer; Past Grand Master R. Verney Clayton, Prestwich Park, Manchester, Grand Treasure;; Past District Grand Master John W. Shaw, Oxford-road, Manchester, Grand Secretary; and G H Bladen, Darlington, senior auditor.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
HORROR AT GERMAN CRIMES. The Delegation's Sympathy with War Sufferers.

No form of ritual marked the opening of the meeting, the Grand Master rising in his place on the stroke of ten and remarking that the

country was passing through a period of very grave anxiety and distress of mind owing to the diabolical, atrocious war into which they had been plunged. Their hearts bled when they thought of the loss sustained in the Dardanelles, in Egypt, in France, in Belgium, and upon the high seas, and more recently, and nearer at home, the very sad loss of life sustained in the train accident at Gretna Green. He supposed that almost every family was more or less repre- sented in the war, and he desired that they should place on record their horror at the atrocities which had been perpetrated by their enemies, the Germans, and he would also like to place oxi record their very sincere and deep sympathy with those who had suffered any loss, and also with those who had. suffered any affliction. He thought it would be fitting if at that juncture they one and all joined in singing the National Anthem.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CIVIC WELCOME.

Springing to their feet the delegates sang lustily the first verse of the National Anthem, and then resumed their seats whilst deputa- tions, firstly from the Corporation, and secondly from kindred societies in the town, were re- ceived.

The deputation from the Corporation was headed by the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), and with him were the Deputy-Mayor (Dr. Joseph), Aldermen Tinnion, Bennett and Pier- point, and Councillors Jenkins, Henshall, Gough and Chrimes.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYOR'S HEARTY GREETINGS.

Warrington A Typical Friendly Society Town. The Mayor, who was loudly applauded, said he could only repeat the hearty welcome which he had had the pleasure of extending to the dele- gates the previous evening. Warrington was a town which was named in history, and a town whioh, as they had no doubt been told, was one of many industries. Warrington's welcome should be a particularly hearty one from the fact that it was a typical Friendly Society town, and why should it not be so when they found that there were so many industries in the town, and when the bulk of the population was cora- posed of the best artisan and working class. He was quite sure that they had discovered for themselves the fait that where they had the best workmen they also had the best Friendly Society men.

Voluntary Societies Not Wiped Out By 1 Insurance Act;

They were all taught that self-help was the best help, and whilst they had been passing through a trying time during the past few years through the Insurance Act, he was pleased to say that the voluntary societies had not been wiped out; indeed, given the help and the grit of the Friendly Society members, he felt quite sure they would never be wiped out of existence. (Applause.) If Warrington was a Friendly Society town, Lancashire was likewise a Friendly Society county, but he felt that he must not say too much about that, because he noticed last night that there were present a large number of delegates from Yorkshire. (Laughter.) However, he was proud of Lanca- shire's part in the Friendly Society movement, and proud, too, of the part played by Warring- ton, to which'town he came when he was one year old, and about which he had learned a great deal. After all, it was, perhaps, not too much to expect that Lancashire, with its popu- lation of more than the whole of Ireland, and more also than the population of Scotland, should have had its fair part in the Friendly Society movement, or any .other good move- ment.

Missionaries in the Battle of Life.

He looked upon Friendly Society workers as missionaries in the battle of life. He had been brought up in active Friendly Society work, and still held an active position; indeed; he tnight add, a paid position—in case anybody wanted to know. (Laughter.) He was, in fact, the District Treasurer of the Warrington District of the Ancient Order of Foresters, for holding which post he got £1 a year. (More laughter.) As he had. said before, he felt that the Friendly Society workers and members were missionaries in the battle of 'life, charged with the duty of looking after their sick and distressed brethren, and he knew that the Friendly Societies had done fine with regard to the great war. They had given the best of their manhood from the ranks of the Friendly Societies, and they had gone a step further, and were standing by the members who had offered themselves in the

===== 0002601_19150529_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
Mayor's Word of Advice.

Might he suggest, in order to keep up the voluntary system, that the Druids and kindred societies would have to look after their juveniles and capture them when they were sixteen years old, because if the Friendly Societies did not do so, the other big societies, which were not really friendly societies, would get hold of them? If the Friendly Societies captured the young people at 16, they were in a position to do much to make good citizens of them. He thanked the Druids for coming to Warrington, and felt that the best words of welcome he could utter would be contained in the wish that their deliberations would bear fruit, and that the centralisation scheme whiclf was to come before them would have their earnest consideration. He had h'ad

his own troubles in the matter of centralisation —(laughter)—but he was not going to tell them what his own ideas, were. He hoped that the social side of the conference that had been ar- ranged would meet with the approval of the delegates, and that despite the murkiness of certain parts of the town; they would go away feeling that Warrington was the best place they had ever been in.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
ALDERMAN BENNETT TALKS OF THE TOWN.

The Antiquities and the Industry of Warrington

Alderman Bennett, who also came in for a good reception, began by saying that he, un- fortunately, was not a Friendly Society man, but he had a great deal of admiration for the work which the Friendly Societies did. Like the Mayor, he thought Warrington was the best place in England, and he thought it was a great mistake that the conference had not been there for forty years, and that a good many all& big conferences had not also been held in the town. He was sure there was a good deal about War- rington that they would learn to like, and there was much that was interesting about the place. For instance, Warrington was mentioned in the Domesday Book, but, since he was told that such mention was peculiar to quite a lot of other places, he laid no special store by that fact. (Laughter.) However, if he wanted to make an excursion into the history of Warrington, he dared say that he could take them back in fancy to the time when the very ancient Order of Druids were cutting mistletoe and building altars in the orchards •where the town now stood. (More laughter.)

Many Things to Fascinate Lovers of History.

He could also descant of the time when there was a Roman camp at Warrington. If they were interested in history there were many things about Warrington that would fascinate them. For instance, the town had a church that dated back to Saxon times, and an old manor house that was associated with the names of some of the finest knights of the Middle Ages—a manor house where a murder once took place and which also boasted the usual ghost and a moat. (Laughter.) They had a bridge of which they were very proud. It was the Most recent of six which had stood in the same posi- tion, the first of the series dating back to the year 1300, and round which many great battles had waged; indeed, it was associated very closely with the history of Warrington. If they were interested in education be could point them to the fine old Grammar School, which dated back to the 15th century, and to the Bluecoat School, which was founded in the succeeding century. Then there was the Academy, which had turned out many of Warrington's most illustrious sons, and the Training College, which was one of the first educational institutions of its kind founded in England.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
A Town of Many Industries.

If they were interested in industries they would find much to interest them in Warring- ton, for it was a town of many industries, and its inhabitants was justly proud of its reputa- tion in that direction. If they were interested in flowers, they were shortly going to have their tastes gratified, for they were to inspect the orchid collection belonging to his friend, Alder- man Bolton, and which was spoken of as one of the finest collections in the country. If they liked beautiful scenery they had but a short distance to go over the somewhat dirty river which bisected the town before they found themselves in the heart of one of the most beautiful counties in the country. Many other things one might see; for, in short, Warring- ton was a wonderful old place. At present the town was passing through a transition ,stage. As they walked about the streets they might notice many evidences .that the old order was changing, giving place to new. improvements the War has Held Up.

They would, for instance, notice a few .fine old black And white buildings, and some narrow and picturesque streets; whilst they would also note that the Council were carrying out great public improvements; that they were, when the war arrested their progress,, busy making four streets in the heart of the town into streets that would be as broad and as fine as any streets in Lancashire. At the end of one of those streets was the Bridge, which was the finest of its kind in England, whilst, certainly, there was no bridge that was wider outside London. But now, in- stead of pursuing all the ideals which they had in their minds for the improvement of Warring- ton, they were devoting their undivided energies and enthusiasm to doing their part in the matter of the prosecution of the present very vital war. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150529_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
Pulling Together to Win the. war.

As elsewhere in the country. so, too, in War- rington, were they forgetting their political dif- ferences, their religious differences, and

===== 0002601_19150529_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
FREEMASON'S GREETING TO FRIENDLY

SOCIETY WORKERS.

Councillor Jenkins, who was introduced to the delegates as one who took a very deep inter- est in Friendly\ Society work, said that he was not a'Friendly Society man in the sense in which those present were, but for many years he had been associated with the• Freemasons, with whom Grand Master Allen was also associated, and for whom he had been a great worker. He was reminded of a few words penned by. John Milton in his introduction to "Paradise Lost"; words which struck him, as being singularly ap- propriate to that gathering, and which ran:

What in me is dark, illumine! What is low, raise and support; That, to the height of this great argument I may assert eternal providence, And justify the ways of God to man.

That was what societies like theirs were doing that day, and he wished them every success in their efforts, and that they might be blessed in every endeavour to raise man to his true sphere. (Applause.) Might they justify in their work "the ways of God to man." The Grand Master said that the. delegates had all been interested in listening to the words of welcome uttered by the Mayor and his col- leagues. •

===== 0002601_19150529_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CIVIC CALLERS THANKED.

Grand Treasurer R. Verney Clayton, P.G.M., proposed a vote of thanks to them for their at- tendance; indeed, the delegates were grateful to all Warrington for the splendid welcome which had been given them. (Applause.) Why, they had actually arranged for good weather. (Laughter.) They were deeply grateful to the members of the• Corporation for visiting them that morning. It was a most encouraging sign for -Friendly Societies that they could always depend upon the recognition of the municipali- ties of the country. (Hear, hear.) It was not to be wondered at that that was the case. He

believed that it sprang from the recognition Which the municipal representatives of the great industrial centres had of the great work of an educative and uplifting kind which the Friendly Societies had done. It had taught men for many, many years—long before National Insur- ance was thought about—how to be thrifty and save up for a rainy day.

How Friendly Societies Have Benefited Social Life.

It had also taught them self-reliance; it had helped them not only to govern the affairs of the societies, but had spread its influence over other phases of social life. It was recognised that that was so by the mere fact that the Gov- ernment had taken a stand in introducing the National Insurance Act, and had designed that Act Very much on lines on which the societies had worked for generations before the State ever talked about it. And if the Friendly Societies had not had all the recognition they would have liked, it was not because they had not given up hope of it, but the present was not the time when they could press any Government in the matter of their own difficulties. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150529_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
A PAST GRAND'S PRAISE.

Past Grand Master Albert Wilde seconded the, vote of thanks, and said he had come to the conclusion that Warrington, at any rate that part of it in the Council Chamber, must be noted for great speakers. (Laughter and ap- plause.). He did not know of any assembly of A.M.D. representatives that had received a warmer welcome from the hearts of the civic authorities and people of so prominent a town. (Hear, hear.) There was bound to be good fruit borne of such a reception and such words of en- couragement and appreciation as had fallen from the different speakers. In conclusion he hoped that the town would go on from great things to greater things. (Hear, hear.) The proposition was carried with acclamation, and in calling upon the Mayor to reply, the Grand Master voiced his own sincere thanks to the civic dignitaries for their attendance and encouragement.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYOR MENTIONS A VIVID CONTRAST.

The Mayor, in acknowledgment, said he was sure the Council would appreciate the vote of thanks that had been accorded them. He was exceedingly grateful that his Deputy (Dr. Joseph) was able to make time to come there— (applause)—particularly now that he had added to an already busy life by taking on additional work in connectiOn with the recently opened military hospital at Winwick. (Renewed ap- plause.) The previous evening the Doctor and he were at Arp,ley Station meeting another batch of wounded soldiers, and- almost immedi- 'ately afterwards they went to the reception to the delegates at the Town Hall, and they could not help contrasting in their minds the two different kinds of reception.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====

WHY THE BRIDGE WAS SO LONG IN BUILDING.

Bro. T. P. Bradshaw humorously recom- mended Any representative who might be pre- sent of a civic authority intending to build a bridge to employ Alderman Bennett as archi- tect, as he (Alderman Bennett) knew more about the Warrington Bridge than the man who built it. (Laughter.) He was there every day during the time it was being built,

===== 0002601_19150529_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
CHIEF RANGER'S WELCOME.

Councillor Bro. John thrimes added a word of welcome as District 'Chief Ranger of the Ancient Order of Foresters. He felt sure the services which had been rendered by their Executive Council under the guidance of so able a Grand Master as Bro. Allen had been Productive of very great and beneficial results. The Order of Druids in the Warrington Dis- trict was always ready and willing to take its share in any movement for the advantage and betterment of every member belonging to the great fraternities of Friendly Societies. "We in Warrington," continued Bro. Chrimes, "will do all that is possible to make your stay in our midst as pleasant and enjoy- able as we can, and I trust that your delibera- tions will, be conducive to the good welfare of your Order and the Friendly Societies in general." (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150529_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
A SPIRIT OF ENTHUSIASM.

Bro. George Mayhury said that the holding of the annual Conference at Warrington had stirred up, a spirit of enthusiasm in the Friendly Societies of the town, and it was to be hoped that that spirit would not die down just yet. He thought it would draw the Friendly Societies of Warrington closer to- gether than they had-ever been before. "I have known your Grand Master," said Bro. Maybury, "and been associated with him for many years, and have always found him to be a man of high honour and integrity, not only as a Druid, but as a whole-hearted Friendly Society man. (Hear, hear.) There are knotty problems to face for the future, and what the future may have in store for the Friendly Societies we' do not know, tut what- ever there may be we shall face them and do our best to make them workable for the highest interest and the greatest good to the Friendly Society movement. tHear, hear.) I hope your visit may be productive of good, and when your deliberations are over and mr Conference is ended, you will carry away with you pleasant recollections of your visit to our good old town of Warrington." (Applause.).

GRAND MASTER'S ADDRESS. Fitting Town for Such a Meeting.

The Grand Master then delivered his address as follows: The day that we have all been so . anxiously looking forward to has arrived, and it gives me great pleasure to have the opportu- nity, on behalf of the brethren of the Warring- ton Equalised District, of once more extending to the Annual Movable Delegation of the Order of Druids a hearty and sincere welcome to the County 'Borough of Warrington. This welcome has already been anticipated and given very gracefully and generously by the Mayor and members of the Corporation and members of the kindred societies. We appreciate to the full-the kindly sympathy and interest of our distin- guished citizens in the good work we are en- gaged in. Warrington is essentially a manufac- turing town, and it is fitting that a society such as ours, being principally composed of working men, should hold its annual meeting in our midst. It is claimed that Warrington is a town of many industries, including wire, leather, soap, iron, cotton, Szc. It is also one of. the few towns in England where the curfew bell still continues to ring, a custom which- has been observed for centuries. It is true we have not many attractions for- the visitor, but we take a sincere pride in possessing the widest reinforced concrete roadway bridge in this country. It is 80 feet between the parapets, and has a clear space of 134 feet,. My colleagues of the Warring- ton Equalised District have endeavoured to make every provision for your comfort and en- joyment during your stay this week, and I can only hope that the weather may be favourable so as to enable you to spend a thoroughly happy time amongst us, and that you will return home refreshed and henefited for your visit. A Steady Drop in the Membership.

"This is the second occasion on which the A.M.D. has been held in this County Borough, the previous one being in 1877, when 69 dele- gates were present; representing 28,868 mem- bers.. To-day we have 165 delegates, represent- ing 77,726 members, but our total voluntary membership at 31st December, 1914, was 62,630, a decrease of 4,779 as 'compared with 1913. Our State membership is 88,424, being an increase of 1,946, as compared with 1913. Our total adult membership of males and females at December 31st was 108,142. It has been affirmed that the ,days of Voluntary Friendly Societies are num- bered, and certainly we have had to record, during the past two years, a steady decrease in membership. am, however, optimistic enough to assert that this decline is only of a temporary character. I do not believe that our voluntary system, which can boast of such splendid and beneficent work in the past, has any cause to fear an early demise. The future of our Order is in your hands, and as we organise and con- duct our business, so will the Order prosper or decline. Let us, then, brethren, be determined to so adapt ourselves that we may look forward with confidence to a service of usefulness for many year to come. • Congratulations for the Grand Treasurer.

"It is with pleasure I record the election of our Grand Treasurer, Bro. R. Verney Clayton, to the office of President of the National Confer- ence of Friendly Societies. On your behalf, I offer him our warmest congratulations. We feel proud of the honour conferred upon him, and the distinction which it has given to our Order. I trust he will have a very successful and enjoy- able year of office.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0189.txt | included=true | included =====
A Hint to Some of the Districts.

"More than the usual claims have been made during the past twelve months upon the Order Guarantee Fund. In most 'cases the districts have been reimbursed immediately the whole of the loss sustained, but in others the Board of Management has had to decline all liability through the insured member not being eligible to held office in accordance with constitutional rules and contrary to the conditions endor9ed upon the policy. In some claims the insured has been allowed to hold considerably more money than the amount for which a premium was paid, and therefore the loss in excess has had to be borne by the district. I particularly wish to draw the attention of districts to these irregularities, and point out to them that it is useless paying , premiums unless they comply with all conditions attached to insurance. The duties of auditors in this connection are most responsible and important, and they ought to satisfy themselves that the insured fully com- ply with the rules, and that no excess of money is held by them. All moneys in the bank ought to be certified by a bank official, and moneys in the hands of lodge and district officidls should be produced for their inspection at the time of audit. Strict observance of these: simple pre- cautions would tend to greater efficiency and

safety. Against Dispensing With the Initiation Ceremony.

"I understand that some districts have made it optional for our initiation ceremony to be given or not when introducing new members in their lodges. This, to my mind, is to be re- gretted, for the service is permeated through- out with high ideals of brotherhood and the principles of self-reliance. The beautiful words contained therein cannot fail to make an im- pression for good upon the most callous. It points out the benefits of friendship, and, above all, 'I-, charges them to be charitable, not only in Nverds, but in-deeds.• Work that will Outlive Life.

"Much depends upon our present conduct and management as to our future usefulness and prosperity. Our work will live after this mortal life has passed away, and our successors will either bless or curse us according to the success or failure of our administration. Let us then, therefore, brethren, be determined .to conduct our voluntary work on the lines established and followed by our forefathers, believing that we can relieve distress, not alone by pounds, shil- lings and pence, but also by sympathy, pity, and visitation. Let us strive that our names shall be honoured and revered by all who follow us consequent upon our foresight, ability, and achievement in everything that means success.

Sickness Experience Causing Anxiety.

Our sielciie,, experience during the past twelve months has been such that, to those in authority, is causing some anxiety: To what extent this excessive sickness can be attributed to over-insurance, laxity of administration, or malingering, or even to any or either of the three causes mentioned, it would be impossible to ascertain. That there has been a consider- able laxity and want of care in the issue of medical certificates is admitted all over the country, and undoubtedly this has, to a very large extent, opened the door to the malingerer. To counteract this influence I strongly advise the appointment of extra sick visitors, with definite instructions for irregular visitations and a freer use of the services of medical referees. I know that the cry for funds to enable socie- ties to carry out this important work is a real one, and if no other means can be found to pro- vide the money, then permission must be ob- tained from the proper quarter to debit the cost to the sick fund benefit account. Satisfaction with National Insurance.

"It is now three years since the inception of the National Insurance Act, and we have had ample opportunities of judging its worth and popularity. Such are the infinitesimal contri- butions payabbS by the insured in comparison to the many benefits receivable that I venture to state that not one per cent. of the insured would vote for the withdrawal of the Act, or even for it becoming in the slightest degree in- operative. To the affiliated Friendly, Societies was entrusted the power to administer the Act, and from my own observations and from in- formation conveyed to me, I am forced to the conclusion that the machinery at their disposal was, in part, altogether unsuitable for the due and proper carrying out of the duties connected therewith. Although a large majority of the branch or lodge staff are most capable officials, there are still many who are unsuitable and incapable of performing the intricate and neces- sary clerical work, yet the societies are allowing these officials to muddle along, with the result that chaos is inevitable.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
Commissioners Compliment Secretaries.

" Oug own particular form of government in controlling lodges by districts has enabled us .in many caseS to appoint secretaries who devote their whole time to the duties, with the result that the work has been carried out with a fair amount of success, so much so that on several occasions we have been complimented by the Commissioners' Audit Department for the way the work has been performed. Our thanks are due to the Grand Secretary, Bro. Shaw, for his foresight in obtaining permission for the staff at the head office to perform the clerical Work ordinarily to be done by lodge or district officials who found they could not satisfactorily carry it out. A few of our branches are still muddling through, but the time will assuredly come, and at not very distant date, when they will have to compulsorily surrender the work to more capable hands. I therefore sound a note of warning that if they desire to carry out the work in the future they must perform the duties with greater promptitude and ability than they , have done in the past.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0191.txt | included=true | included =====
A Surprise and a Disappointment.

"Much surprise was given expression to at the outset when it was found that under the National Insurance Act insured persons over 65 and under 70 years of age were excluded from medical benefit. Strong representations of the great injustice to members affected were made to the Government, with the result that a special grant in aid of 2s. 6d. per member per annum was secured, subject to the amount being claimed not later than a given date. To my very great surprise and-disappointment, not 50 per cent. of the money which our society was entitled to last year has been claimed by the branches. It is useless to agitate for a benefit, and, when granted, to let it lie in abeyance through indifference or neglect.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0192.txt | included=true | included =====
The Question of Centralisation.

"Many questions of great importance are brought up in the reporkof the Grand Officers and Board of Management, which will be sub- mitted to you this week for your consideration and decision. The subject of centralisation is of such importance, and may be of far-reaching consequences to our society, that it must be the excuse foi my referring to it now at length. You are aware that under Section 36 of the National Insurance Act it is obligatory upon all Approved Societies to have their liabilities and assets valued every three years. As we are at present constituted, each branch 'will be separ- ately valued, and upon that finding each mem- ber of such branch will have to submit to the conditions disclosed goperning the benefits pay- able for the following three years. NEW GRAND MASTER APPOINTED.

When the Delegation resumed its delibera- tions on Wednesday morning it was announced that the voting strength represented by the delegates present was 76,236, and that a clear Majority .would be 38,114, but later on, when the time for the elections came round, it was stated that, owing to the absence of delegates, the figures had altered to the following: 75,765 and 37,883 respectively.

FORESTERS' FRATERNAL GREETINGS. The Grand Secretary announced that a tele- gram had been received from the Executive Council of the Ancient Order of Foresters con- veying their hearty and fraternal greetings and best wishes for prosperity, combined with the hope that the Delegation would prove of real advantage to the Druids and to the Friendly Society movement as a whole. • The Manches- ter Unity of Oddfellows, who were meeting at Manchester, acknowledged, by wire, the fraternal greetings forwarded them the pre- vious day, as also did the 8.U.0.0.F.

CREATING AN ERRONEOUS IMPRESSION. Bro. Thomas H. Roberts (Manchester), hav- ing got permission from the Grand Master, called attention to a report of the previous day's proceedings which had appeared in one of the Manchester morning papers, which re- presented the Grand Master as suggesting that the Order was run by a lot of incapable offi- cials, a misinterpretation which, he thought, was due to wholesale condensation of the Grand Master's speech, but which was calcul- ated to do serious harm to the Order. A better society did not exist in Manchester, or elsewhere, than the Order of Druids. (Hear, hear.)

GRAND MASTER'S DISCLAIMER. The Grand Master said that what he actually stated was that there were a few incapable officers not only in that society, but throughout every society connected with the working of the Insurance Act. The great majority of the officials connected with the Friendly Society movement, who were carrying out the duties connected with the Insurance Act, were most caVable men. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150529_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
EXTRA WORK, EXTRA MEETINGS.

The Grand Officers and Board of Manage- ment, In the course of their annual report, stated that it would be recognised that the volume of business to be transacted by the Board had enormously increased since the ad- vent of State Insurance. The ordinary meet- ings now occupied two days each quarter, but the time had arrived when three days were necessary. . In the circumstances it was thought that a meeting .one day each month would be to the advantage of the society and convenience of the members of the Board of Management. The Grand Master, in proposing that effect be given to the Board's proposal, said that the cost of 'the Meetings, as at present held, was about XllO, whilst if the proposal were adopted the cost was estimated at about X 135, and for the slightly increased cost they would, un- doubtedly, get more attention paid to matters of management. The business which devolved upon. the Board had so accumulated and been itn!reased since the inauguration of the National Insurance scheme that it was an utter impossibility for the . members to do justice to everything that came before them in two days a quarter. • It was decided that the rules be altered on the lines of the Board of Management's pro- posal. POSITION OF ENLISTED MEMBERS.

How the Order Proposes to Safeguard Their

Interests.

The report of the Grand Officers and Board of Management Set forth the fact that shortly after the outbreak of war between Great Britain and Uermany it became necessary to consider the position of members enlisting with the -Forces. After •careful thought, the Board caused a circular to be issued to the branches, embodying certain recommendations which had for their object the safeguarding of the funds and at the same time ensuring that members fighting for their country would be kept in benefit. Briefly the recommendations were : (a) That where a branch had a benevolent or other fund at its disposal the contributions of serving members should- be paid from that fund; the levy or contributions in support of the fund being increased to such extent as, might be found necessary; and (b) that where no benevolent or other fund was available the contributions of serving members be paid from the proceeds of a levy on the other members. It was not known to what extent the recom- mendations had been adopted, but there was reason to believe that some branches had favoured a course that in the near future was likely to prove highly detrimental from a financial point of view, viz., the excusing of all contributions during service. If the re- commendations of the Board were likely to prove a burden heavier than reasonably could be expected to be borne by the members, then the wiser course would appear to he to pro- vide, by an amendment of rule,. for the sus- pension for the period of the war from sickness benefit, coupled with the retention of the death benefit.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
NOT A CONTROVERSIAL PROPOSAL.

The Grand Treasurer said he did not think there would be anything _controversial in this proposal; it was a resolution which was neces- sary to enable the financial part of the Order's administration to properly locate the amounts. WHAT MANCHESTER DRUIDS HAVE DONE FOR SOLDIER MEMBERS.

Bro. Thomas 11. Roberts said that with re- gard to the matter of retaining in benefit those who had gone to the war, the Druids of Man- chester had endeavoured to do all that was possible. They were in the fortunate position of having a benevolent fund, and it was through that that they proposed to find the necessary funds for keeping the soldier and sailor members in benefit. In many districts, however, there' were no benevolent funds, and consequently levies had been or . must be imposed. The clause was adopted.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
ADDING HONOUR TO HONOUR.

Grand Treasurer's 'Picture To Be Hung At Stonehenge.

Reference was made in the report of the Board of Management. to the fact that the Grand Treasurer (Bro. Verney Clayton) had been unanimously elected President of the National Conference of Friendly Societies, and the Grand Master thought the Delegation ought to take steps to record their pleasure at the honour and distinction which had been accorded, not only to Bro. Clayton, but, also, to the 'Order, by the appointment. He proposed that the appointment be entered on the records of the society. - Bro. E. Stanley (Newcastle) seconded, and, incidentally, remarked • that he thought the Delegation owed thanks to the Friendly Societies ,Conference for the work they had done, especially since the inauguration of the Insurance Act.

Bro. A. Billingtou (Crewe) supported the Grand Master's proposal, referring to Bro. Clayton as one (!if the prime leader in Friendly Society work in the country. NAME ON THE MINUTES NOT ENOUGH. The proposition having\ been carried. Bro. Thomas 11. Roberts opined that, if the Delega- tion's sense of the honour conferred upon Bro. Clayton was merely to be entered in the minute book, it was a barren honour. He favoured some more public record of the Order's appreciation, and suggested a framed and enlarged photograph, to be hung in the halls at Stonehenge, together with an illum,in- ated address, embodying a resolution expres- sive, of the Order's sense of appreciation of the Grand Treasurer's services and of the honour done, through him, to the Order. Bro. J. Caunt (Sheffield) seconded, and it was carried.

HIS TWENTY-FIFTH DELEGATION.

In returning thanks the Grand Treasurer mentioned that that was tht twenty-fifth con- secutive Delegation he had attended; indeed, he believed that nobody present had attended more Delegations. (Applause.) He first at-. tended the Delegation, at Salford, in 1892, and since then had attended every year, as well as being present at the extra Delegation held at Manchester.

• THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 29, 1915.

THE NATIONAL INSURANCE ACT.

More Sickness Since It Came Into Force.

During discussion of another section of the Board of Management's report it was stated that there had been more sickness claims, since the passing of the Insurance Act, than there had been before.

"What," .Said the Grand Master, in submit- ting .the nomination to the meeting, "was said on his behalf last year gained your approval then—when he was appointed Vice Grand Master—and during the past .year your opinion of him has been; both confirmed and justified, and it-gives me the greatest pleasure to declare that he be Grand Master for the ensuing twelve months."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
NEED FOR KEEPING AN EYE ON THE

PURSE.

The Grand Secretary said that it was a fact that sickness had become more acute since the advent of the National Insurance Act. It was highly advisable that Friendly Societies to-day should give extremely careful attention to their finances. Much greater interest was being taken by the working classes in sickness insurance than ever before. The soCiety that did not trouble to put itself in a safe financial position, and in a position to meet its contracts, would not be able to live in the future, in view of the competition amongst the societies. The Order of Druids was keenly aware of those necgssities, and was doing a great deal in the directions indicated, and it was hoped that by the end of 1916 they would be able to carry Out the recom- mendations of the actuaries to take such steps as might be necessary to place each branch in a solvent position. The Vice Grand Master seconded the resolu- tion. He thought it was patent to everybody that, for the wellbeing of the Society, they should so construct their units that their Order should become the greatest factor in the Friendly Society world. The resolution was adopted.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GUARANTEE FUND. Several Claims Pending.

With regard to the guarantee fund, the Grand Officers and Board of Management reported that it was to be regretted that several officers had been proved to be defaulters during the past year. The sum of £144 had been paid in claims and costs, and it was feared that very little of that sum would be recovered. The premium income for the year was £lB6 16s. 3d. and at the end of the year the fund had a credit balance of £461 17s. 3d. Several claims were pending, so that any reduction in the premiums could not be recommended at present. _ _

The Grand Treasurer said that he was one of the originators of the matter. There were many of the branches who would have been in- convenienced financially if they had not been able to obtain help from headquarters. He would like to see more in the fund. If they could get about £l,OOO in hand, they might be able to manage without a premium at all. They ought to be in a satisfactory position before they did anything of that kind. _ _ _

The -resolution was seconded and carried.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0203.txt | included=true | included =====
PRAISE FOR THE HEADQUARTER'S STAFF,

Arising out of another item, the Grand Master said he wished to testify to the ability of every clerk at head office. The Order had in its Grand Secretary a man of marked ability, and also of very great energy. He had a very happy knack of disseminating that energy amongst the rest of his staff. What rises had been given had been given to junior members of the staff; the senior members who had intended to apply for increases had agreed, in view of the war, to postpone their applications. The Vice Grand Master seconded and gave his own testimony to the excellent calibre of the staff.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0204.txt | included=true | included =====
AN UNSUPPORTED SUGGESTION.

Questions as to salaries were raised, and a Brother suggested that it would be a good thing if, in the filling, of vacancies, the committee should see that they engaged no single man who was available for the Army. The suggestion received no support', and the clause was adopted, after the Grand Secretary had pointed out that the Board of Management knew what they were about, and did nothing without careful 'consideration.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0205.txt | included=true | included =====
A "CATCH-'EM-YOUNG" CRUSADE. The Need For More Juvenile Members.

The Board of Management, referring in their report to the matter of propaganda, regretted that little or nothing appeared to have been done in the matter of the suggested vigorous campaign to secure juvenile members, for the figures furnished by branches relating to juveniles showed a further decrease in the membership. A member said that he thought there was a bad lack of propaganda work amongst, the juteniles. It was due to a great extent tO the exorbitant charges of the medical profes- sion for attendance on juvenile cases. The fee had gone up from 3s. Id to 6s. 6d., which meant that in large families certain members dropped out. The Grand Master proposed that branches which had no juvenile section be urged to institute one without delay, and that ever branch be strongly recommended to adopt the new table of benefits issued by the Order and bring them 'before the members, with a view to securing the insurance of their children throughout the Order.

A number of the members paid glowing tri- butes to the Grand Secretary in connection with his big part in the formulation of the new rules for the government of the district.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0206.txt | included=true | included =====
MANAGEMENT. EXPENSES. Proposal To Reduce Levy Defeated.

The report of the Board of Management, opined that a levy of 2d. per member was neces- sary for management expenses in respect of voluntary members. The estimated expenses for the present year are the same as last year, viz., .L5OO.

The Grand Treasurer moved that the fore- going clause in the report, be adopted. Despite the increase in the cost of 'everything, he said, the -Board thought they could manage with the same letiy as last year. It was proposed by the Rotherham represen- tative that the levy be reduced to lid.

AN UNWORKABLE PROPOSAL. The Grand Treasurer said he did not see how it was going to be done. The Board felt that they ought not to encroach on the nest-egg they had managed to get; and without doin'g that it was practically impossible for the Board to carry out the work as it should be carried out. There was no Friendly Society "Board that worked more economically and justly than their Board. The expenses were more likely to go up than down. £5OO would be brought in by a levy of 2d. A representative of the Manehester Equal- ising Society seconded the proposition of the Rotherham delegate. A vote was taken and only 41 hands were held up in favour of the amendment of the Rotherham representative. The clause was therefore adopted by an over- whelming majority.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0207.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DOCTORS' SUPPORT. Is It What It Should Rs?

A new epitem of medical certification of ,in- capacity for work, reported the Board of Mao.

ag-emen-t, was introduced by the Insurance commissioners in the month of January: The system possessed several advantages over the one previously in operation, but its success depended entirely on the co-operation of Approved Societies, doctors and insured persons alike. When that was thoroughly understood and given effect to the system would perhaps work a little more smoothit than itv,was re- ported to be doing at the moment.

A Member said-that the 'Friendly Societies were not getting the support from the doctors that they ought to. He thought that a deter- mined effort should be made to obtain a better hold upon the doctors than they now had.

HIGH RATE OF SICKNESS CLAIMS. Continues Despite Checking-Steps Taken:

The Board of Management reported that the high rate of sickness claims in 013 was con- tinued in the year 1914, although the methods recommended by the Commission for checking excessive claims had been adopted by the society. Those recommendations were: Weekly reports by the lsick visitors; the reference of doubtful cases to a medical referee; separate weekly. cer- tificates and irregular and surprise visits to per- sons in receipt of benefit.. In spite of this and other steps, however, the expenditure remained at about the same figure as in 1913. This matter was dealt with in committee.

Bro. John Lavin, V.G.M., of the Hanley Equalised District, who was nominated by the Hanley, Hull, Glasgow and Meager Districts.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0210.txt | included=true | included =====
WHERE TO GO NEXT YEAR.

Should Business and Pleasure Be Combined?

Blackpool was nominated by the Board of Management as the town in which the A.M.D. should be held, and their nomination gave rise to a long discussion. The Grand Secretaiy said that the Board had considered the advisability of holding a future Meeting at a seaside resort, recognising the fact that many of the delegates made the A.M.D. their holiday, thereby combining useful busi- ness with pleasure. That being so, it was felt that the delegation could just as profitably con- duct its business by the side of the sea as it could in an industrial town. Provided Black- pool was decided upon, the Mayor had very generously promised the delegates the free use of the lecture hall of the Public Library for their meetings, as well as the use of the neces- sary adjuncts, in the shape of tellers' rooms, &c., whilst each delegate would also be pro- vided With a free guide book, and eyerything possible would be done to make their stay in the town an enjoyable one. He Moved that the next conference be held in Blackpool. ' The Delegation As An Encouragement.

Bro. George Blackburn (Leeds) said that no- body enjoyed Blackpool more than himself, but what Lodge had they got in that town connected with the Union? He thought the Delegation should be held in a town where the Order already had a foothold, and should, so far as possible, be used as a means of encour- aging some local branch to further effort, and

he therefore proposed as an amendment that the neat A.1.1.D. sh.ould be held in the city of Leeds.

Bro. Caunt (Sheffield) seconded the amend- ment, remarking that the care and mainten- ance of the Order should be the first considera- tion, and not the question of where they were likely to get the best entertainments. The Warrington branch was being lifted up by that assembly; let them go and uplift the Order somewhefe else where it already had foothold. Why Not "Shoulder Their Own Guns";

Bro. Arthur Billington (Crewe) opined ihat the day for dissipating the social side of the movement was "fast becoming a fever," and deprecated the idea that the delegates made selection of a rendezvous just because they thought they might find entertainment there. Why not, he asked, let them shoulder their own guns and go to that place where they thought they could benefit the most. He supported the proposition to go to Blackpool.

Could Do As Much For The Order At Blackpool As—Timbuctoo!

The Grand Secretary said that the members must not think that in putting forward the proposal they did the Board of Management had not had the best interests of the Order at heart, for they felt that they could do just as much in the interests of those they repre- sented by meeting at Blackpool as they could by meeting at Leeds, Sheffield, or anywhere else. Far from it being thought that they were going to Blackpool for the sake of the social side, they need not go until Whit-Tues-, day, and they could commence at ten o'clock in the morning and put in a full day's work— the same as other Friendly Societies—until, say, five o'clock, sticking at it rigidly for two days and finishing on the Wednesday night. One-of the ideas in the minds of the Board when they suggested Blackpool was that it should be a business meeting, and that there should be none of the ordinary social functions.

GRAND MASTERS, PAST AND PRESENT.

GRAND MASTER LAVIN.

Miner Reaches head Of his Order.

Brother John Lavin, last year's Vice-Grand Master, who became this year's Grand Master on Wednekday, is by occupation a miner, and he lives at 29, Piccadilly, Tunstall, Stoke-on- Trent. As his portrait indicates, he is a typical Englishman, and though he modestly and generously says that Bro. Allen, of War- rington, has filled the chair so well and ener- getically that he will have difficulty in following his lead, no doubt is entertained as to his ability to maintain the best traditions' of the high office. - •

===== 0002601_19150529_art0212.txt | included=true | included =====
P. GRAND MASTER ALLEN,

Who has Played His Part Vell.

Bro. Edwin Allen, who formerly lived in Orford-road, but now lives at The Limes, Grappenhall, and is a paint and wall-paper merchant, carrying on business in Butter- market-street, was one of half a dozen friend's who founded the " Fearnhead" Lodge in 1898. He was Vice Grand Arch, and thus started -connection ^with .the Order half-way up members on the first night. The following year he rose to the position of District Grand Master, being presented, -upon the expiration the ladder. The " Fearnhead" Lodge was merged into the "Druids' Hope" Lodge in 1906.

In 1900 Bro. Allen was, out of five candidates, elected Deputy District Grand Master—before he had been a Druid two years! That is an unequalled record, for no other brother has ever attained so exalted a position after so short a membership. Within a year he assisted in opening the "Sir Gilbert Greenall", Lodge, Walton, initiating no fewer, than 100 of his term of office, with a special illuminated address—a distinction hitherto conferred on no other officer.. In 1905 he was unanimously ap- pointed district corresponding secretary. Dur- ing the followinc, four years the society saved practically .21,001, the greatest progress made in any similar period. Honoured many times with the appointment of delegate to the A.M.D.—at Crewe in 1905, Glasgow in 1908, and Newcastle-on-Tyne in 1909 —Bro. Allen was also elected President of the Widows' and Orphans' Fund two years in suc- cession, and has frequently acted as auditor. He is a member of the Royal Arch Chapter a higher branch of Order—and was appointed delegate to the annual meeting (Manchester) in, 1906.

A firm believer in the social side of Friendly Society work, it is natural that, under Bro. Allen's supervision, the " Fearnhead" Lodge should have been the first to hold its annual bowling main; and that a most successful social should have been held in 1909, when the grand officers visited Warrington for the purpose of initiating Sir Gilbert Greenall, Mr. Robert Pierpoint, and Councillor Fulton-Smith as hon. members. In fact, Bro. Allen has been • the means of making more hon. members than any- one in the district, and in 1900 presented the four principal officers of the district with chains.

These are only some of the many services Bro. Allen has rendered the Order, which just twelve months ago ionferred its highest honour upon him.

Few members have worked harder in the interests of the Order than Bro. Allen, and at the end of the term of his office as Grand Master nothing but praise has been forthcom- ing for the manner In which he has maintained ,the best traditions of the post.

. . . . ~ • . , •

===== 0002601_19150529_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
"From His Youth Up."

He has been •a Druid, and an active one, since his young days. It is just 29 years ago since he joined the " Tunstall" Lodge of the Hanley Equalised District of the Order • of .Druids, as :a juvenile; at the age of 18 he was elected on the Lodge Committee, a position to which he has been. elected twice annually ever since. Shortly afterwards he passed through the various Lodge offices, and in June, 1897, Was elected Lodge Delegate to the District Board, which position he held until elected Deputy D.G.M. in 1908, and after serving two years ag D.G.31.-1909 and 1910—he again acted as dele- gate for the Lodge.- He was elected District Trustee in 1912. At the Welshpool A.M.D. in 1911 he was appointed on the Executive Board of the Order, and at the Sheffield A.31.D., 1914, was elected Vice-Grand Master. Strenuous and Productive Record.

lie.lias spent considerable time in furthering the interests of Friendly Societies in general,. and his own Order in particular, and has seen his district grow numerically from 1,700 to nearly 4,000 voluntary members. At the in- ception of the National Insurance Act, along with other officials of the district, he was very busily engaged attending meetings and organ- ising the forces of the Order that they might be able to deal with the business accruing through the Act, efforts that were not made entirely in vain, as the district now has a State membership of over 6,700. He has been a member of the Stoke-on-Trent Insurance Committee, dealing with over 90,000 insured persons. All his "leisure" time is fairly well occupied, as, in addition to what has already been mentioned, he is a member of the • llis- trict . Management Committee, which is responsible for the administration of the National Insurance Act for local members. Even greater demands will be made On ..his time during the next year, but he faces these formidable duties with a -determination to Succeed.

Bro. Lavin only occasionally finds time to attend meetings of his Lodge in connection with the North Staffordshire Miners' Federa- tion.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0216.txt | included=true | included =====
A SYMPATHETIC NOTE.

Delegation Feels For Grand Master

===== 0002601_19150529_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
Allen In Hour Of Trial.

WISHES THAT ALL MAY BE WELL.

A human note that found response in the hearts of all present was struck at the outset of Thursday's proceedings, when The Grand Treasurer, rising in his place, said he 'would like, before the business was entered upon, to say a few words with regard to an event which had occurred. Since they left. that hall yesterday, and whilst they were dining together the previous night and making flattering reference to the gallant Navy, that unit of the splendid force on which Graid Master Allen's son was serving—H.3l.S. Triumph—fell a victim to the enemy, and now rested at the bottom of the Dardanelles. They all realised what a terrible effect the news was bound to have upon the Grand Master, and their hearts went out to him and his wife in the anxiety they were now experi- encing whilst they were trying to ascertain whether their son was among the many who had been saved, or whether he was included among the lost. They all hoped that the best possible news would be received in reply to the Inquiry which had .been addressed to • the proper quarter. (hear, hear.) A Red-Letter Day Marred.

The delegates would quite understand that the terrible blow which had befallen the Grand Master on the very day which he .himself described as the red-letter day Of his life, must preclude him from being with them that morn- ing. He was at home with his wife, who had Collapsed under the terrible strain, but he had sent a message to the. delegation expressing the hope that the proceedings wonld be carried on in his absence. The first duty of the .delegates that. morning was to place on record their very earnest and den) .sympathy with the Grand Master and his family in their hour of anxiety and trial; indeed, he moved that the delegation most earnestly and, respectfully expressed and placed on record its sincere sympathy with Bro. gdwin Allen in his acute anxiety as to the fate of his son,, and that the delegation also desired to express the hope that the news which came from the Admiralty would be of the brightest and most encouraging .description. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150529_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANY'S PUNISHMENT SURE TO COME.

Bro. Albert Wilde seconded, remaiking that they believed that the Almighty would_ have vengeance on the men who had instigated the war and who had been guilty of such dastardly things. Their punishment was certain to come. Bro. J. It. Copping (Darlington) said he was at their Grand .Master's house that morning when the news that the Triumph had gone down was .broken to ,the anxious household .through the medium of the newspaper. - They earnestly hoped that Bro. Allen's son might be among the saved. The resolution was carried in silence, and 13ro. /Copping undertook to convey it to the Grand Master.

IDENTIFICATION OF INSURANCE CARDS.

A Concession to Approved Societies.

The Grand Master-elect (Bro. John Lavin) then took the chair pro tem., and the delegation proceeded to the further consideration of the report of the Board of Management, it being first announced that the voting strength repre- sented by the delegates that morning was 70,058, so that 38,030 votes were necessary before a clear majority could be obtained. The Board of Management reported having dealt with the matter of the identificaticin of State insurance cards by approved societies, and the Grand . Secretary explained that as the result of representation made to the Insur- ance Commissioners, consequent upon so many cards haying gone astray, the Commissioners had consented to the approved societies putting a confidential mark upon the insutance cards before they were issued. As a result. the Board of Management had decided to adopt a notifi- cation number, which would be placed on the front of the card, and would at a glance show the approved society to which the member be- longed, and the particular branch of the society. It was decided to approve the action of the Board of Management.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT INSURANCE ACT COSTS THE ORDER.

It was reported by the Board of Management that the amount estimated to be required for head office purposes for National Health Insur- ance administration expenses .for' the year ending December 31st next is £1,400. The delegates were asked to confer authority on the Board to deduct an amount equal to 2d. per member per half-year for this purpose from sums issued to the society by the several In- surance Commissioners.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
TRANSFERENCE OF INSURED PERSONS.

A Matter of Arrangement Between the Approved Societies,

A report by the Board of .Management as to transfers under Section 31 of the National Insurance Act called forth a statement from the Grand Treasurer with regard to disputed,,, transfers, who said that if their Order had been put to a great deal of trouble the trouble ex- perienced ,hy the bigger Orders must be tre- mendous. Disputed trantAfers had so far been settled by the Commissioners, who had gener- ally decided in favour of the man applying for transfer, whether he had any valid reasons for transference or not. The attitude which the Friendly Societies had taken up was that the Act worked, in this respect at any rate, on a wrong basis. In other words, the burden of proof was placed on the Order to show that it had not :unreasonably withheld consent ,to transfer.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
Commissioners and Societies at Variance.

On the other hand, the Friendly Societies considered that before any member was entitled to transfer he should show that he had good reasons for wishing to transfer to another society; not that the societies should be ptit tor the trouble—the almost impossible task—of proving a direct negative. The attitude taken by the Commissioners had been

somewhat of a wholesale character, if one might so phrase it, and, generally speaking, they had decided all matters in a manner which, he contended, was never contemplated when the Act was passed. The Druids con- tended that matters of this sort in dispute should be settled by arbitrators of the Orders concerned, but the Commissioners did not take that view, but after numerous negotiations a working arrangement had been come to, and he proposed that the Board of Management be empowered to enter into agreement with any Approved Society that transfers would neither be given nor accepted except under special cir- cumstances, and then only by consent of both

parties. The proposition was carried

===== 0002601_19150529_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
ALLOWANCE FOR MILITARY AND NAVAL

MEMBERS.

It was reported to the delegation that instead of the 8d: per annum allowed by the Insurance Commissioners for Army and Navy members of approved societies, the figure in future would be is. 81d.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
TRUSTEE AND AUDITOR ELECTED.

During this session the results of the election by ballot of a trustee and an auditor were de- clared.

For the .vacant trusteeship there were origin- ally six candidates, but one of them withdrew. The following five went to the ballot:— Bro. Wm: Bromfield, P.D.G.M., Hanley Equalised District. Bro. John B. Copping, P.G.M., Darlington Equalised District. Bro. John Davidson, P.D.G.A.1., Wigan Equal- ised District.

Bro. Richard 11. Mason, P.G.M., Oswestry and Mid-Wales Equalised District. Bro. Albert Wilde, P.G.M., Sheffield Equal- ised District.

On the first round the votes of the delegates were cast as follows: Bro. Bromfield, 6,637; Bro. Copping, 31,404; Bro. Davidson, 1,753; Bro. Mason, 2,664; Bro. Wilde, 32,190. The, names of the two highest were again sub- mitted to. the meeting, when the ballot resulted in the election of Bro. Copping by 40,235 votes, as against 35,823 cast for Bro. Wilde. There were two candidates for the vacant auditorship, and the voting resulted as follows: Bro. James Jackson, P.D.G.M. (Wigan Equal. ised District), 19,185; Bro. T. Marshall, D.D.G.M. (Sheffield Equalised District), 56,687, the last-namedsbeing therefore declared elected.

AMENDING THE RULES. The rest of the session was devoted to minor amendments to the constitutional rules.

ORCHIDS AND DRIVE.

Delegates See Alderman Bolton's

Wonderful Flowers.

More delightful conditions than those pre- vailing on Tuesday could riot possibly have been provided for the drive into Cheshire, nor could the countryside have presented a more charming aspect for the delectation of the visitors. The company, something over two hundred strong and including Grand Master Allen and Mrs. Allen and the Misses Allen, drove in conveyances supplied by Messrs. Maginn through Stockton Heath, Grappenhall, Thelwall and Latchford, calling at the cele- brated orchid houses of Alderman Bolton at Wilderspool at the beginning of the journey. The party were met by Alderman and Mrs. Bolton; and the manager, Mr. Cain, and his staff conducted them through the houses, the resources of whiqh were proved by the fact that each of the party was supplied with a bloom—in some cases with several blooms— without any perceptible effect being made upon the general appearance of the plants. The Alderman also entertained ' the party to re- freshments. Before leaving the grounds

===== 0002601_19150529_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
ORCHIDS,FOR THE QUEEN.

District Grand Master Woods moved a vote of thanks to Alderman Bolton for his great kindness in allowing the delegation to visit his orchid houses and for his hospitality. ,it had been a great pleasure to view the choice selec- tion of orchids, which were second to' tone in the kingdom. P.D.G.M. George Shaw (Warrington), second- ing; ventured .to say that was the first time many of them had seen orchid houses, and he knew he was expressing the opinion of all when he said they had been delighted with all they had witnessed. *Alderman Bolton had that day sent a .box, of orchids as a birthday gift to Her Majesty the Queen, who had inti- mated that she would be-7graciously pleased to accept the flowers. The receipt of such a letter, said Bro. Shaw, -was an annual occurrence.

The vote having been carried by loud cheers,

Alderman Bolton expressed his pleasure as the pleasure of his • visitors. lie always noticed to discover what most surprised his visitors. If one thing surprised them more than another it was that the houses were so hot. (Loud laughter.)

===== 0002601_19150529_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
ALDERMAN BOLTON'S ORCHIDS. 100,000 Plants In Perfect Condition.

Alderman Bolton's active interest in orchids dates from 1869, when be received from his uncle, Mr. Samuel Barlow, of Stakehill, Mid- dleton, several young plants from the tops of Dendrobium nobile and a few pieces of Cypri- pedium insigne. In 1872 he sold his green- houses and plants and went to America; but two years later he returned. Ms continued interest in flowers led to his return to the culture of orchids in 1883. Some early failures had no deterrent effects; in fact, about 1893 he sent Mr. Kromer to Brazil and Mr. Jensen to Colombia for various species only to be found there. Ever since the collection has steadily increased; it has been carried on tpractically as that of an amateur, although various sales have been held for the disposal of surplus stock, which accumulates owing to good culti- vation.

There are no fewer than 32 greenhouses IC various sizes, the principal ones being about 60 feet long, arranged on either side of long corridors. Alderman Bolton said he had noticed the surprise shown at the heat of the houses; ,we fancy more surprise was expressed at the method of heating rather than the temperature produced, for it is not often that several large boilers similar .to those used in factories are to be seen engaged in the work of heating greenhouses. Whichever house is entered there is the same multitude of plants to be • seen, species and hybrids of every description. Cypripe- dium Boltonii (F,C.C. Royal Horticultural Society, 1909, and P.C.C. and silver medal Manchester Orchid Society, 1909) is one of the finest and most beautiful hybrids yet pro- duced. The whole culture treatment of Alderman Bolton's orchids is managed by Mr. Cain, who has been at Wilderspool for 23 years, and who, assisted by an able staff, keeps this immense stock, • roughly estimated at 100,000 plants, in. perfect order.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
TEA AND CONCERT.

Returning to Warrington, the party gathered for tea in the assembly room of the Lion. Hotel, which, by the way, had been consti- tuted the Order's headquarters for the week, though their place of meeting was, as stated above, the Patten Hall. Ample justice having been done to an excellent spread, an enjoyable smoking concert took place, under the chair- manship: of the District• Grand Master. A thoroughly, enjoyable programme of glees, quartets, solos, monologues, and humorous songs was provided by that excellent combina- tion of local artistes, known as the Brooklyn Quartet, and consisting of Messrs. T. .Crowe (alto)-, J. Sephton (tenor), W. H. Dutton (bari- tone), and W. J. Hopkins (bass), iiith Mr. A. Trow as accompanist.
