COMPLIMEPITAWI BAiIQUET.

Presentation to Grand Master and Mrs. Allen.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0236.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. CROSFIELD AND THE MAD KAISER.

A complimentary banquet given to the dele- gates by the Warrington Equalised District took place at the Lion Hotel, on Wednesday evening, "in honour of the Annual Movable Delegation being held in Warrington." Mr. A. H. Crosfield presided, and was supported by the Grand'Officers of the Order and other local gentlemen, including the Mayor, Aldermen Tinnion, Bolton, and Bennett; Councillors Chrimes, Locker, and Osborne; and Mr. T. P. Bradshaw. A first-class dinner 'was placed before the guests by Mr. Lee, and a capital pro- gramme of vocal music was submitted by the Brooklyn Quartet, Messrs. Crowe, Sephton, Dutton and Hopkins, with Mr. A. E. Trims" as accompanist. A tong toast list had to be some- what curtailed owing to the present early clos- ing restrictions. _ _ . .

Mr. Mitchell, the Mayor's attendant, acted as toastmaster.

THE DUTIES OF ROYALTY.

The Chairman, in proposing the toast of "The King," said they would drink it that night with intensified feeling, and, if possible, with a deeper loyalty than ever. (Applause.) All appreci- ated the spirit of self-denial in which the King performed his duties to the State and to the Empire and the strenuous zeal, tact, and true patriotism with which His Majesty threw him- self into the work and responsibility which fell upon him in this time of great national crisis. (Applause.) The toast having been drunk and followed by the singing of the Nationhl Anthem, The Chairman proposed "Queen Mary, the Prince of Wales and the Rest of the Royal Family." They. could well understand, he said_, what a 'helpmate the Queen, with her keen sympathy, natural sagacity, literary gifts, and exceptional capacity, was ~to the King at this crucial time. (Hear, hear.) With. regard to the Prince, all had been pleased to read the accounts which had come from the Front. (Hear, hear.) We knew how, without ostenta- tion, but with modesty and pluck—(hear, hear) —and energy, the Prince was throwing himself into the duties falling to his lot during the enacting of history such as had never been enacted in this world before. We could under- stand the effect it would have on the Canadians and others from the outlying parts of the Em- pire when they found the Prince of Wales play- ing his part like a man. (Applause.) The toast was enthusiastically pledged.

be reaped of their thrift and forethought, a harvest which would be participated in not only by themselves, but by others, who would benefit along with them. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150529_art0237.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FINEST FEAT OF ARMS.

Bro.. Wm. Bibby proposed 'The Army and Navy," the services, he said, were passmg through such a time as, twelve 111011t118 ago, we never tbought.tnee would be called upon to do. (Hear, hear.) lie thougnt we should find the spirit of their grandfathers, that fought and won at Waterloo, would again fight and win Au .Germany. (Applause.) Tile recent feat of arms by the Australians in the Dardanelles had been pronounced by competent judges to be the finest teat of arms ever performed in the history of the world: The men persisted in landing in spite of the fact that boatload after boatload had been annihilated by hidden and entrenched forces on shore. What would be thought of these men in time to come? (Applause.) The 'Navy had not been -able to do a great deal because the German Navy would not come out, but what .was possible had been done, and the seas were clear of enemy vessels. (Applause.) He believed the waiting of the men in tue Navy had been more trying to them than 'duty had been to the men who had been occupied in the trenches in France and Belgium. (hear, hear.) He would like to add to the toast " The Forces of our Allies." (Cheers.) P.G.M. A. Billington, speaking as an old Volunteer who had been 25 times efficient, said the nation now knew, as it never/ knew before, how much it was indebted to their great forces of a national defensive character that had ex- isted, but to which, in the past, too little im- portance had been attached. (Hear,. hear.) Whether it was necessary to compel men to stand their corner or whether we ought to con- tinue to rely upon voluntary disposition, re- mained to be settled by those who,were wiser than they. On behalf of the forces who were seeking the national tellbeing, lie thanked the company for having 60 heartily honoured this toast. (Applause.)

WARRINGTON INDUSTRIAL ACTIVITIES.

Bro. R. Verney Clayton, G.T., proposed" The Town and Trade of Warrington," and said he had been to many delegations, but the Order had never received a warmer welcome than from the ,Mayor and Corporation and town of Warrington. (Applause.) On seeking advice from Alderman Bennett as to this toast he was told that .there was always one thing to say-. that Warrington was "a town of many indus- tries." (Hear, hear, and laughter.) Re found in a pamphlet written by the Town Clerk that Warrington was a town of many industries— (laughter)—but there were added the further attractions of low assessments and rents, low rates, low charges for water—nothing said about the whisky—(laughter)—low charges for gas, low charges for electric light and power, and land "at reasonable prices." (Laughter) Of the many ixdustries he would mention only the first item—beer! (Laughter and applause.) He was glad they agreed with him. (Laughter.) The history of a town was the history of its people, and the history of this town was_ re- flected in the men they had met that week who had run the Corporation and the businesses of the place so successfully. (Hear, hear.) War- rington Bridge was a beautiful and enduring; structure. They all hoped the place would con- tinue to be the town of many industries, and to achieve a great amount of municipal success. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150529_art0238.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COMING LEAN YEARS.

Alderman Tinnion, replying, assured the visitors that the people of the town had been delighted to have the honour and pleasure of their presence this year. He had heard a .good many men say they were "Warrington bred and born." Well, that was not their fault. (Laughter.) Alderman Bennett once told him he was Warrington born and bred, and his reply was: "Well, never mind; live it down !" (Laughter.) He (the speaker) was one of Warrington's adopted sons and he was sincerely proud of the town—which had a soap- works carried on in a spirit of industrial enterprise that might be copied with advantage in many parts of the country. They had also leather, iron and wire known all over the world, so great was its reputation. (Applause.) It was the splendid feeling that existed be- tween the masters and the men locally that contributed largely to their success. They had their fine works' recreation grounds, and the higher branches of recreation were culti- vated at such works as Messrs. Crosfield's, where music and kindred • higher sources of enjoyment were cultivated. (Hear, hear.) Although the town, owing •to its many trades, seldom experienced vicissitudes; he was afraid the war would bring about some very lean years. When , they remembered the effect of the South African War, which cost only £250,000,000, they might ask what was going to, be the ex- tent of the damage caused by the wasting of thousands of millions by the present war? The time ,was coming when the Friendly Societies would be wanted, and when the harvest would

===== 0002601_19150529_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====
BROTHER CROSFIELD ON STRIKING

CONTRASTS.

Bro. A. H. Crosfield, who was received with applause, proposed the toast " The Order of Druids Friendly Society." That, he said, had been a red-letter week for the Druids of War- rington, And one of the .most memorable weeks that the members of Friendly Societies in War- rington had ever known. He was very glad when he heard they intended to proceed with the delegation, because he believed they would be playing the enemy's game if they allowed the unspeakable Goth to bring our national life to a standstill. (Hear, hear.) Man—if he might venture, without disrespect, to paraphrase the Scriptures—could not live by war alone; and assuredly it would not hasten the conclusion of this infamous war if we simply went about in sackcloth and ashes crying " Woe ! woe !" in the street, with lugubrious faces. (Hear, hear.) That was not the way we should most speedily bring these criminals—he used the word ad- visedly, for they were nothing else—(hear, hear) —to book. He felt it was .a good thing for them to come together as members of one of the greatest Friendly Societies the world has ever known in order to remind men of what was such a striking antithesis of the very spirit that ani- mated the vile and pernicious gospel of Prussian militarism, based upon cruelty and blood and iron. (Hear, hear.) tr. M was a good many years since that the late r. Gladstone—(hear, hear)—referred to the noble work done by the Friendly Societies, which he splendidly epitomised in the words, "You seek your own good through the good of others. AAp- plause.) He did not think they could imagine a more striking contrast than the contrast between the whole spirit and purpose of our Friendly Societies and this gospel of re- lentless and ruthless brute force now running amok through all the laws and traditions of civilisation. In Belgium we saw this hideous gospel providing us with the picture to which Lord Macaulay referred, threequarters of a century or more ago, when he was engaged upon his brilliant essays, and spoke of "that most frightful of all spectacles, the strength of civilisation without its mercy." (Applause.) That was the record that these precious Prus- sians. with their vaunted Kultur, had written for themselves to their indelible disgrace as long as history continued to be written or read in the civilised world. The other day his old friend, Eugene Wason—when he knew intim- ately _a few years ago when he (the speaker) represented Warrington in the House of Cora- inons, and whose „friendship lie still cherished —came back from Germany, where he had met an American who was studying the condition, of things in Europe and the position of the Kaiser. "So far as I can make out," said the American, "the Kaiser has only one friend left, and that is the Almighty "—and he added "we have only got the .Kaiser's word for that." (Loud laughter.) He repeated that they could not imagine a. greater contrast than the magnificent spirit of co-opera- tion which formd the foundation and very life blood of the Friendly Societies of Great Britain, and the self-centred, selfish, self-seeking and. probably at bottom cowardly, nature of these Prussian miscreants. , Here in this Druids Society as in the other great Friendly Societies, they had the finest type of co-operation. All had been struck by the allusions made by Alderman Tinnion and others to the fine and merciful work which, in a terribly hard world, it was the duty of the Friehdly Societies to do for those who had fallen upon unlucky times.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
Rational Co-operation.

The progress of the civilised world was based upon intelligent and rational co-operation, using the word not in its narrow commercial sense, but in its widest signification. It lay at the root of the whole of our national and Imperial life. Nobody could follow the devel- opment of modern commerce in Britain or the world without realising how it became more and more interlaced, how the network grew, and how the act of a criminal lunatic—for the Kaiser had really shown himself to be noth- ing less—could affect, because of this interlac- ing principle, the whole civilised world. It might be thought that though this war was a terrible thing for Europe, distant pacts of the world would have no concern in it; but that was not so.

As a single illustration he might mention that a correspondent of his; a very able busi- ness man in Rio de Janeiro, wrote him shortly after the war was declared, describing the awful commercial consequences in Brazil, the result of this madman's work of which the Kaiser, misled by his Prussian advisers, had been guilty. Adequate reparation could never be made by Germany or anybody else for the unspeakable ruin, agony and despair which had spread over Europe, for the loss of life that we had experienced and had yet to face, or for the • destruction upon so huge a scale of the fruits of men's industry and the accumulated savings of the nations. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150529_art0241.txt | included=true | included =====
The Silver Lining.

There was no fear of indulgence in careless optimism. Britain knew too well bow formid- able was our task. The times were dark and the cloud over Central Europe was black, hut for Britishers there was still a silver lining. We had an Empire, great in its achievements, great in what it defended, and great in the possibilities for humanity for which it stood; but never in the jtistory of the Empire had we felt ' it so cemented—by the bonds of blood, if it came to that—as at the present time. • (Applause.) The deeds of the Canadians in Belgium, and of the Australians in the Gallipoli, Peninsular, would be quoted thousands of years hence by their descendants. (Applause.) Let us then remember, concluded Mr. Crosfield, in these terrible times what this will mean to the Empire throughout the future, and let us, therefore, with all the more' confidence and determination go forward with the work in hand, because, friends, it concerns everything that we value, and, from our point of view as Druids and members of other Friendly Societies in the country, let us never forget that if we fail, as it is unthinkable that we should fail, it would be a poor look-out for Friendly Society work, as indeed for. any sort of tolerable national existence, in the future. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150529_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
STRONG MUST HELP THE WEAK.

Bro. J. W. Shaw, who replied, said they had been delighted and inspired by, the speech to which they had just listened. It would en- courage them to proceed and to keep their s9ciety in the honourable and successful posi- tion it had held in the country for so many years. They had had critical times and would clearly have more critical times. They had 5,000 members serving His Majesty and keeping the flag flying—(hear, hear)—and they were going to see that none of the benefits these men joined the society to gain were lost. (Applause.) But they must take care of the funds placed in their hands, and some re- organisation might take place, in which the stronger branches would Come to the help of the weaker ones. 13RO. ROBERT TIIORNLEY,

266, Knutsford-road, engaged at the ~Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co.'s offices, joined the Court Hope" Lodge (431) of the ..Ancient Order jf Foresters at an early age. He passed. through the various chairs, and in 1900. was appointed secretary of his lodge. Shortly after- wards he was asked to undertake district work. In 1903 he was appointed Sub-District Chief Ranger, and in 1901 District Chief Ranger, an office which he held for 12 months. In 1910 he succeeded Bro. L. Davies as District Secretary, and is still in office. He relinquished the secre- taryship of his lodge and devoted the whole of his attention to district work, and his services were eventually recognised by the Presentation to him of a, handsome secretaire. Bro. Thorn- ley has represented the district at High Court • meetings at Southampton and Leicester. It is interesting to note that the Mayor' is District Treasurer of. the Foresters, and that' his Worship and Bro. Thornley have followed each other. in the various offices.

The. Grand Master's wife, in her garden home.

PRESENTATIONS

To The Grand Master and Mrs. Allen.

The Mayor, on behalf of the Kindred Friendly Societies in Warrington, then pre- sented to Grand Master Allen a silver flower stand and two silver fruit dishes, with the in- scription:

Presented to Brother Edwin Allen, Grand Master of the Order of Druids. From the Friendly Societies of Warrington. A.M.D., Whit-Week, 1915.

At the tirao of the lust Conference, said His Worship, he was in Germany with 150 other Alayors from this country at the Kaiser's re- quest, endeavouring to, show the people of Germany how friendly we in this country were towards them. He ,wiered to the Delegation to assure them of the heartiest welcome if the Delegation decided tO honour their Grand Master's town with this visit, little thinking we should be in the midst of this terrible war. He and Bro. Allen had many things in com- mon. Besides their interest in Friendly Society work, Bro. Allen had a son in the Dardanelles, serving with the Navy—(ap- plause)—and they all wished him a safe return home. He (the speaker) had a son in Belgium. (Applause.) All had relations one way or another doing their duty for the great Empire they loved so well. (Hear, hear.) It gave him great pleasure to see Bro. Allen's official year in the high and distinguished office he had held, marked by this note of appreciation. Ailey hoped Bro. Allen would live long -to enjoy the gift and that his family would look upon it as an acknowledgment of the good work the recipient 'had • done for Friendly Societies m Warrington and in the country. (Cheers.) Bro. G. T. Rushton, on behalf of the War- Anton Equalised District,, handed to •the Grand Master a gold watch bracelet for Mrs. :Olen. Bro. Allen had been an able man at he head of affairs and had steered them through times of stress and trial as nobody also in their district could have done. He had brought the funds to the state they were in, and for that alone they felt deeply in- debted to him. (Applause.) Mrs. Allen must have sacrificed much while her husband had been so closely occupied upon their work, and they desired to show their appreciation of that in the heartiest possible manner.' (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150529_art0248.txt | included=true | included =====
GRAND MASTER'S THANKS.

Grand Master Allen, who was received with cheers, said their eulogy and generosity made him docile as a child. This had been a red- letter week in his life—and he thanked the Mayor for his hospitality and Alderman Bolton for his large-hearted generosity. (Applause.) It was true that his son was a midshipman on the Triumph in the Dardanelles—(applause)-- and his appointment and position gave them cause for grave anxiety. -(Hear, hear.) On the other hand, he offered his sympathy to Bro. Peacock and his dear wife, the Mayoress. He held that if one wanted real sympathy and generosity one got it from the Friendly Socie- ties, composed, • as they were, of working men, who gave it in the fullest sense of the word, (Hear, hear.) He sincerely thanked them for this kindness they had shown to himself, his wife and his family. (Applause.)

The present was supplied by Air. Finla,yson

CONTINUED ENTHUSIASM.

Bro. George Shaw, proposing "Kindred Societies, Hon. Members and Visitors," repudi- ated the suggestions made in some quarters that the voluntary side of the Friendly Society movement was doomed. The splendid enthu- siasm shown in Warrington this week, awl the type of men who were supporting them—Bros. Bradshaw; Chrimes, Tinnion, and the Mayor himself—proved it to be a libel to say—that the movement was dying out. If they worked up to their ideal the Friendly Sotieties. were more assured of their position than ever before. (Ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19150529_art0250.txt | included=true | included =====
ALDERMAN BOLTON'S ADVICE.

A Islerman Bolton, in the 'course of an exceed- ingly humorous reply, said the ,only advice he could give them was: "Look after your funds, pull together, and do all you can for one another."

Bro. T. P. Bradshaw replied on behalf of the kindred societies, and complimented the Druids upon the manner in which they had conducted the business of the delegation. They had two of the best permanent officials in the Friendly Society movement. The Friendly Societies in Warrington were thankful to the Chairman for the way he had for many years encouraged the voluntary side of the Friendly Society move- ment, and he would like to take that opportu- nity of thanking Mr. Crosfield for his kind ser- vices in that respect. He hoped the Chairman would often come amongst them and continue his interest in the Friendly Society movement. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150529_art0251.txt | included=true | included =====
AN IDEAL CHAIRMAN.

P.G.M. A. Wilde proposed the health of Mr. Crosfield, who, he said, was an ideal chairman;

CONFIDENCE IN KITCHENER.

Upon the initiative of Mr. Crosfield, that gentleman was instructed, by resolution, car- ried by acclamation, to convey the delegation's heartfelt and undeviating loyalty towards and confidence in Lord Kitchener.

Shaw's Friendly Society caretr was his appointment under the Insurance Act as the first secretary for the Warrington Equalised District Order of Druids, which entailed a great deal of care, thought and study. VALUABLE AUXILIARIES.

When it wtrs made known that the Druids' Delegation was to visit Warrington the other Friendly Societies in town, with true brotherly instinct, set about the task of devising a scheme whereby they could show their interest and do their best to aid the general cause. A Friendly Society Committee was constituted to arrange for welcoming the Druids to War- rington, and for making a suitable presentation to the Warrington Grand Master of the Order. Of course, the Mayor (Alderman Peacock), in some respects the leading Friendly Society man in town, 'came on to the committee automatic- ally, and he placed himself and the Town Hall at the disposal of his Colleagues.' The officers of the committee were: Bro. T. P. Bradshaw, 1.0.0., M.U., chairman; Bro. John Chrimes, A.O. Foresters, vice-chairman; and Bro. Thornley, A.O. Foresters, secretary: These three were primarily responsible for the arrangements. .

How The Kindred Societies Showed Their Interest.

bury being the only other one to have held the office locally for a second time. Bro. Chrimes has been a Forester for 31 years. He was initiated a member of "Court War- rington," No. 430, on November 22nd, 1884. Gradually and deservedly all the honours asso- ciated with Forestry fell to his lot, and many are the interesting mementoes which' adorn his home for valuable labours rendered to Forestry and to the Coopers' Society, of which he is also a member. Bro. 'Chrimes was appointed secre- tary to his Court in 1896, and the happy finan- cial state which it enjoys is a standing tribute to his care and organisation. As the delegate to the various High Courts, Bro. Chrimes has been honoured repeatedly, and on many occasions he has fearlessly pressed himself on questions of administration at conferences held at Norwich; Manchester, Leicester and Burton-on-Trent. He will attend the High Court to be held this year at Oxford in August. Bro. Chrimes is a trustee of the Widow and Orphans Fund, and treasurer of the District Burial Society. Bro. Chrimes is a member of an old Warring- ton family, and for many yeas his parents were well known in the Heathside district. For 27 year's Bro. ,Chrimes has been secretary to the Warrington Coopers' Trade Society, and- has attended all the conferences.

BRO. T. P. BRADSHAW.

Bro. Thomas Porter Bradshaw, who is a shining light in Oddfellowship, was, owing to his close acquaintance with the National _lnsurance Act, co-opted a member of the Dis- trict Management Committee and had the duty of visiting the different Lodges in the district, to explain the scheme of working. He was one of two representatives selected by the Warrington District to attend the special Con- ference held in London by the Manchester Unity. Bro. Bradshaw joined the "Prince of Wales" .Lodge of the L 0.0., 3.1. U., in 1900. He fostered the fraternal and philanthropic principles of Oddfellowship, passed the various offices in his Lodge, and thus acquired a great knowledge of the inner workings of the Order to fit him for higher offices. Honours came to him iii rapid succession, and in 1903' he filled the office of vice-president of the Widows and Orphans Fund, and the following year he was chosen as president. In 1905 he was elected Deputy- Grand Master of the Warrington District, and, in the following year, be was installed •Grand Master, Many presentation g have been made to him, and these include a silver tea and coffee service from the Lodge for services on the social side in connection with State Insur- ance, a Past Grand Master's , gold jewel, a P.P.G.M.'s framed certificate, and a gold merit jewel—the first local member of the Manches- ter Unity to receive the last-named honour. He has acted as representative of his Lodge at many important meetings, including the A.M.C.

Bro. Bradshaw is a manager of the Warring- ton Savings Bank and of Hamilton-street School. He is in the Solicitors' office of Messrs. Henry Greenall and Co., and assists the head of the firm in his duties as Magistrates' Clerk to the Warrington and Daresbnry Petty Ses- sional Divisions, and clerk to the Commissioners of -income. tax

===== 0002601_19150529_art0255.txt | included=true | included =====
A HAPPY HIVE.

The Druids At Messrs. Crosfield's :

Your Soap Vl'hile You AVait.

A happy' hive of industry: that was the impression "The Examiner" man formed of Messrs. Crosfield's famous soap works, when he visited„ them. on Thursday afternoon, in company with our Druidical friends, thanks to the courtesy of the firm. The swami of girl workers might have been playing tennis or tiddly-winks, so blithesomely did they push the business on.

The visitors were shown over the large pre- mises in batches of twenty-five. There were the'7 chemical laboratories, where studious- looking people of both sexes crouched apxiously over test tubes and beakers and reminded one vividly of schooldays and indescribable effluviums. never to be erased from the olfactory memory. Then there were the printing and packing departments, and down below there were the boiling rooms, where huge round basins, with weird-shaped .crusty leavings inside, gave out a threatening heat. In this .department one found men instead of girls, but this constituted an exception, for the great majority of the workers in this in- dustrial hive are girls. Fresh weird sights were to be seen in other departments, and al- together the visit was very enjoyable and full of interest. One thing that struck one was the excellence, of the 'conditions under which the girls worked.

As each visitor passed out of the fire brigade building, which was the last part of the pre- mises to be inspected, lie or she was presented with a handsome box of three tablets of Cros- field's best soap—a gift one might have ob- jected to from some sources, but not in this particular ease.

WOMEN AWHEEL.

• The 'number of women riders is on the in- crease. Judging by the experience f various makers the demand this season for ladies' cycles is considerably in advance of what has been the case during the past few seasons. Cycling is a pleasureable and rapid means of travelling and perfectly adapted to the use of women. It will be remembered that in the early days of the. cycling boom; the number of lady riders will show whether the pastime is being taken uP again .by the softer .sex. Feminine Britons do not appear to appreciate the beauty (!) of akirtless contraptions, and. really there is no necessity for them to wear them. The drop frame of to-day is made so strong and rigid that ,there is nothing to be said against this pattern of machine. Women riders should, however, select a cycle built by a reliable firm. The dearer machine is but slightly above the price of a cheap one, and the extra money is well spent, in reliability.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0260.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOLISH FOOTPATH LAWS.

The majority of. people nowadays are users of both the footpath and the. road. When chang- ing froni one to ,the other the absurdity of the difference between • the regulations for pedes- trians and cyclists or drivers of vehicles must strike them. The absurdity would be nothing— we are more or less accustomed to this in many , existing regulations—if „it were not for the danger'. 'A foot passenger, by keeping to the right, walks in ,the same direction as the traffic. To step' into the road even for a moment, with- out looking round is dangerous. Some heavy Vehicle, power-driven or horse-drawn, may. be just behind one. If the law regulating footpath users were altered, it would be for the better. To • keep to the left would Mean that when walking on the kerb the oncoming traffic would be faced. It would be a simple matter to change the law as it now stands and bring it into uni- formity with that applicable to road traffic. Which will be the first authority to act.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0262.txt | included=true | included =====
Prisoner's Excuse In A Maintenance Case.

Before the Borough Bench on Monday morn- ing, Arthur John Brighton, of no settled abode, was charged with miming away from his wife and child, and leaving them chargeable to the common fund of the Union.

Mr. Basil Shearer, collector to the Warring- ton Guardians, said that oil:January 16th last the relieving officer called at the house in which prisoner had been living and there found the wife in a totally destitute condition, the house being devoid of furniture or food.. The woman was in a certain condition and a child was born on March 2nd. Since then She had found a situation.

Mr. F. IL Aukland, relieving officer, gave evi- dence as to the woman's destitution, and said that the reason why there Was no furniture in the house was that such goods as the couple had had were procured on the hire purchase system and had been taken back by the owners because the instalments were not paid. The man was a waggon builder and could earn well over 1:2 a Week if he liked to stick to his work. Prisoner said that the womait was not his wife; be was only living with her. - Mr. Shearer said that that made no difference to the Guardians' case.t Cohabitation, it had 'been decided, was prima facie evidence of mar- riage -and implied responsibility for mainten- ance.. •

Prisoner added that he had been- living with the woman about eight. or nine months. It was his intention to marry her, but she told him that she was a married woman and that her husband was in an asylum. Prisoner was remanded in custody for seven days in order that inquiries may be made.

Out of the Mouths of Babes.

Little Arthur stood peering down into the .couhtenance of his baby sister, whom the nurse was singing to sleep. "Say, nurse," he finally whispered, "it's aearly unconscious, isn't it?" The nurse nodded in the affirmative, and sang on.

"Then don't sing any more, or you'll kill it."

St. Mary's A‘Thist Drive.

The club rooms attached to the new St. Mary's hall were informally opened blast week by the members of the Catholic liming Men's Society connected with the church. They have been splendidly furnished, and the new billiard table—one of three—is a credit to Messrs. Orme, of Manchester.

It may be noted that out of the total mem- bership of over a hundred % about forty men from the St. Mary's C.Y.M. have gone to the war.

TO-DAY'S WAR-FUND HANDICAP.

How The Players Have Been Drawn

For The First Round.

On Tuesday evening the draw for the first round in connection with the bowling handicap, which the Warrington Works Amateur Bowling Association have arranged on behalf of the local war funds, took place,- and the handicap will be played off to-day at the Association's head- quarters! A good entry has been secured, but there is yet time for other trundlers to come forward and have a shot at the really excellent prizes on offer, for entries will be taken right up to the close of the first round proper. Players are warned to be in readiness to start at the times specified, for those who do not answer to their names promptly will be struck out of the list.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0281.txt | included=true | included =====
UNION MAN'S ASSAULT.

Strong Criticism and Heavy

Sentence.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Wednesday- morning there .were on the bench : Mr. J. W. Hayward (in the chair) ; Alderman Smethurst; and Messrs. C. Jackson Holmes and W. T. Williams.

Abel Victory Smith, labourer, of 3, Prince-street, was charged with having on Saturday, May 22nd, assaulted and beaten Daniel Morris, of 10, Wellington-street--a fel- low worker at Messrs. Rylamas' Dalton-Bank works.

Mr. T. S. Steel, solicitor, of Warringt.n, ap- peared for the prosecution.

MR. STEEVS STORY.

Mr. T: S. Steel said he had no doubt that when their Worships had heard the evidence they would agree that Smith had been guilty of a most brutal and cowardly assault Prisoner was a member of the Gas Workers' Union, the complainant was not, and from time to time efforts had. been made to induce him to become a member of the union. Smith had told him "that it was about time he joined and if he didn't there was going to be trouble." . _ _

On the night of the assault he went into the works to see a man named Greerihnlgh as to what time he should relieve him on Sunday morning, and after coming out stood talking in the lodge; when defendant and three oilier members of the union—Webb, Holmes and Taylor—came up and stood looking in. They went away into the works, and presently Smith came back alone and said he wanted to speak to Morris. Suddenly Struck.

Morris was then standing with his hands in his pockets, -and followed him through the Wicket-gate and into Dalton-Bank without taking them out. As they were passing through the gate Smith said: "What did you want to speak to your foreman about the Gas Workers' Union for?" Complainant told him that he had asked the foreman if he was com- pelled to joins and had been told "No." Suddenly and without a word Smith struck complainant in the mouth with his fist, knocking him to the ground. His head struck the stone and he immediately lost conscious- 1108s. He lay there still with his hands in his pockets!

- The moment Smith had done this he rushed into the lodge and said to Hargreaves, the lodgeman: "I'll—well give him more next time." Hargreaves went and found the man lying in the roadway with his hands still in his pockets, and Dr. Anderson was shortly called. He had to put several stitches in Morris's head The matter was reported to the police; and prisoner, when charged, replied: "No, not un- lawfully. In self-defence, please." "I think," concluded Mr. Steel, "you will re- gard this as a most violent and brutal assault, and an evidently premeditated one. Morris is a very quiet and decent man, following his work regularly and well; and has a perfect right to say whether he will belong. to the union or not: I yenture to suggest that this is not a case where justice would be met by the infliction of a fine."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0282.txt | included=true | included =====
WAS A KNIFE FOUND?

The complainant gave evidence, and in re- ply to a question said he was still under Dr. Anderson's care, and had not been able to work for some days. Prisoner: When I called you out, Morris what did I say to you F- "Did you tell Mr. Reddish about joining the union?" And what did you say to me? (Here 'Smith gave a, string of the most filthy langauge which he de- clared Morris had (used.) And with these words didn't you pull a knife out? I put my left arm up to guard off the knife and hit you with my fist. The knife was found afterwards by the foreman of the shop. Complainant denied all this.

Hargreaves, the watchman: said Morris came at about twenty past nine. In corroborating Mr. St4l's statement, he said he saw no knife on the ground when he first went out, and none was mentioned. Morris was bleeding from the head, and he could not deal with him himself.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0283.txt | included=true | included =====
EYE-WITNESS'S EVIDENCE.

James Frangleton, plasterer, of 101, School Brow, said at the time of the attack he was sitting at his front door—right opposite the wicket-gate. He saw two men come out, but did not know whether one pushed or struck the other; however, he saw him fall. He went across, as he thought it such a dastardly trick, and found Morris. He did not see any knife.

Detective Turner, who arrested and charged prisoner, said that morning was the first occa- sion on which he had heard about a knife being used.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0284.txt | included=true | included =====
WAS COMPLAINANT DRUNK?

Prisoner stated that on the Friday he had just simply asked Morris whether he was going to join the Union, and said he had better— because the men were "kicking against it. 4 He then heard that Morris had told the fore- man. He went to see Greenhalgh. He (pris- oner) collected the contributions for the Union. "Morris was drunk," he continued, "and I wasn't, because I don't take it." Morris came outside, and he asked him about telling Mr. Reddish. He contradicted it, and .made the re- mark previously reported. "I. said," prisoner went on, "Do you mean that?' and he an- swered Yes.' When they picked him up the knife was on the floor."

. Prisoner added that he could not get his witness to the court.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0285.txt | included=true | included =====
"DANGEROUS GANG OF THIEVES."

The Chief Constable: Undoubtedly if there had been any suggestion of a knife the police would have investigated it for the information of the Court, quite apart from anything else. This man knows perfectly well what the rules and regulations are—from previous experience. He has ,not been here since 1909, when he was up for Army desertion. In 1907, at the Liver- pool Quarter Sessions, he was sent to gaol for two months' hard labour for warehouse-break- ing. He was then an associate of a dangerous gang of thieves. He is a most knowing cus- tomer, and has been knoWn to the police all these years. "We cannot understand," added Mr. Nicholls, "why a more serious charge has not been pre- ferred against him. The police have had noth- ing to do with the case, or else such a charge would undoubtedly have been made."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0286.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHAIRMAN AND THE UNION.

The Chairman (to prisoner) : The charge has been very clearly proved, and the case is a very bad one. How a Union can emplor a man like you to collect money one can scarcely realise. Talk about tyranny—the tyranny of one man over another—it .is more like the Germans'. You 'must go to Prison or eight weeks' hard labour.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0288.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN LIBER ALS.

Miss Truda Cresfield On Work Of The Council.

THIS RIGHTEOUS WAR.

Tho annual, council meetings of the Women's Liberal Federation have been held in London.

There was a new note in the Federation gather- ings this year, and one which it was very right should Come, especially from Women. I can best describe it as spiritual' insight. The meetings in Caxton Hall were brisk, earnest, well attended; delegates followed their leaders' speeches with keen attention, seizing on the points they made, and heartily approving them; but again it was the reflections that dis- closed this same spiritual insight which most went to their hearts. We were urged to "think rightly" that the world's sorrow might thereby be mitigated, if it be beyond healing, at present; to bear always in mind that this war stood for righteousness as against materialism; in fact that it was to be tersely summed up in our women's belief (on which we base the suffrage claim) that "Right is Might" as versus "Might is Right." It reminded me of the words addressed by President Roosevelt to :Andrew Carnegie at the opening of the first Hague conference: "Peace is normally a great good; but I beseech you to remember that she is only the handmaid of righteousness. Better a righteous war than an unjust peace."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0289.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GERMAN BLASPHEMY.

And the note all our speakers sounded was that if ever a war were righteous, this one is. May I quote the words of that veteran truth- seeker, Richard Whiting, which seem to me to sum up very nearly why the Allies are fighting? "I think these are the throes of new birth. All that was evil in human society, the arro- gance of wealth and caste, the worship of material success, and even of the false signs of success; the contempt, though it was usually unconscious, shown for the quiet virtues, for modesty, gentleness, shy merrt; the assured dominion over the many milder and more humane natures by a few hard, energetic, cunning and self-seeking materialists—all these evils found their easiest expression in the German temperament of modern manufacture. Success, justified by nothing except the power to make it, and a sword to defend it, was wor- shipped in Germany. And, as you see, the whole world has risen horrified against that blasphemy at last, to end it—a brand of Ger- man materialism which is without its peer in all the concepts of the time. This has brought the whole world to its senses in the interest of that first of all human concerns, the integrity of the human soul."

Is not that a fine description of the spiritual inwardness of this titanic war? The author of No. 5, John-street," is 75; ant:l..old eyes dome- times see with a remarkable clearness, esnecially when they have been truth-seeking all their life.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0290.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME GAPS.

There were one or two gaps in the " Front Treasury Bench" on the platform this year. First of all, of course, one noted the disappear- ance of Lady Carlisle; full of years and honour, she has at last resigned after, I believe, over 30 years of work as President of ,the Women's Liberal Federation. Lady Abereonwy, ac- counted of all to be one of the most able women in England,. now reigns in her stead. Those Who have not read the new President's "Women's Charter" should do so. It is a masterpiece of clear thinking. Mrs. Eva McLaren, to our regret, was also absent, as well as her great friend, Mrs. Heron Maxwell. Mrs. Eva's strength had been somewhat overtaxed at Cherbourg, and she is at present troubled with sciatica, and ordered a month's rest. Lady Cecelia Roberts, Mrs. Walter Rea and others were gone, but Warrington Liberals will be Pleased to hear that Mrs. Stewart Brown was back in her place, restored to health after the trying neuritis she suffered from subsequent to her detention with her husband in Germany.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0291.txt | included=true | included =====
DANGER OF A WRONG SETTLEMENT.

The speeches of the day were made by Mrs. Runciman, Miss Alison Garland and Mrs. Alderton.

Miss Garland was speaki,g with her usual eloquence and knowledge when Mrs. Crosfield and I arrived. Sloe was quoting the Congress of Vienna, 100 years ago, when the world was nearly as overwhelmed by war as we are now, and when the nations earnestly hoped to estab- lish peace for good and all. Yet, because in- justices were permitted in the settlement, be- cause natural, racial and geographical boundaries were not observed, discontent only sowed the seed for further war in the fulness of time. She urged strongly that "the views of Liberal women be ascertained with regard to the principles on which the terms of peace shall be based."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0292.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN FOR WORK. -

Mrs. Runciman made a good and careful speech on the organisation of women's work in war time. She urged women not to fear the Labour Exchanges. Let them take up new lines of work, "and don't think too little of your powers. Let me tell you of a Scotch. woman who offered to replace an M.P. whilst he was away at the war, as being the work most suitable to her! She offered, by the way, to replace him on either side of the House!" Mrs. Runeiman dwelt strongly on the point of women being paid the same as men; "do not let yourselves be made use of to under-cut the men while they are away' was her last note.

Another speaker put in a fine, sensible plea for supervision of the time-sheets And wage lists of sub-contractors to Government. This would at last get somewhere near to dealing with the terrible problem of the sweated home worker. Mrs. Runciman agreed to having this embodied in her resolution.

Another speaker mentioned that of 60,000 women who registered on this special war regis- ter, only 942 have so far been "placed," but our President pointed out that this register is a reserve on which the Government will draw more and more as men leave the country. It does not interfere with the normal Labour Ex- change register for women. and those names on this latter list are taken first.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0293.txt | included=true | included =====
BAD COOKING FOR RECRUITS.

is. Alderton spoke nobly on the deplorable hardships of our recruits due to a bad system of cooking, or rather no system at all. As her sensible speech is reported in most papers, I will not dwell on it; but should like to mention that there are men training at Colchester, where Mrs. Alderton lives; that she and her fellow workers have 5,000 a night to look after in a. big club which the town has managed to instal for the men at a cost of "..500; that she strongly condemns the rule "whereby the last meal to private soldiers is served at 4.30, because it is a direct incentive to their seeking further refreshment in public-houses"; and that we Liberal women may be proud to have such organisers and such thinkers as Mrs. Alderton amongst us.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0294.txt | included=true | included =====
PEACE REGISTER TO COME.

In conclusion, it is a proof of our vitality that the meetings were so well attended in this year of the great war. Dark colours were almost universal—more than one, alas! wore black, with a meaning; notably our hostess, Lady Cowdray, whose son, Hon. Geoffrey Pear- son, was killed whilst escaping from the Ger- man trenches some months back; Mrs. Handley Spicer, and others. Mrs. Bannister Fletcher, better known to us as Lady Bamford Slack, was in her place flanking the President as co-secre- tary, with Mrs. gradley Reid the other _side. Mrs. .Fletcher had been speaking the day before at the Liberal Women's Suffrage Union gather- ing in Caxton Hall, and never hayed heard her more en veine, as she pithily put it, referring to women's work: "Whilst men are at the Front, women are to the front," and "As there is a war register for women, surely a peace register must also come."

If the feeling was more sober than in most years and the faces around one graver than usual, there was also hope in the air; and the. keynote to all the proceedings, the note which rang in every speech—except that of a few drivellers, those afflictions who want to hear themselves talk—was more and more this: "Women's, work, women's work." I will close with an apt paraphrase of Charles Kingsley,

which one speaker put forward, "For women must work and men must figitt." T. H. CROSFIELD.

RESOLUTIONS PASSED.

Among the resolutions passed at the Confer- ence were the following :—That at a time when war is causing such grievous loss of life it is pre-eminently war work for women to safe- guard. the conditions and improve the health of the new generation; that children's educa- tion should not be in any way curtailed or impaired at the present crisis, and that if, in the last resort, children are liberated from schr- for agricultural labour, very careful re- str7 cins should be imposed as to the kind of woik, hours and wages; that the Council was of the opinion that the employment of police- women is urgently needed for the preventive and protective side of police work. A resolution drawing the attention of the Government to the unsatisfactory position of British women living in this country who were the wives or widows of aliens, and expressing the opinion that so long as sucli women re- mained on British soil they should not by their marriage be entirely deprived of their rights of citizenship was carried unanimously.

FEWER UNEMPLOYED

The Board of Trade announce that in the trades compulsorily insured against Unemploy- ment—mainly building, works of construction, engineering, shipping, vehicle-making, &c,— the percentage of unemployment at 7th May was 0.95, as. compared with 1.40 a month ago and 3.27 a year ago. These figures relate to the whole of the 'United Kingdom, and include all unemployed workmen in the insured trades. The rate of unemployment in these trades less than a third of the rate a Year. ago.

As regards the uninsured trades; the- munber of men and women on the registers of the labour exchanges at 7th May shows an increase on the figures of a month ago, being 57,904, as compared with 50,038. For men alone the corresponding figures were 16,818 and 16,371, and for women 41,086 and 4-1,667.

STORIES FROM THE TRENCHES

300,000 ME 1/ WANTED !

Warrington Private W Ito Tells The Reason Why.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0305.txt | included=true | included =====
STIRRING APPEAL.

An old Warringtonian serving at the Front with the Cheshire Regiment writes as follows: " Yes! If only the men in England to-day could have seen the sights that I have seen here in Belgium and Northern France, I don't think they would hesitate about enlisting. Just think of the value of the sea that separates England from the enemy's shores. " It have witnessed endless streams of refugees plodding through the muddy lanes and streets, women with .a bundle on their back, all they possess; and a little girl holding on to her mother's skirts and a' small boy on the ,other side, not able to understand what it all means. The poor little thin-faced woman looked as if she was going to fall every step she took, and the sweat ran down her cheeks, perhaPs mingled with tears, as the day was hot, and the poor little girl would look up into her mother's face as if saying 'Poor mother,' and the little boy carrying his hat in his hand, and on they are going, goodness knows where! Makes Strong Men Weep.

• • "That is only one of the many sights to be seen 'daily here, and as I stand with some of my comrades, some of whom have the ribbons of Egypt, Burma and South Africa on their breasts, like myself—for this is my third time— you could see the pity in their faces and also tears in the eye, as the procession went by. _ _ _ _ _ _ .

"I think that this war is not realised by thousands of the, young men at home who can, but will not, enlist. If only they could stand by a roadside in Belgium where I have stood! Could they stand there and not enlist? For every man who does not enlist and can, makes it easier for the Germans to cross the Channel, and, believe me, if ever they do, there will he processions passing along the roads in England, too.

"You can believe me when I tell you that I saw, with my comrades, here the other day between 20 and 30 children, and some of them not more than 12 months old, along with their mothers, and the majority of them had lost an arm or a leg, and the poor mothers lying beside the little ones, unable to look after them, through being blown out of their houses by German shells.

"I wonder if the young men at home yet know that out here there are men over 50 and 60 years of age that have done their bit in the trenches, and did not wait to be dragged out here, which will have to be done, but volun- tered to go out if medically fit, and left good homes and good wives and families and regular jobs. If We Had Stayed at Home.

"If we stayed at home, conscription would be in force now, and the thousands of young men would be out here. We ought to let the world see we don't want conscription, that our men volunteered to defend their Country, their flag and their ring, and let every an who can enlist come out at once and stand side by side with the "contemptible little army," as we have been called, and fight with the brave French and Belgians, and then; and not till then, can we drive the Germans out of France and Belgium and end this war."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0306.txt | included=true | included =====
"AWFUL CADS."

A sergeant-major of the 2nd Border Regiment has written an indignant letter to Mr. W. H. Drucquer, of Elgin °Avenue, W., in which he expresses the prevalent opinion at the Front of " strikers"•who imperil our gallant fighters in the trenches. The writer of the letter has been three times mentioned in despatches, twice promoted on the field, and was awarded the D.C.M. for con- spicuous bravery at Hill 60. Here is an extract frOm his letter:

Dear Drucquer,-7-I say, isn't it rotten about the "strikers" at Northampton? What awful cads—they .are the limit! They want to come out here and have a taste of this, then per- haps they woultd get a little manhood into them.

Men? They disgrace the name of English- men! I hope they have ceased to designate themselves as Britishers from now on.

I don't care a jot what their grievance may have been—if any. To think that. any person wearing trousers in England at the present 'time can be so despicable is very hard to believe. Bah! They are not worth the paper I am writing on. My opinion is not the only one. All the soldiers out here think the same and say so; but they put it in true military par- lance: "Send the out here and let them do something for their living; they- ,would soon run away." That is what is said out here. I'd like to meet some of the cads, assez ! Quite enough for me; but I mean it, and so do all our chaps out here. It's a damn, rotten shame that the Government allows them to play the fool so.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0307.txt | included=true | included =====
• MERCY,:

Germans Ask For It. But Show None

MURDER OF WOUNDED.

, Mr. G. Yeats, writing,to the Wycliffe- P.S.A., of which he was a member, from somewhere ill Franwg, under. date May 16, says

"Dear Mr. ,Farrar and all 'members,—l am sorry I have not written before, but we have been so busy with oyes 'friends' opposite us. Of course, you will have read of the attack on Sunday last. It was a lovely .morning when the bombardment started on . the German trenches. My word, 'I don't know _how they live, but I should say they ought to live well, as our artillery sent plenty of iron rations that morn- ing to last them a month, but that was not all. When we got the order to advance was thel time We had to ativance about a mile and a quarter over open country, so you can guess what we, got from their artillery and rifle fire, although it was not so, severe, but in the after- noon it was terrible. I shall never forget it if I live to be a hundred. Of course, they tried thir best to shell us out,- but, no, ke stuck to ouV- trenches, -waiting 'patiently folo a counter- attack, but mine came, and it is a good job for them that they did not, as we were all ready for them.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0308.txt | included=true | included =====
German Inhumanity.

"Our company was. holding a sap trench half- way between our lines and the Germans,- and I think it was the awfullest night P have spent since I have been in France. Of course, it was, full of dead, dying and wounded, of both sides, and the groans from the wounded were pitiful to hear, and to make things worse we could not help them; You can, imagine how one feels to see a comrade in agony antl one cannot help him in any way. Of course, we did our best for them, but we dare not shift them, so we had to stay at our posts, knee deep in water and mud, until the stretcher-Wearers and doctor came. We had been in this awful position :all night and day, and during the, day the dirty dogs put-a black Maria amongst us, so we had to retire as quick as we could, leaving the poor unfortunate wounded to the mercy of the shells. But we were soon back again, thank God) and they had not killed any of them. We got the wounded out, and, of course, the dead we had to bury. "It will be a glorious day when peace is pro- claimed, as this war is inhuman. It is not war, it is murder. If you do not get hit, you have to face those awful gases the brutes are using. You can only imagine the horrors of it all, as you read in the papers.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0309.txt | included=true | included =====
"I Don't Think!"

"I am. sure I could not show any mercy to any of them for what I have seen wiith my own eyes. We. were watching them through the periscope last Monday afternoon after the attack, and we saw them deliberately shooting at our wounded as they lay in front of their lines. Is it not awful? Could any Christian man show mercy for such men?—or, at least, not men, but murderers; and yet they ask for it and they. get it—l don't think! "God knows how awful it is, and I hope and pray it will soon be over, and I know every- body is of the same opinion, but not until we have taught them what civilisation means."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0310.txt | included=true | included =====
TWICE 'WOUNDED.

Rifleman Harry Starkey, 2nd Battalion Rifle Brigade, is another local man to be wounded. He received his wound on Sunday, May 9th, and writing from the Manchester Royal Infirmary to his .wife, he says: .

"I got Wounded, in the leg and foot last Sun- day, but don't upset yourself. I have only done my share; it is a terrible war.' Our regiment has caught it, I can tell you. lam a lucky man to come through with this lot. I lay from Sunday until Monday night before they could get to me at all. My secogd wound I got in the foot while crawling to a place of safety. It is murder—not war—and I think everyone who is able should go and give a helping hand to the others." •

===== 0002601_19150529_art0311.txt | included=true | included =====
A Diverted Parcel.

Later, Mrs. Starkey received the following letter from Sergeant Jordan, who, belongs to the same battalion as Private Starkey :

"I received the parcel from you, addressed to Rifleman Starkey, which I. opened, as he has been wounded. I cannot say what sort of a wound he has received, but I am trying to find out, as I .am his platoon sergeant. I have given the contents of the parcel to his mates, and they 'wish me to thank you for it. I will wait a reply, from you, and then, perhaps, I may liave more news to tell you." Rifleman Starkey, whose home is 16, Forster- street, was formerly employed at Messrs. Rey- nolds' tannery, Mersey-street. He joined the Rifle Brigade in September, and went to the Front at the beginning of this year. He is associated with the Order of Foresters, and is also a member of the Junior Conservative Club, Orford-lane.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0312.txt | included=true | included =====

PuzzlNl By Government Changes Against Conscripts.

Company Q.M.S. David Ridyard, A company, 4th South Lancashires, who is porter at the Warrington Museum, writing to Mr. G. A: Dunlop, the assistant curator, say4:7- "How are you? - It seems ages since I wrote you before. I 'suppose it is weeks. One loses touch with time on this job. . liiiyour last letter von said you'd heard we were at Wipers, Yerpes, 'eaps, &c. No! By sheer luck we struck east and missed the or fun (?) All I saw was he results. (in part). It was awful. On duty myself during the business, and fully two miles away, I got into 'a cloud of it for a few minutes and was no good for days. Wonder what our people, are going to 'do about it? The feeling amongst the majority is that it would he a pity were we to descend to the .same level. -However; we Shall see. '

"I saw a paper yesterday containing Mr. Asquith's announcement to the House 're new Coalition Government. Am not in a position out here to pass a fair opinion on things, but so far as I can see the Government have done well, particularly well under the circumstances. They've had a tough job, without doubt. Is it that the retiring members are done up with the strain?, I Must say that the tone of certain papers for some time has been' positively pain- ful—given our fellows the hump. A sort of "we must be going to the dogs "• (feeling. , This doesn't do any good. Gbd knows our work is hard enough, without any. discouragement from home. _Fair and honest criticism is all right, but to my mind there has been some low hit- ting.

“As for the appeal for cons,C' ription, well, for my part, I don't want any of 'em out here along with me. And I 'don't think there's any need for it. Let employers honestly encourage the young chaps and not merely pretend to do so, and all we want we can get. This kind 'of game is soon picked up, and during periods of 'rest' the other training is carried on (for open work).”

===== 0002601_19150529_art0314.txt | included=true | included =====
WATER-BOTTLE SAVED HIS CIFE

Rifleman Tells Of The Wor,,i Day fie

Remembers.

Rifleman C. Scrutton, of the King's Royal Rifles, Who was formerly a member of the Latchford Cadet Corps; writing to Mr. J. B. Starkey, of 2, Enville-street, Latchford, ex- presses his* thanks for a parcel which had been forwarded him and intimates that the arm in which he was wounded is healing nicely, whilst his eyes, which had also been, damaged, are a bit better.

"We .had. _a rough time of it in the trenches the day I got hit," he adds. "Shells were corning over eight at a time, and I did not expect to _live through the experience., I owe my life to my water-bottle. It was cut two by a, piece of shell, which would have crashed clean through my ribs, had it not been that the bottle stopped it. I got hit at 7.30 in the mOrn- ing and had to lie where I fell until dark. I think it was the worst (hiy in all my life. . . .

" Tommy " Finds a New Name for German Shells.

"There had been some terrible fighting round, for, about three weeks, the place where I got shot—Ypres. - . . The Germans sent some shells over us, which we dubbed 'travelling trains,' for they made a noise just like onrush- ing expresses."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0315.txt | included=true | included =====
Secret of Spring Happiness.

"The coming of spring makes us happy," said an ethnologist, "because millions of years ago it • made happy the hairy, gibbering creatures • that men then were. Primeval man lived in trees, in caves and burrows. He could not build .and he had not yet discovered fire. Hence winter, with its sleet .and snow and. ice, was a time of terrible suffering to the poor naked wretch. With the spring's coming, the return of the flowers, the birds, the blue skies. and, above all, -of the warm sunshine, naked. man rejoiced with an intensity we can hardly imagine. He lay on the green grass, the sun- light poured its gold floods of warmth on his reddish hide and he was profoundly happy in the thought that for five or six months he would be quite comfortable. It is a faint, dim memory of • that happiness which makes us to- day vaguely rejoice in the beautiful spring weather,' vaoiely hope that something 'pleasant is going to happen to us." •

FARM AND GARDEN.

VALUE OF VARIETY

No matter haw fond stock are of a certain plant, or how nutritious the crop may be, they freely partake of other plants among the main crop. Take afield of rape, for instance, a food sheep are particularly fond of. If there are any, other plants growing among the crop, even though they are only weeds, they are eaten and relished just because they represent a change from the predominating plant. This is the chief reason for sowing such as mustard, oats or barley with rape, as it, in a measure, counteracts the heating effects of the rape, and thereby acts as a corrective and prevents alimentary troubles so common while feeding with rape.—" New Zealand Farmer." '

===== 0002601_19150529_art0318.txt | included=true | included =====
HARES AND FIELDS OF WHEAT.

"A Patriotic Observer" writes to the Ormskirk Advertiser " : "There are many lamentable things being done' and many pathetic scenes at the present time, but one pitiable scene is to be witnessed any day in a walk from Aintree Station through Netherton on to Sefton Church.. To see ,the, hares, almost like droves of sheep, feeding on and scamper- ing over the fields of wheat, now when every field of wheat should be carefully tended, is depressing to say the least. In a short time the crops will cover these marauders, but think of the damage which will be 'done without its being seen until the reaper reveals the bare and 'broken- patches."

===== 0002601_19150529_art0319.txt | included=true | included =====
PRODUCTION OF MILK.

Milk is produced by the building up and breaking down of minute cells within the udder of the cow (writes. Mr. C. W. Walker- Tisdale, in Hensen'S Dairy Bulletin). The quantity of milk a :pow can produce in a year depends upon its capacity of manufacturing these cells, while the quality of milk will de- pend more upon the natural characteristics of the cow and the food she receives: Cows which are not naturally good milkers, so far as the quality and quantity are concerned, cannot be made to yield a large quantity of rich milk by giving them an excess •of food. The carefully selected cow for milking pur- poses will give more milk ,on a fair amount of food than the bad milker which is over-fed. So long as a cow has just sufficient foods of the right kind to enable her to manufacture her natural quantity of milk, and repair her daily' bodily waste, any extra feeding will not produce more milk for any length of time. The only case where a chanqe or increase of food has a marked and lasting improvement on the milk yield is when the cow has previously not been receiving sufficient food of the right kind. Some cows require more food than others in order to produce the same quantity of milk. Therefore, in building up a herd of cows aver- aging a thousand gallons of milk a year, an important point is to take the feeding into consideration, and select cows which give a maximum quantity of Milk without consuming too large, a quantity of food.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0320.txt | included=true | included =====
MILK RECORDS.

One reason why milk records have not been more generally kept is because so many farmers consider it a laborious and expensive procedure. This idea is, however, quite erroneous, as the keeping of milk records is a very- simple matter. The milk-weigher, con- sisting of a spring balance which registers the quantity of milk in both pounds and gallons, is hung • up in a convenient place near the door of the cowshed. As each cow is milked, its milk is weighed and entered on. a milk- record sheet bearing the name or number of each cow in the herd. With regard to the testing for quality, this takes much longer than the weighing, and on that account may be carried out only once or twice weekly.

IN THE GARDEN.

GARDEN NOTES.

As you •make your, garden bed, so you must lie about it: Never count your chickens until they get back from your neighbour's garden. -It's a long garden that has no backache. The early worm gets the garden prize. Nothing plant, nothing to worry about.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0322.txt | included=true | included =====
RHUBARB.

It is painful to see the treatment that is some Ames meted out to this indispenskible vegetable. It is often found growing in some out-of-the-way corner, where never a bit of manure is given it, and yet succulent stalks are looked for. Owing to the poor crop resulting from such treatment is has to be closely pulled to supply the daily wants of the household. Can it be wondered at that the crop in after years is unsatisfactory? It is not fair to such a useful vegetable that such treatment should be 'given.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0323.txt | included=true | included =====
WALLFLOWERS.

• Now is the time when seed should be sown if flowers are desired next winter and spring. Many people, anxious to get strong plants, sow too soon,- with the result that the seedlings get. too large by the wintert, and if the weather should happen to be severe, they succumb. The first week in June is a suitable time to sow, the best place being the open ground. If the seeds are sown • thinly the seedlings are dwarf and stocky, and grow sturdily until it is time to prick theth out for a time previous to placing them in their winter quarters. There is now a large choice of colours, but the best, in my opinion, are .a good selection of the dark red and a bright yellow,

===== 0002601_19150529_art0324.txt | included=true | included =====
INSECT PESTS.

As soon as insects are ,een on fruit tress growing against walls and on roses, energetic meaSures should be taken for their destruction, as; if once allowed to gain a footing, they in- "crease very fast, and it will be a difficult mat- ter to clear them off. It is not so much a question of using this or that manure for their destruction, as the prompt application of a remedy as soon as the first signs of the pest are 'seen.

===== 0002601_19150529_art0325.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GOOSEBERRY CATERPILLAR.

There are many so-called remedies for this pest;, but many of them are of a poisonous natuf*e and positively dangerous. The old- fashioned plan, of dusting the •lower brandies with 'a mixture of lime and soot the moment the pest. is discovered is hard to .beat. The caterpillar invariably attacks the lower branches first, and if the mixture is dusted in an upward direction the insects will fall to the ground, when another dressing of the lime and soot will at once destroy them.—" Wick" in " Farm and, Home."

Magnified Vision. He had attended a club smoker and was stroll- ing home with a load of good 'spirits. On reach- ing a big hotel he made his way in and asked for a room.

His personal appearance being that of a gen- tleman he was given a room. About 20 minutes later the telephone bell at the cashier's desk tingled.

." Hello," said a"pice at the other end, "there are two racks np id this room; which one do I' use?"

"You have two bats; put one on each rack," answered the clerk. The man at the other end must have been satisfied with the answer, because he wasn't heard from for the rest of the night.
